# The small CAD bench

Commands a session calls over files in its working directory, each answering in plain text and numbers, for the local model's first project (M18 in mqucifer/project-print `ROADMAP.md`, ADR 0010 there, and mqucifer/print-shop#14). A trial on 2026-11-01 showed tools reporting success when they were wrong, so **every answer gets a second check by a different method**, and the bench says when the two differ. It never touches the printer, and it holds no model call. Motion, balance, stiffness and strain come as their own issues when a project's number fails without them.

```
scripts/bench.py build                       # once: the first image, if the disk has room
scripts/bench.py build cae                   # once: the second, with FreeCAD, for fit-on, bend, load and optimise
scripts/bench.py check                       # each image and the disk against their limits
scripts/bench.py hosts                       # each remote host the settings name, checked
scripts/bench.py send NAME [bench|cae]       # the images sent to a remote host, checked by ID
scripts/bench.py clearance a.step b.step     # any command below, over the current directory
scripts/bench.py python script.py            # your own script, in the first image's container
scripts/bench.py freecad script.py           # your own script, in the second's, with FreeCAD
```

A settings study's `load` reads can also run on a remote host's Docker over SSH, as [the study's page](settings-study.md) says (`bench/remote.py`, mqucifer/print-shop#373): `hosts` checks each one, and `send` pipes `docker save` here into its `docker load` and checks the IDs, where its disk keeps the floor free with the image.

**A `load` that meets its container's memory is offered a host with more** (mqucifer/print-shop#380, `bench/pool.py`'s `offer`). Where a remote host's settings state a larger memory for a load, `BENCH_HOST_<NAME>_LOAD_MEMORY` (more than a load's own 4g, within its `_MEMORY`), a `load` whose read met its container's memory on the way to an answer that isn't the whole read is read again there in that memory: the same command over the same files, with the same limits of time, the same image by ID and the same code, so the same method, checks and figures. It's met where:
- the kernel stopped one of its processes for memory, by the container's own `oom_kill` count, or a step's need was held against the cgroup's room before it began and didn't fit (each a `memory` unread in `load`), and the answer that followed is NO ANSWER, or a glass band's lower bound read in its place; or
- the kernel stopped `load` itself for memory, which the host says (`host.killed_met`) only where Docker's own `oom` event for that container, its own and asked of it alone and matched by its whole name, says so (`host.oom_killed`): any other kill, as a stopped study's, is no memory met. The events are asked for by the daemon's own clock (`docker info`'s `SystemTime`), from a minute before the command began to a few seconds past its end, so a late event is read and a remote's clock is never compared with this one's. Nothing is offered while a study or a run is stopping.

`load` says it on its stderr, `bench: met` with its container's limit, the most it held, the need and the room where a check said them, and the step it was in: once as it's met (`final=0`), and again as it ends where the answer isn't the whole read (`final=1`, `progress.say_met`); the host keeps the lines and doesn't pass them on. A read that met the memory and went on a slower way, as a band's direct solve that met it and went on to the iterative one, may run past its time limit and be stopped before it can say its answer: what it said as it met the memory is kept, and it's offered more as well, its reply saying it was stopped after (`job.Met.of`). One that ends with a whole read said only the first, and is never offered. The read there is held within that host's budget and over its floor, before it starts, as any remote job is, and its record says what it met first (`ran.offered`): its reply's `offered:` line names the host, its limit, and the need and room on both, the first host's and its own. **Only a job's own plain files go over** (`remote.open_plain`): each is opened one step of its path at a time, never through a link, and only where it's a regular file, so no file outside its folder is ever sent, as a container here, with only the folder mounted, could never read one. A job that names a link, or a file under one, is refused before anything goes over (`remote.NotPlain`), and what a job saved is written back the same way, never through a link in its folder. **The offered reply is kept only where it reads more** (`job.reads`): the whole over a glass band's lower bound, or either over a NO ANSWER. Where it reads no more, as a NO ANSWER after a bound here, the first answer stands, and both are said. Where no host states more, or none takes it (its MemFree under its floor plus the larger memory, with nothing of this process's there to end, it fails the read, or the read raises), the first answer stands, as before, and the study's budget line or the round's reply says why, a failure by its kind (`HostLost.kind`: the step, and SSH's or Docker's part). A host that fails it takes no more jobs in that run, said. A round's offered read has the run's own limits of time, as here. A study offers it through its pool of hosts (not with `--serial` or `--here-only`); [a design round](rounds.md) through the hosts its settings name, and an eval's run only with `--offer-memory`, so its answers stay comparable between batches. Each host's `_MEMORY` is a process's own budget there, not shared between processes; the MemFree floor, which counts every bench container there by its label, is what holds the host safe across them. Nothing here reads in less: the larger memory only lets a read go on that the smaller stopped.

[The rounds](rounds.md) use it as a session's tool. A session inside the image can call the same commands directly, by mounting `src/print_shop/bench` read-only (`host.py` shows how). Nothing fences the model off: these are tools it may use, and it can write its own scripts.

## What comes back

The first line is the verdict. Then come the numbers, each with how it was read, then at least one `second check:` line, and last `saved:`, naming the text and JSON files written under `bench-out/`. Every figure a report may cite is in those files, and each says how it ran: the image's id, the code's commit, and the CPU, memory and network limits as the container read them, with the most memory it held, its `memory.peak`, where the kernel keeps one (mqucifer/print-shop#295), and the time limit it ran under (`ran: 1981.19 s of its 3600 s limit`, `ran.limit_s`; mqucifer/print-shop#325).

| Exit | Verdicts | It means |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | `OK`, `CLEAR`, `TOUCHING`, `HELD` | both methods agree, and nothing's wrong |
| 1 | `FAIL`, `INTERFERE`, `TOO CLOSE`, `TOO LOOSE`, `NO CHANGE`, `PARTIAL`, `NOT HELD`, `HELD BY A SEPARATE BODY`, `BLOCKED` | both methods agree that something's wrong |
| 2 | `DISAGREE`, `NO ANSWER`, `UNREAD` | there's no answer to rely on: read the numbers, and don't pick one. `UNREAD` is `load`'s: what would give first has no figure to be read against |
| 3 | | the command itself was wrong |

An answer with no second check behind it becomes `NO ANSWER`, whatever it first said.

## The commands

| Command | Answers | First method | Second method |
|---|---|---|---|
| `info FILE` | size, volume, bodies, flat heights, the file's size and its flat faces under 1 mm across | the mesh; OCCT's oriented box round each flat face | OCCT's volume on a STEP; sections stacked on an STL; each flat face's width by points along its edges |
| `clearance FILE... [--min-gap G] [--max-gap G2] [--lift D [--along A]] [--slide DIR [--hold D]] [--box NAME=X0,Y0,Z0,X1,Y1,Z1]... [--facing NAME=DIR]... [--placed ASSEMBLY [--part PART...] [--as PART]] [--exact] [--plan]` | overlap and gap, every pair, across files or a file's solids, a gap range for parts meant to mate, the last file lifted off its seat first, and each pair's least gap at each place named; with `--slide`, what holds the last file named: its overlap where it lies, its first contact on its way out, and everything on its path | Manifold on a fine mesh for the overlap; the gap by OCCT's exact distance on a STEP, the mesh's on an STL; the slide by Manifold's overlap at each step, halved at each contact | OCCT `common` and `cut`, and the mesh's gap, on a STEP; sections clipped by Shapely and sampled points on an STL; the slide by rays along it from points on both parts |
| `readback BEFORE AFTER [--cutter C] [--expect-delta V] [--min-share S]` | volume before and after, and how much of a cutter was in the body | the mesh | OCCT, or stacked sections |
| `rays FILE --centre X,Y,Z [--axis A] [--d D] [--depth H]` | a cut feature measured from its axis: rays that hit nothing, the distance across, the nearest wall, the wall beyond, the floor, and from a point in air, what `across` measures | rays on the mesh; given a STEP, at a peaked bore each ray's hit as the diameter it says (`bore_d_mm`) | a plane section, with a circle fit; at a peaked bore the section's outline the same way, and the circle fit of its round |
| `panes FRAME --pocket-z Z [--lip-z Z] --clearance C [--pane-thickness T]` | a pane from each opening, written to `panes/FRAME/`, its fit, and the edge with no lip | polygons on the section | the pane as a solid against the frame's walls over the pocket's height, and rays up from the edge |
| `capture FRAME --pocket-z Z --clearance C --pane-thickness T [--pane-floor-z F] [--min-lip M] [--layer L] [--panes N]` | whether each pane is held by shape on both faces, wherever its play lets it sit: the lip, how thick, where it's thin, and whether it can slide or swing out | sections stacked beyond the pocket, read as polygons | rays beside the pane's edge, and the pane as a solid swept out of the frame |
| `thin FILE [--nozzle N] [--layer L] [--min-body V] [--one-body]` | features under the nozzle's width, floors under a layer, and tiny pieces | sections opened by the nozzle | rays across each spot, level rays through the walls and straight rays through level floors, and OCCT's count of solids |
| `pause FRAME --pocket-z Z --pane-thickness T [--layer L] [--first-layer F] [--max-gap G] [--min-room R]` | the layer to pause after, and the room over the pane | sections layer by layer | the flat faces' heights |
| `wall FILE --hole X,Y,Z [--axis A] [--min-wall W] [--z FROM,TO]` | the least wall round one hole, along its whole length or the stretch `--z` names, and toward what: the part's outside or another opening; where the hole opens into a hollow, and the stretch that reads it alone | sections across the hole, a layer apart, as polygons | rays out to the hole's wall, then through it along the wall's normal |
| `render FILE... [--views ...] [--section Z...] [--close X,Y,Z,R[,VIEW]]... [--cut AXIS=AT[,AXIS=FROM:TO]]... [--cut at=X,Y,Z,normal=NX,NY,NZ[,up=UX,UY,UZ][,u=FROM:TO][,v=FROM:TO]]... [--gaps] [--look FILE=COLOUR[:LABEL]]... [--test-piece FILE]... [--placed ASSEMBLY [--part PART...]]`, or `render --variant NAME=FILE[,FILE...]... [--close ...]... [--cut ...]... [--gaps] [--look ...]... [--test-piece FILE]... [--placed ASSEMBLY [--part PART...]]` | pictures from the standard views, and a sheet of them, or a sheet with a labelled row for each variant, each file in its colour, marked by its name, and named in a legend. A close-up is R mm all round a point. A cut is every file cut by one plane, square to an axis or any plane through a point, and with `--gaps` each pair's least gap is marked on it; one exactly on a file's face is degenerate, the whole face by OCCT and nothing by the mesh, so it's `NO ANSWER`, with a cut a little off it to copy on each side that passes through a file (mqucifer/print-shop#191) | VTK, offscreen | the mesh projected by arithmetic against the painted outline, each close-up's triangles projected and filled against its pixels, the section's area against its pixels, and each file's area on a cut by the mesh against OCCT's section, and each gap on it by the outlines against OCCT's sections |
| `params SCRIPT --set NAME=VALUE [--max-left N] [--keep DIR]` | which dimensions, volumes and pieces moved when one parameter changed | each piece's figures, three runs | the meshes' symmetric difference, and a run with nothing changed as the control |
| `light FRAME PANE... [--with FILE]... [--min-open P]` | the share of each pane open straight through the frame, in percent, and the narrowest band of frame a person sees between two open pieces | each pane's footprint less the frame's shadow, the frame cut at every layer and each level face | rays straight up through the frame from a grid in each footprint, and rays along the line across the narrowest band |
| `thickness PANE... [--max T] [--min T]` | each pane's thickness straight through, its thickest and thinnest | the fine mesh's height | rays straight up through the pane from a grid, each reading the material it crosses |
| `layout FILE.json [--inset D]... [--join mitre\|round] [--min-width W]` | a flat layout of named pieces, in 2D: whether each is one piece and where each part is, its least width and where, its sharp tips, what an inset leaves of it and where it cuts it, any two that overlap, and a picture with each piece labelled | rays square to each edge, to the edge facing back | the nearest point facing back, from each point of the edges; the parts by the picture's own pixels |
| `edges FILE... [--with FILE]... [--min-radius R] [--box NAME=X0,Y0,Z0,X1,Y1,Z1]... [--facing NAME=DIR]...` | each outside edge a finger meets: sharp, and how far it turns, or rounded, and the least radius, with where, overall, for each group (upright, top, bottom, side) and for each place named | the exact surface's normals along each edge and its curvature over each rounded face; a 12 mm ball's reach on a grid | the mesh cut square to each sharp edge, and along each rounding where it bends most, with a circle fitted; each ball's centre held clear by the mesh's exact distance |
| `faces FILE` | a STEP part's flat faces by plane, the way each faces, where, its area and box; its round holes and bosses, centre, axis, radius, depth and whether each end is open | OCCT's faces: each plane's normal and offset, each full cylinder's axis and radius | the fine mesh's triangles in each plane, their area; the mesh cut square to each axis half way along, a circle fitted round it, and the depth from the mesh's points on the cylinder |
| `slice FILE --at AXIS=AT... [--box NAME=X0,Y0,Z0,X1,Y1,Z1]...` | the part cut by a plane, level or upright: each separate piece's area, box, outline and least width, and each hole in it, with its centre and, where round, its diameter; how many pieces and holes; and the section inside each box named | Manifold's section of the fine mesh, as polygons; each least width from the polygon's edges that face each other | OCCT's section of the exact surface: area, pieces and holes; on an STL its triangles cut one by one; each least width by rays along each edge's inward normal |
| `pieces trace IMAGE --width MM`, `pieces check MAP`, `pieces merge MAP A B`, `pieces split MAP ID --line ...`, `pieces extend MAP --at X,Y`, `pieces drop-line MAP --at X,Y`, `pieces widen MAP A B`, `pieces retrace MAP --box X0,Y0,X1,Y1`, `pieces fill MAP --at X,Y`, `pieces paint MAP ID COLOUR`, `pieces move-line MAP A B --line ...`, `pieces fix MAP STEPS`, `pieces build MAP --thickness T --pane P --lip FULL[,LEAST] --play C [--min-visible S] [--flush]`, `pieces dome --diameter D --height H --repeats N --bands FILE`, `pieces flatten MAP --slices N`, `pieces build SLICE ... --seam`, `pieces came SLICE --thickness T --lip L`, `pieces ring SLICE --thickness T`, `pieces seam A B --frames FA FB [--came FILE] [--rings FILE...]` | a stained-glass panel as a [piece map](pieces.md): traced from a picture, read against glass-cutting rules, fixed a piece, a line or a place at a time, and built into a frame and a pane file for each colour; and a dome shade's map made from rules, cut into flat slices with the bend at each fold, which join by bevelled seams held from the inside by came strips, and two rings ([below](#a-piece-map-traced-checked-fixed-and-built)) | the pieces' outlines, in Shapely: areas, the largest circle that fits, gaps filled out and back with mitred joins, `layout`'s rays; a dome's pieces from its bands, and each slice's rows from the ellipse | the map as pixels, each piece's id at each pixel's centre it covers; rays from a dead end; on a build, OpenCascade's volumes against the outlines' areas; each slice's file bent back by its own angles, against the dome; a seam's faces and grooves and a came strip's ribs read back from their solids, each ring asked at every slice's edge, and two placed slices, and what lies over an outer face, by points with no boolean |
| `fit-on MOVING --onto FILE... --along DIR --from MM\|clear [--step MM] [--placed ASSEMBLY [--as PART] [--part PART...]]` | a part's way on: placed at each step from MM out along DIR, its way out, down to where it sits, each piece of its overlap with what it goes onto, where, and how deep across, which is how far what's there has to bend | FreeCAD's `common` of the solids (OCCT) at each step, and each piece's least width seen along the way | rays along the way through a mesh of each part, with no boolean: 0.1 mm apart over the whole outline at every step, and 0.02 mm apart over each place for its depth |
| `bend FILE --arm X0,Y0,Z0,X1,Y1,Z1 --root AXIS=AT --push MM --toward DIR [--at far\|near\|both] [--layers AXIS] [--material petg] [--limit PCT] [--mesh MM]` | the strain an arm takes across its layers when an edge of it is pushed aside, its peak and where, against what the material takes across layers, and how far the arm's back moves against the room behind it | FreeCAD's FEM workbench: gmsh's mesh of the arm and CalculiX's linear static solve | the arm as a beam, by hand: 40 slabs from root to pushed edge, each with its own section from OCCT's volume properties |
| `load ASSEMBLY --weight KG --at X,Y,Z [--at ...] [--toward DIR] [--over MM] [--friction MU] [--mesh MM]` | joined parts under a weight: each part's worst pull across its layers and its largest stretch any way, with where and how far out of its layers' plane, against its material's limits; each joint's force and moment, a bolted joint's worst bolt against what it holds, and whether a touching joint is pulled apart or tipped; how far each loaded place moves | FreeCAD's FEM workbench: the parts as one body with shared faces, gmsh's mesh and CalculiX's linear static solve; each joint's force from the nodes' own balance | the same solve on a mesh half as coarse again; the held faces' reaction against the weight; statics for a joint with every held face on one side of it |
| `optimise ASSEMBLY --weight KG --at X,Y,Z [--at ...] --volume FRACTION [--keep X0,Y0,Z0,X1,Y1,Z1]... [--keep-face X,Y,Z]... [--up DIR] [--toward DIR] [--over MM] [--mesh MM]` | **a reference for a design round, never a part:** where one part's load goes, as the material kept when the rest is taken away down to a target volume; the volume kept, the peak stress before and after against the material's limits, pictures from three-quarters above and below, and the kept material as an STL file | BESO (fandaL/beso) on CalculiX: the part solved, the material with the least strain energy taken away and the most added back, over and over; the kept material solved again alone | BESO's own last solve, with what's taken away left in at a millionth of the stiffness; BESO's first solve for the peak before; the held faces' reaction; the STL file's volume read back from its triangles |
| `fit-bed PARTS... [--bed X,Y,Z]` | each part against the bed: the flat side it's laid on, its footprint turned to fit, its height, and whether it fits; given an assembly file, each part on the side its `layers` names | the fine mesh's points seen from above, their hull turned to the least turn that fits | OpenCascade's own box round the exact solid, turned the same way |

`fit-on`, `bend`, `load` and `optimise` run in the second image, `print-shop/cae`, and every other command in the first ([the way on, and the bend](#the-way-on-and-the-bend), [joined parts under a weight](#joined-parts-under-a-weight-and-each-part-against-the-bed), and [where a part's load goes](#optimise-where-a-parts-load-goes)).

**Toolsets** (mqucifer/print-shop#37). The Sponsor, 2026-11-03: "not everything is going to be a stained glass print". So a run has the core, and a request's `## Kind` adds its kind's commands. The core is `info`, `clearance`, `readback`, `rays`, `thin`, `wall`, `pause`, `params`, `render`, `layout`, `faces`, `slice`, `fit-on` and `bend`. Stained glass adds `capture`, `panes`, `light`, `thickness` and `pieces` (mqucifer/print-shop#152, its `dome` and `flatten`, mqucifer/print-shop#154, and its seams, `came` and `ring`, mqucifer/print-shop#166), an enclosure `edges` (mqucifer/print-shop#60), and furniture `load` and `fit-bed` (mqucifer/print-shop#156). `rounds/toolsets.py` holds the sets, and [the rounds](rounds.md#toolsets) say how a run uses them. From the command line, every command works as before.

**A flat layout, read in 2D** (mqucifer/print-shop#61). On `suncatcher-3`, 12 of the 35 calls that reasoned past 20,000 characters and repeated themselves stalled on which piece or neck an inset makes, and which piece is which, and the model printed bounding boxes to find out. `layout` reads a layout the model's script writes: JSON with `pieces`, each name and its outline as GeoJSON (`shapely.geometry.mapping`) or WKT, in mm (`bench/layout.py`).
- **Each piece:** one piece or not, and each part's area, a point inside it and its box, largest first, so "which is which" is a reading. Two pieces that overlap are named, with how much.
- **Its least width:** straight across, from an edge to the edge facing back within 20°: a waist, a bar, an arm, a ring, a cell side to side. Where the sides run further apart it's a corner or a tip, which an inset moves back and never cuts, so it isn't a width. Rays square to the edge are the reading, and the nearest point facing back the second: across parallel sides the two are the same, and within 20° of parallel the second reads as little as cos 20° of the first, never more. Further apart than that, and the points' spacing (at most 0.05 mm), is `DISAGREE`.
- **Sharp tips:** a corner sharper than 75°, read from the outline's own points, with where and its angle. It narrows to nothing, so it's under any `--min-width`.
- **An inset of D** (`--inset`, mitred as a layout's pockets are, or `--join round` for the true distance): its parts, its least width, and where it cuts the piece in two or takes an arm away, each stretch of the piece narrower than 2D by its narrowest and how far it runs. A negative D is an outset.
- **The picture** comes to the model, as `render`'s does: each piece in its colour, labelled on each part, its least width in red, its tips ringed, each inset's outline in black.
- `--min-width W` fails a piece narrower than W, or with a sharp tip, saying where. Without it the answer is `OK` with its readings. It reads a layout, not the part, so no need names it.
- **Back-tested** on planted pieces with arithmetic answers (`test_a_layout_says_each_piece...`), and on layouts made from scratch, up to 61 pieces with two insets in 30 s (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-lacked/`). The reading went through three first methods before this one, each caught by the second: chords at up to 37° from square read an arm's round end at 0.8 of its width, the widest disc that fits read a wedge's rounded tip by its cap, and Delaunay edges miss the nearest point across a cell with no two sides parallel.

### A piece map: traced, checked, fixed and built

(mqucifer/print-shop#152, mqucifer/print-shop#153, M20.) A stained-glass run was given a picture and worked out its pieces itself. On the first test from a traced scene it spent about 250 calls (7 h) before its plan, writing its own Shapely code. Then, asked to merge a sliver, run a dead end on and split a neck, it ran one morphological opening over every piece: every corner was rounded, 15 to 22% of one band's glass became grey patches up to 19 mm wide, and the glass share fell from 91% to 83%. So the bench reads a picture's pieces once, the same way every time, and each fix is a command on one piece. The model chooses each fix; the bench does the geometry and the readings (decided with the Sponsor, 2026-11-17). [The piece map](pieces.md) is the format.

- **`pieces trace IMAGE --width MM [--palette NAME[=#rrggbb],...] [--dark N] [--whole] [--to FILE]`** reads flat colours between dark lines (`bench/pieces_trace.py`). The panel is the picture inside its dark outer frame on all four sides: on each, the first row or column from the picture's edge that's mostly dark, and those after it that are, so a margin outside the frame, and any speck in it, isn't the panel. The lead is each pixel with every channel under `--dark` (40), closed by a square 0.5 mm across and at least 3 pixels, so a hairline gap doesn't join two pieces and a hair of colour beside a line isn't glass. A piece is a patch of the rest joined edge to edge, a patch under 40 pixels being lead. A dark speck inside one piece, joined to no line and shorter than the lead is wide, is the picture's noise: it's filled as that piece's glass and counted in one line (`specks_filled`), so a real line that ends in glass stays one flag. Its outline is its pixels' own edges, each corner kept at its pixel's corner and each staircase between two corners read as the line or curve it stands for, within a pixel of the pixels' edges. Each ring of a patch is read on its own (mqucifer/print-shop#161): a ring too short to read a corner on, a dark speck a few pixels across, is kept less its steps, and the rest are smoothed. One such ring used to leave the whole piece as its pixels' own edges, a step at every pixel. On the operator's scene, with 55 specks, four pieces were that, of 457 to 1,173 points, and the frame built from the map was 47 MB and 10,608 faces. `speckled.png`, 20 curved pieces with five specks inside two of them, traced those two at 314 and 282 points, 806 in all, and its frame was 7.0 MB and 1,686 faces; it traces 244 points now, and its frame is 3.9 MB and 856 faces (`~/print-shop/proofs/m20-pieces-2/speckled-size.txt`). Where two smoothed outlines cross, over a line a pixel or two wide, each loses what they share and half a pixel round it, so a pixel's line lies between them and both stay smooth (mqucifer/print-shop#161). Each kept its pixels' own edges before, as a piece with a speck did. `thin_line.png`, 20 curved pieces with one line a pixel wide, traced two such pieces of 262 and 230 points, 724 points in all, and its frame was 6.0 MB and 1,341 faces; it traces 263 points now, and its frame is 4.2 MB and 917 faces (`~/print-shop/proofs/m20-pieces-2/thin-line-size.txt`). A picture whose lines are all one pixel wide had no answer, its pieces overlapping, and still has none: its lead is thinner than the map's pixels can join. Its colour is the nearest of `--palette`, or the nearest colour the bench has a name for. The reply's picture comes to the model: the picture, and beside it the map with each piece numbered and each rule break marked. **Second method:** the map drawn back at the picture's scale over the patches it came from, which may differ by a pixel's width along every outline. It uses what the image already had, SciPy and Shapely: the operator's prototype read the same pixels with OpenCV and scikit-image. It reads a rectangular panel; a round one isn't traced yet.
  - **What's taken for a frame holds no glass** (mqucifer/print-shop#240). A band of glass on a dark ground, or slanting across one, has rows over 60% dark all the same: they were cut away as frame, `--width` was taken over what was left, and the verdict was `OK`. The frame is found as before, and what it would cut is read before anything is traced: a patch of colour there that would be a piece, past the hair that's closed as lead and 40 pixels or more, is glass. A patch that comes in from the margin and stops short of the panel, at a frame's thinner stretch, is the margin's. With glass there the rule can't tell a frame from a ground, so it's `NO ANSWER` with no map, saying each side it would have cut, how much glass was on it (`frame_cut`), and the window and scale it would have given.
  - **The scale is said first:** where the window traced isn't the whole picture, the verdict's own line starts with it, as `traced 344 x 244 of the picture's 400 x 300 pixels, 69.9% of it, at 0.2907 mm a pixel` (`panel_share_pct`, `picture_px`). A margin outside a frame still isn't the panel, whatever is in it, so this is where a window that's too small shows.
  - **`--whole`** takes the whole picture as the panel, for one already cropped to it: no frame is looked for. The map keeps it (`source.whole_picture`), so `pieces retrace`, `paint` and `check` read the same pixels. The frame's rule stays the default.
  - **A `--dark` other than the default says what it cost** (mqucifer/print-shop#172): the pieces, the lines and the dead ends it reads (`lines_count`, `dead_ends_count`), against the default darkness on the same picture (`pieces_at_default_dark`, `lines_at_default_dark`, `dead_ends_at_default_dark`), in one line, and a note that `pieces retrace` reads one place at that darkness and keeps the rest. A line is two pieces with a line of their own between them, no further apart than a line and a quarter. On a traced scene (2026-11-18) a run lost one dark patch by tracing the whole picture at `--dark 18`, and 41 of its lines broke into dead ends: the reply that did it said nothing of them.
- **`pieces check MAP`** reads a map from either source (`bench/pieces_check.py`), each flag a figure with its piece, its limit and where, each read two ways:

| Flag | It is | Its limit | By its outline | Second method |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `dead-end` | a lead line that ends in mid-glass, or one inside a piece joined to no other | 1.5 leads long | the piece with every gap under two leads filled, out and back with mitred joins: a gap open at one end | rays from the line's end, which meet that piece ahead and on both sides; the map's pixels, for lead with one piece round it |
| `surrounds` | a piece with another inside it: the lead round the inner piece joins no line, and the frame would be two bodies | none | each hole of its outline that holds a piece | lead joined to no other, counted where its pixels join |
| `sliver` | a piece too narrow | `--min-width`, 3 mm at its widest | the largest circle that fits | its pixels' distance from its edge |
| `small` | a piece too small to handle | `--min-area`, 25 mm² | its area | its pixels |
| `neck` | the narrowest place straight across, with glass `--min-width` wide on both sides. With it on one side only it's a spur, a piece's thin end, and isn't flagged | `--min-neck`, 1.2 mm | `layout`'s nearest points facing each other, and a cut across the place | twice the pixels' distance to the edge at the place's middle, on pixels drawn round it 64 to its width: the same line across. `layout`'s rays square to each edge are a note |
| `notch` | a deep notch | `--max-notch`, deeper than its mouth is wide | the hull less the piece: each part's furthest point from its mouth | the furthest pixel in it from that mouth, short by whatever of the notch is too thin for a pixel's centre to lie in |
| `gradient` | a colour that runs across one piece | `--max-colour-run`, 40 of 255 | the picture's colour at the piece's two ends, as the trace read it, or read from the picture again on a piece a fix has changed | a plane fitted to its colour |
| `wide-lead` | a patch of lead, not a line | `--max-lead`, three usual leads | the largest circle that fits in the lead | the furthest lead pixel from glass |
| `no-opening` | with `--lip L`, the lip a build will be given: a piece that lip leaves no one opening in, as `pieces build` would refuse it. A piece with no pane whatever the lip is `no-pane`'s | one opening | `pieces build`'s own plan, the piece's pocket as it would be built: a waist the lip closes over with the line to split it along, an opening pinched in two, or none two nozzles wide | the piece's pixels further than the lip from its edge, which hold at least what a build's opening holds |
| `no-face` | with `--lip L --flush`, a flush pane whose raised face isn't one once `--face-play` (`--play` unless given) is taken from its opening, or would touch the lead as its body moves in its pocket ([the piece map](pieces.md#a-pane-level-with-the-lead)) | one face, off the lead | `pieces build --flush`'s own plan, in its words, with the place and the play that keeps it off | the piece's pixels further than the lip and the face's play from its edge, where a face can be |
| `no-pane` | a piece `pieces build` refuses whatever lip it's given: the play leaves no one pane of it, or its outline can't be fitted to lines and arcs with one pane in them. In the build's own words, with what cuts a pane in two and where. So `check` never passes a map a build refuses (mqucifer/print-shop#174) | one pane, at the build's `--fit` and `--play` | `pieces build`'s own reading of the pane and fit of the outline (`pieces_build.pane_refused`) | the piece's pixels further than the play and half a nozzle from its edge: one lot, and it's the outline's points the fit can't take, a step or two points all but on each other; none or two, and it's a thin place |
| `thin-lead` | lead between two pieces that no nozzle prints: the frame would have a wall that thin between two pockets | `--min-lead`, 0.4 mm | the nearest the two outlines come | twice the pixels' distance to the glass at the middle of that place, on pixels drawn round it as a neck's are |

  With every answer: the **glass share**, the pieces' area over the panel's, against the share the map's source had, and the **lead's widest point**. `FAIL` with any flag, naming the fix for each kind. The limits are the operator's tracer's on the first traced scene, and each is an option.
  - **A second check measures the same thing, and carries its resolution** (mqucifer/print-shop#161, decided with the Sponsor, 2026-11-17). On the first eval with these commands five of the first 25 calls ended `DISAGREE`, none of them two readings of one thing. A neck read 1.022 mm by the nearest points and 1.828 mm by rays square to its edges, at the same place: on a slanted neck the rays run the slant's long way, another line. And a notch read 2.77 mm deep by its outline and 1.74 by its pixels, its end too thin for a pixel to show. After every split `check` still ended `DISAGREE`, so a real one couldn't be told from these.
  - **`DISAGREE` is two readings of the same thing on opposite sides of a limit, each beyond the other's resolution** (`limits.apart`). Closer than the coarser one's resolution they're the same reading, and what's between them is a note, even with the limit there. Further apart on one side of the limit they agree on the rule, and the gap is a note.
  - **A neck's two readings** are the same line across: the nearest points, and twice the distance from the place's middle to the nearest pixel that isn't the piece's glass, since the nearest edge to the middle of the narrowest place is one of its two ends. Those pixels are drawn round the place, 64 to the narrower of its width and the limit, so the reading is good to 3.4 of them (a pixel at each end, and half a diagonal for where the middle lies): 0.05 mm on a 1 mm neck. `slanted.json` reads 1.022 mm by both, with the rays' 1.83 mm a note. `hairs.json` is the control: two cuts two microns wide, their ends 1 mm apart, which the outline has and no pixel shows, so the pixels read no edge within 2.5 mm, and that's `DISAGREE`.
  - **A dead end's pixel reading** looks a lead and a quarter round each lead pixel for one piece's glass and nothing else. So it can't surely show a line under two pixels wide, or one with another piece, or the panel's edge, nearer than that all along: such a dead end is flagged by its outline, and a note says the pixels can't show it and why. A trace of the operator's scene at `--dark 18`, where lines go a pixel or two thin beside narrow pieces, ended `DISAGREE` this way ("isn't lone lead by the pixels"); `thin_lines.json` is that, drawn in code. A line inside a piece nearer than two pixels to other lead isn't looked for as a body of its own either, as the pixels join the two. And a dead end is matched to the pixels by where it lies: a lead pixel alone with its piece, in the dead end's own shape. It was matched by the middle of the stretch of lead it's part of, which is far off where the line all but meets a long line inside the same piece (`joined.json`): on the scene a dead end 5.4 mm long and 0.68 mm wide, with nothing within 13 mm of it, wasn't found.
  - **Lead under a nozzle's width is flagged on the map** (`thin-lead`, mqucifer/print-shop#161). On the operator's scene a frame failed `thin` at ten places, the thinnest 0.135 mm, and the run learned it only after building: the map had 17 pairs of pieces under 0.4 mm apart, the nearest 0.16 mm. Each is now a flag with its two pieces, how wide and where, and its fix: `pieces widen` first, which keeps the line, and `pieces merge` only where the two are one colour and the picture doesn't mean the line. Sixteen of the scene's 17 lay between two pieces of one colour, a sun's rays, a river's inner lines and bush outlines: lines the picture means, which a merge would take out. Lead between a piece and the panel's edge isn't read, as the frame's border stands outside it (`close.json`).
  - **A second reading is said only where a rule flags the place.** A place under `--min-neck` that's a spur or a tip has no second reading, and a notch shallower than its mouth has no note, however its pixels read (`quiet.json`). Lead that the pixels read as alone with one piece where no outline has a dead end is one line with a count, not a note each. On the scene each check had printed four to six notes on places nothing flagged.
  - **A notch's pixel reading** is short by whatever of the notch has no pixel's centre in it. A pixel's centre lies within half its diagonal of any point, so what's left of the notch that far in from its edge is surely seen: the resolution is the outline's depth less that, and a pixel at least. `spiked.json`, 2.77 mm by its outline and 2.46 by its pixels, with 1 time its mouth between them, reads `FAIL` at the outline's figure with a note. A notch too thin all along for any pixel says it has no second check.
  - **A merge can't hide a gradient** (mqucifer/print-shop#172). A merge kept one piece's colour and dropped its run, so two colours joined read as one. A piece with no run of its own, one a merge, a split, a fill or a moved line made, has it read from the picture again, over the piece as it is now and only on pixels that aren't lead in the picture, as a line a merge took out is still drawn under the glass. The fix's own reply flags it, and its map keeps the run. The picture is the one the map's source names (`source.image_path`, as the trace was given it), or `--image FILE`. **Where the picture isn't there, the verdict says so:** it names the pieces whose run wasn't read and why (`colour_unread`). With no other rule broken `check` is `PARTIAL`, not `OK`, as a rule that wasn't read hasn't passed; with one it's `FAIL`, and still names them; and a fix's own verdict ends "Not read: ...". A map traced before this kept only the picture's file name, so one traced from a picture in a folder reads this way until it's given `--image` (`bench/pieces_picture.py`, `test_a_merged_pieces_gradient...`).
  - **The advice names only commands that exist** (mqucifer/print-shop#172). For a patch of lead `check` said "redraw it as glass", and no command did. `test_the_advice_names_only_commands_that_exist` reads every `pieces` command the advice names for every flag, and every one the bench's code and the skill name, and runs `--help` on each.
- **The fixes** (`bench/pieces_fix.py`), each writing a new map and saying the pieces, the glass share and the lead's widest point before and after, and each changed piece as `check` reads it:
  - **`pieces merge MAP A B`**: A joins its neighbour B, which keeps its number and colour, and the line between them is glass. The line is what a closing of the two fills, out and back by a lead and a quarter with mitred joins, within reach of both, and all the lead the two face each other across. It's refused when they don't touch, naming A's neighbours. **Two pieces with no line of their own between them,** further apart than a lead and a quarter, are joined across the patch of lead between them: the lead inside the smaller one's hull with what of the larger lies near it, clear of every other piece (mqucifer/print-shop#161). On the operator's scene a piece 1.6 mm from its nearest neighbour of its colour was joined to it by a neck, with two scraps of the old lead left as lines inside the piece, and a run fixed it over three builds. `apart.json`, a diamond with one corner 1.6 mm from a large piece, wasn't joined at all ("don't touch"); it's joined now for 27 mm² of lead, with no hole, and builds first time. The reply says how far apart they were.
  - **`pieces split MAP ID --line x1,y1,x2,y2,...`**: a new lead line across a piece, the map's lead wide, run on past each end. One that ends in the piece's glass is refused, saying which end. Across a piece that surrounds another, from its hole to its edge, it opens the piece.
  - **`pieces extend MAP --at X,Y [--aim X,Y]`**: a dead-end line runs on, as wide as it is, straight on or toward `--aim`, to where it leaves the piece's glass.
  - **`pieces drop-line MAP --at X,Y`**: a dead end is filled with its piece's glass. On a line between two pieces it's their merge, the smaller joining the larger.
  - **`pieces widen MAP A B [--lead MM]`** (mqucifer/print-shop#161): the line between A and B, where it's under `--lead` (0.4 mm, a nozzle's width), becomes that wide. Each piece's edge moves back by half of what's short, only along the stretch that's under, tapering into the rest, so the line keeps its path and its middle. The cut is a band the width asked, round the middle of the lead as each piece's own edge finds it. It's refused where there's nothing under the width, or where the wider line would cut a piece in two. The reply says the width before and after, how far along the line, and the glass that's lead now: `near.json`, a curved line 0.2 mm wide along 10 mm, is 0.40 mm after, along 10.5 mm, for 2.2 mm² of glass, and every point outside the stretch is as it was.
  - **`pieces fix MAP STEPS`** (mqucifer/print-shop#161): a file of those fixes, made in order in one call with one reply. A map takes dozens of fixes, and the first eval chained them from its own scripts. STEPS is a JSON list, or `{"steps": [...]}`, each step a fix's name (`merge`, `split`, `extend`, `drop-line`, `widen`, `retrace`, `fill`, `paint` or `move-line`) and what its command takes ([the piece map](pieces.md#a-file-of-fixes)). Each step is made on the map the one before left, as the next command would read it from its file, so the map it writes is the one the same commands write one by one, point for point (`test_a_file_of_five_fixes...`). The reply says each step's result with the pieces and the glass share after it, then the pieces, the glass share and the lead's widest point before and after them all, and each changed piece as `check` reads it on the last map. **It stops at the first step that's refused** and writes the map up to it: `PARTIAL`, naming the step and why, so the steps left go to that map. Refused at its first step it's `NO ANSWER` and nothing is written, and a file that can't be read changes nothing, as every step is read before any is made. **Second method:** each step's two maps as pixels, as a fix on its own.
  - **Four fixes work on one place and keep the rest** (`bench/pieces_local.py`, mqucifer/print-shop#172). Every fix above is a piece's or a line's, and nothing worked on an area: with most of a map right and one place wrong, the only lever was to trace the whole picture again. Each of these is also a step in `pieces fix`, recorded in the map's `history` like the others.
    - **`pieces retrace MAP --box X0,Y0,X1,Y1 [--dark N] [--palette ...] [--image FILE]`** traces the picture again inside the box and splices it into the map. The box is moved in to the picture's pixels (`box_mm`). Inside it the lead is read as a trace reads it, at `--dark` (the map's own unless given), on the box and a margin round it, so an outline that crosses the edge is smoothed as it runs and then cut there. A piece that reaches out of the box keeps all it has outside, point for point, its number and its colour, and joins what's found inside wherever the two share two pixels of the box's edge or more: so a line that crosses the edge stays where it was and runs on into the line found inside. A piece wholly inside is replaced, in the nearest of `--palette` (the map's colours unless given). The reply says the pieces, the lines and the dead ends the box held before and after, the lines that cross its edge (`lines_across_box_edge`) with the widest step where the two sides meet (`box_edge_step_mm`), which the outline found inside takes up so no piece's outline runs along the box's edge, and each line that ends at the edge with where (`lines_ending_at_box_edge`): lead on one side of the edge and glass on the other. **Second method:** the new map drawn back at the picture's scale over the patches found in the box, and the two maps' pixels outside the box, none of which may differ.
    - **`pieces fill MAP --at X,Y [--lead MM]`** turns a patch of lead into glass. The patch is the lead at X,Y wider than a line and a half, to its own edges and none of a line that leaves it. Each piece round it takes the part nearer to it than to any other, less half a line along each border between two shares, so a line `--lead` wide (the map's) is left down the middle between each two pieces and the lines that met the patch run on to each other. The borders are found from points 0.1 mm apart along the pieces' edges, and each is then drawn as the line those stand for, to the map's own detail, a pixel of the picture it was traced from: a share's edge is its border's side, `--lead` wide, and not the steps it was found from (mqucifer/print-shop#174). A scrap of lead beside the patch that the share shuts in or leaves as a line ending in the piece, too short to be a line, under a dead end's length, is glass in that piece too (`patch_scraps_count`, `patch_scraps_mm2`). Only lead becomes glass (`glass_taken_mm2` is 0). The reply says the patch's area and widest point, each piece's share (`patch_shares`), what's left as lines, and the dead ends at the patch before and after (`dead_ends_at_patch_before`, `dead_ends_at_patch_after`). A point on a line is refused, saying how wide a patch is. **Second method:** each pixel that was lead and is glass, against the piece nearest it in the map before, by the pixels' own distances.
    - **`pieces paint MAP ID COLOUR [--image FILE]`** gives one piece another of the map's colours, one the bench has a name for, or a new one as `NAME=#rrggbb`. Nothing moves. The reply says how far the colour is from the picture's own in that piece, as a distance in RGB (`colour_off`), how far the old colour was (`colour_was_off`), and the nearest of the map's colours. **Second method:** the picture's colour over the piece read now, against the colour the trace kept.
    - **`pieces move-line MAP A B --line x1,y1,x2,y2,...`** draws the line between two neighbours again along new points, as wide as it was (`line_width_mm`, the middle of the distances between the two). The two pieces and their line are one shape, and the new line cuts it in two from edge to edge, so every line that met the old one still meets lead; each piece keeps its number and colour. It's refused, naming which, where the path runs into another piece's glass or leaves the two across lead that isn't their line ("crosses another line"), where it doesn't cut the two in two, and where it leaves either piece a sliver, a small piece or a neck by `check`'s limits that it wasn't before. A longer line is more lead: the reply says the glass each way.
    - **`retrace`, `fill` and `move-line` read what they made** (mqucifer/print-shop#174): each changed piece's outline points before and after, and whether it builds, by `pieces build`'s own fit at its usual `--fit` and `--play` (`outlines`, each with `points_before`, `points_after`, `builds`, `built_before`). A piece that built before the fix and doesn't after it is `FAIL`, naming the piece: the map it leaves is written for looking at, and in `pieces fix` the step isn't made. On an eval a fill's shares followed their borders step by step, one outline went from 62 points to 268, and the build refused the map the fill had called `OK`.
    - **Back-tested** on pictures and maps drawn in code (`tests/test_pieces_local.py`): a 9 by 7 mm dark patch in one cell, read as lead 7.08 mm wide, is cleared by a retrace of a box round it at `--dark 20`, which adds the patch's 49.5 mm² as glass and takes none, with the 20 outline points outside the box the same and no pixel outside it changed; a band of lead between three pieces is shared 324.0, 158.76 and 158.76 mm², the hand figures; a colour's distance is the hand figure, 277.63; and a line bent 4 mm moves a 40 mm² triangle from one piece to the other.
  - **A piece on the panel's edge is inside it,** to 0.01 mm. One further out is refused by every command, saying which piece, how far and where, and the panel's corners. A merge joins two pieces up to the panel's edge and no further.
  - **Lines meet as lines.** A fix is one piece's: every other outline is the same point for point, which the reply checks, and no corner is rounded. Two cells merged across a straight line are one rectangle again (`test_a_merge_joins_a_sliver...`).
  - **A fix becomes glass, not lead** (the Sponsor, on the first test's sheet, 2026-11-17: "it should have replaced it with more glass and less lead line"). A merge and a dropped line only add glass; a split or an extension takes one line's worth, and a widening half of what a line is short, each side. **Second method:** the two maps as pixels, the glass added and taken counted.
- **`pieces build MAP --thickness T --pane P --lip FULL[,LEAST] --play C [--min-visible S] [--layer H] [--border MM] [--fit MM]`** writes `frame.step` and `pane_COLOUR.step` for each colour, each pane its own solid where it sits (`bench/pieces_build.py`).
  - **Each pane** is its piece inset by the play, less what a nozzle can't print: a tip or a spur under 0.4 mm, its corners rounded by 0.2. **Each pocket** is its pane and the play round it. **Each opening** is the pocket inset by the lip, cut through, so a lip stands over the pane's edge on both faces: the lip asked, on every piece (mqucifer/print-shop#161). A piece's lip used to step down by tenths of a mm, to a second figure, until its opening was one piece: on the operator's scene a piece 14 mm wide with a waist took 0.6 mm all round, and `capture`, at the rim of 1.2 the request asked, failed it along 144 mm of its edge. A piece with a waist the lip closes over is now refused, with the waist's width and place and the split's own line, `pieces split MAP ID --line x1,y1,x2,y2`, as a glass cutter splits a neck (`waist.json`). The line is the shortest straight one across the piece at the narrow place that leaves a part of the opening on each side, tried every 5 degrees and then every half, and cut as `split` will cut it, so `split` takes it as given. The place is found in the piece itself: what the lip closes over between the opening's two parts, a waist, a narrow run round a bend, or where an edge comes in and pinches the opening (`slanted_waist.json`, `bend.json`). On the scene one refusal had given a slanted piece's waist as 18 mm, a line along it, and another, for a piece round a bend, had given no line and "the parts 49.84 mm apart". A piece is judged on its pocket as it's built, its curves moved inside its outline, and refused the same way: judged on its outline alone, a waist 2.45 mm across passed and the build then ended `NO ANSWER`, "leaves no one opening", with no place and no fix (`curved_waist.json`). `pieces check --lip L` flags each such piece first (`no-opening`): it makes the build's own plan, so what it flags is what the build would refuse, in the build's words, and nothing else. **A build names everything that stops a piece together:** each line inside it, and what would still stop it with those out, where it named one a build. A piece that can't be built is named, with what stops it and the command that fixes it, and nothing is built: too narrow all over; a dead end or a narrow place that cuts its pane or opening in two, said with where; a line inside it joined to no other; or another piece inside it. No line says a wide piece has no pane without what cuts it.
  - **The heights** stand on layer lines: the pocket in the middle of the frame, its top the first layer line that leaves the pane 0.1 mm or more. The reply names the pause's height, the checks to run and `done`'s `glass`.
  - **Curves are built as curves** (the Sponsor, 2026-11-05: "Build it right the first time with right tools"). A turn sharper than 30° is a corner. Between corners the outline is a line, one arc, or a pair of arcs that meet without a kink, each spanning as many points as stay within `--fit` (0.1 mm) of it, and the sides between them within twice that. The pocket is those arcs and the pane the same arcs moved in by the play, a line moving over and an arc keeping its centre, so the play is exact. Where a pane has a spur, or a tip sharper than about 45°, which no nozzle prints, it's cut back and rounded and its pocket follows it, within 0.003 mm, and the reply names the piece. An opening is the pocket's arcs moved in by the lip, or where a bend is tighter than the lip, fitted so the lip is the lip asked for or up to `--fit` wider: an inset half of `--fit` deeper, fitted within a quarter of `--fit` of its points, so within half of it between them. It was fitted within half of `--fit` of its points, and a side between two stood up to `--fit` off: on the operator's scene `capture` read a rim of 1.155 mm for 1.2 along 15 mm of one pane, and on `speckled.png` 1.177 mm (mqucifer/print-shop#161). OpenCascade's own `offset2D` failed on 11 of 36 outlines of the test scene, so it isn't used.
  - **Second method:** the frame's volume by OpenCascade against its faces' areas and the heights, and one solid; each pane's volume against its inset; each pane's edge against the play, at points round it.
  - **With `--min-visible S`, each piece's lip is the rim `capture` would ask of its pane** (mqucifer/print-shop#161), by `capture`'s own function (`capture.rim_needed`): FULL where the pane keeps S percent visible with it, else the widest lip that keeps S, never under LEAST. A piece that can't keep S at LEAST is refused, with its two shares and its fix. So a build and `capture --full-rim FULL --least-rim LEAST --min-visible S` can't differ on a rim. On the operator's scene every piece had taken the full 1.2 mm, and a long curved piece was 43.1% visible where 60 was asked: `capture` said to set its rim to 0.874 mm, and the build's plan now gives that piece 0.874. `crescent.json`, 5 mm across, was 51.9% visible at 1.2 mm and `capture` failed it; built with `--min-visible 60` its lip is 1.031 mm, the figure `capture` gave, and `capture` passes. A lip sized this way is the pane moved in at a true distance, round at a corner that points in, as `capture` reckons the share, and held to 0.003 mm. It's `capture`'s shape whole: a part of it under two nozzles wide, a tail past a pinch, is cut too, and the reply says a slot that narrow may print closed. Left out, three panes of the operator's scene read 58.8% to 59.5% visible at the rim that keeps 60, "the frame covers it elsewhere"; `tailed.json` read 55.8%, and passes. There's no default: the share is the request's, as `capture`'s is. `pieces check --lip FULL,LEAST --min-visible S` reads the same.
  - **A pocket never stands past its piece's outline, into the lead** (mqucifer/print-shop#161), so a wall between two pockets is never thinner than the map's line. The outline is fitted to lines and arcs within `--fit`, and those are then moved in by however far the furthest of them stood past the outline (`pocket_moved_in_mm`): a line moves over and an arc keeps its centre, so they're the same curves, as few, and all inside. Left as fitted, a pocket stood up to twice `--fit` past its outline on each side of a line: on the operator's scene every line was 0.4 mm or more in the map, and `thin` found eight walls of 0.21 to 0.40 mm in the frame. `near.json`, widened to 0.4 mm and built, failed `thin` at 0.30 mm and passes. **What it costs is glass:** each pocket is up to 1.6 of `--fit` inside its outline where it was up to that either side, so at the default `--fit 0.1` about 2% to 2.7% of the map's glass is lead (`glass_left_as_lead_mm2`: 2.00% on `hills.png`, 2.65% on `speckled.png`, from 0.07% and 0.10%), with the faces as they were (415 to 428, 849 to 856) and no strips. A smaller `--fit` gives the glass back for more faces and strips: at 0.05, 1.25% and 44 strip faces on `hills.png`; at 0.025, 0.26% and 148. A pocket fitted again round a cut-back pane that still stands past is made again further in, and one that's past by more than 0.012 mm is `DISAGREE`.
  - **A pane is held to its play square to its pocket's sides** (mqucifer/print-shop#161): at each point of its edge that lies square to the pocket side its own side was moved from, the gap to that side is the play, to 0.012 mm, and nowhere is it nearer than the play to any of the wall. Round a corner that points into the pocket the pane's own corner is rightly further off, 0.354 mm for a play of 0.25 at a square corner, as a corner stays a corner. The reading was the middle gap of all the points, and on the live eval a piece whose outline was a traced edge's pixel steps, 1,022 sides of 0.228 mm, read 0.284 mm and `DISAGREE`: `stairs.json` is that, drawn in code. A point is read against its own side, not the nearest of the wall: beside a tooth a tenth of a millimetre long, the nearest wall to a pane's corner is the next side along, further off than the play, which read 0.338 mm on `thin_line.png`'s piece 14 and 0.292 mm on the scene. A pane really 0.03 mm further in is still `DISAGREE` (`planted_play.py`), saying which piece's pane, where along a side, the gap there and at its nearest, and whether its pocket was fitted again. That's a pane against its own pocket: `--fit` is how far the pocket may be from the map's outline, and doesn't govern it.
  - **With `--flush [--face-play C] [--lip-thick MM] [--body MM]`, each pane is level with the lead** (mqucifer/print-shop#287): its body in the pocket as now, and a raised face its opening's shape, the play smaller all round, down through the opening to the bed. Each colour's panes are also written turned over for printing, `pane_COLOUR-laid.step`, and the build writes `section.png`. A raised face that would touch the lead as its body is pushed round its pocket is refused, naming the place and the `--face-play` that keeps it off. [The piece map](pieces.md#a-pane-level-with-the-lead) has the heights, what's refused, and its second method from the files.
  - **The existing checks read it as any frame** (`test_the_existing_checks_read_a_build...`): `capture`, `pause`, `thin`, `light` and `info`, which finds no strips.
  - **A build is byte-stable** (mqucifer/print-shop#180): the same map, options and image write the same STEP data every time, whatever the process, its hash seed or where it lies in memory, so `paths.content_hash` is the same and a pick on the files holds after a clean run builds them again. Each ring of lines and arcs is built with one vertex at each joint, which both its edges share. It was built edge by edge, each edge with vertices of its own, a line's at its points and an arc's where its circle gives them, 1e-16 mm or so apart, and joining them kept one of the two at each joint by where each lay in memory: the first step that varied was the prism, `BRepPrimAPI_MakePrism`, laying a side face's line through one point or the other from an identical face, and so a 2D curve's point in the STEP file, `0.` in one build and `-1.08E-16` in the next. On eval-organic-3's map, twelve builds in fresh containers, each with its own `PYTHONHASHSEED`, gave its frame four versions and two pane files two each; the same twelve give one each now, with the same volumes (`~/print-shop/proofs/m20-build-stable/build-stable/`). `test_build_stable.py` builds a map drawn there three times in fresh processes and holds every file to one hash: before, its frame and one pane file came out two ways in eight builds. A pick doesn't rest on it alone: `done` holds a pick by geometry where only the bytes differ ([rounds](rounds.md)).
- **Measured** on a scene drawn in code, 37 pieces of curved bands at 160 mm and 8 pixels a mm (`~/print-shop/proofs/m20-pieces/valley-after.md`, in `print-shop/bench:0.15` on the Mac): the trace takes 11 s and flags one sliver; merged into its neighbour, `check` passes in 5 s; the build takes 8 s. Its frame is 10.1 MB and 2,241 faces, about 62 a piece, and the harness's own `info` reads 0 strips in it: 400 flat faces under 1 mm, none in a run. Each pane's edge is within 0.01 mm of the play. `capture` holds all 36 panes in 26 s, and `pause`, `light`, `info` and `thin --one-body` each answer in 8 to 13 s.
- **Fewer arcs** (the operator, on mqucifer/print-shop#158). The first build of that scene was 25.2 MB and 5,154 faces (`valley.md`). Three things cut it to 10.1 MB and 2,241: a traced outline loses each point within a pixel of the line through its neighbours, where it kept a shallow slope's one-pixel steps and each took two arcs; the pocket is the outline's own arcs, where it was fitted again from the pane; and one arc stands where one fits, in place of a pair. What's left is the scene's own: about 24 points and 10 corners a piece, each side an arc or a line. At `--fit 0.2` the frame is 7.8 MB and 1,729 faces, but two outlines then pinch a line between them, and the build says `DISAGREE`: the frame is two solids.
- **From the operator's run on a traced scene** (mqucifer/print-shop#158, 2026-11-17), each now a fixture drawn in code (`margin.png`, `specks.png`, `deep.json`): a margin above an uneven frame was traced as a piece and made the panel too tall; dark specks inside pieces read as dead ends, most of the flags; and `build` said two wide pieces had no pane, giving their width and not the dead end that cut it. On the commit before, those fixtures read 6 pieces at 88.3 mm tall for 5 at 80.1, 12 lone lines for none, and a reason with no cause (`~/print-shop/proofs/m20-pieces/before-operator-fixes.txt`).
- **A trace that ended `DISAGREE`** on the operator's second run, in two halves, each now a fixture (`notched.json`, `lone.json`) that read `DISAGREE` on the commit before and reads its flags now. A neck is the narrowest place by the nearest points facing each other, as the rule says it; rays square to the edges are its second check only where one runs between the same two sides, and where their narrowest is another place that's a note. Where both read under the limit at different figures (`stepped.json`, 0.854 and 1.1 mm) they agree it's a neck: the flag stands at the nearest points' figure, with the rays' figure and the gap said, as rays square to a curved or a stepped side read wider. Rays square to the edges were then its second check where one ran between the same two sides, and `DISAGREE` where the two landed on opposite sides of the limit: mqucifer/print-shop#161 made them a note there too, as they measure another line, and gave the neck the pixels' reading above. Lead joined to no other is matched by where it lies, not by how many: two lines a hair apart are one body in the pixels, and a line thinner than half a lead isn't one a pixel shows. And a stretch of lead along a thick line between two pieces is no longer noted as a dead end to look at.
- **`pieces dome --diameter D --height H --repeats N --bands FILE [--top MM] [--to FILE]`** makes a dome shade's map from rules (`bench/pieces_dome.py`, mqucifer/print-shop#154): one motif repeated N times round, in bands from the top to the hem, each band a `grid`, a `swag`, `jewels`, a `strip`, `triangles` or a scalloped hem (`scallop`), with its own figures and its cells' colours. [The bands file and the map](pieces.md#a-dome-from-rules-and-its-flat-slices) are written down once.
  - **The surface** is half an ellipsoid, open `--top` across at its top (a fifth of the diameter unless given), and the reply says so, with the dome's side from the opening's edge to the hem (`meridian_mm`), which the bands' heights add up to.
  - **Lines meet as lines, by construction:** every band's edge runs all the way round, every repeat's side from the top to the hem, and every other line from one of those to another. A piece is its cell less half the lead along each line, as far on the dome wherever it is.
  - **Its picture** comes with it, as a trace's does: the dome in its colours from the side and from 35° over it, one slice laid flat with its pieces numbered and each fold's bend, and the chart ([the six-band dome's](pieces-dome.png)).
  - **The map is the dome's chart,** round by up, true to size up and down and round the hem. `check` and `build` read a flat panel, so the reply names the next command, and `check` on the chart says its widths aren't the glass's.
  - **Second method:** the glass share on the dome by each outline's own area there, against the chart's pixels each weighed by the dome's radius at its row; and the pixels' separate patches of glass against the pieces, with the lead between them one body.
- **`pieces flatten MAP [--slices N] [--rows R] [--bed X,Y] [--tolerance MM] [--max-off MM] [--to NAME]`** cuts a dome's map along lead lines into N slices, the map's repeats unless given, and lays each flat as its own map (`bench/pieces_flatten.py`).
  - **A slice is a stack of flat rows,** one between each two lead lines that run all the way round, read from the map. Its four corners are on the dome, and a place between them goes as far between the folds and the cuts as it is on the dome.
  - **Rows against bends.** A slice folds at every such line unless `--rows R` asks for fewer, when it folds at the R − 1 that leave its rows nearest the dome. The reply says the rows used of those the map allows, how far they stand from the dome, and what every other count would give (`rows_choice`): on the test dome 7 rows stand 4.17 mm off with folds up to 16.0°, and 4 rows 4.46 mm with folds up to 23.6°.
  - **The reply gives** each slice's flat outline and whether it fits `--bed` (256 by 256); each row's tilt; each fold's height on the map and the angle it bends through (`folds`); the angle between two neighbours' rows across a cut (`join_deg`); which slices are the same (`alike`); and how far the flat rows stand from the curved dome (`rows_off_dome_mm`). Each slice's map holds its folds and the edge it shares with each neighbour, on the map and on the dome.
  - **A slice's panel ends at its glass,** half a lead inside each cut's middle: `pieces build --border` of half the lead puts the frame's edge on the cut, so two slices meet in one line as wide as the lead.
  - **Verdict:** `OK`; `FAIL` where a slice doesn't fit the bed, or the rows stand further off the dome than `--max-off`; `DISAGREE` where a slice bent back isn't the dome's within `--tolerance` (0.01 mm). A cut that crosses a piece is `NO ANSWER`, naming the pieces.
  - **Second method:** each slice's file read back and bent by its own angles, a chain of rows from the hem up, each turned by its fold's bend, then turned round the axis. Its corners are held to their places on the dome by the ellipse and the angle round, each piece's points to the flat through their row's three corners, and each edge to its neighbour's. That's the same thing read two ways, so apart is `DISAGREE`. How far a flat row stands from the curve is another thing, and a reading.
- **A dome's slices join with no glue** (`bench/pieces_seam.py`, `bench/pieces_came.py`, mqucifer/print-shop#166), **held from the inside** (mqucifer/print-shop#190). The Sponsor, 2026-11-18: "I'm not a fan of glued seems for 3d prints. It needs some mechanical hold." And 2026-11-20, of the first strip's flange over the outer face: the seam "should be hidden". [The piece map](pieces.md#the-seams-between-slices) has each figure, and why this hold.
- **`pieces build SLICE ... --seam [--seam-gap MM]`** builds a slice's two long edges as seams.
  - **Each straight length of an edge,** one a flat row, is bevelled by half the angle between the two slices there, `flatten`'s `join_deg`, and set back half the gap, 0.6 mm unless given: two neighbours' faces stand face to face, with nothing between them. The frame's inner face lies on the slice's rows.
  - **Each length has a groove in the inner face** for a came strip's rib, 0.6 mm wide and 0.4 deep, 0.4 mm in from the seam's face. Its roof is a bridge as the frame prints, and it leaves 0.4 mm of lip over it under a pocket.
  - **Verdict:** `FAIL`, with nothing built, where a face would leave under 0.4 mm of lead beside a pocket or stand back past a pane's edge, or a groove under 0.4 mm of lead beside a pane's opening or of lip over it: each place with its figure, and the lead that would leave room (`lead_needed_mm`). A map that isn't a slice's is `NO ANSWER`.
  - **Second method:** each join by the rows' own corners on the dome against `flatten`'s figure, each seam's face read back from the frame against its bevel, each groove asked of the frame by points, and the frame's volume against its sections in Shapely less the grooves. The dome's own surface normals give the curve's angle, not the rows': a note.
- **`pieces came SLICE --thickness T --lip L [--gap MM] [--play MM] [--grip MM] [--bar REACH,THICK] [--hold rib|h] [--flange REACH,THICK] [--hem MM] [--top MM]`** makes the came strips for a slice's right seam, one for each straight length: a bar inside the dome under both slices' inner faces, with a rib square to each into its groove, `--grip` (0.10 mm, the Sponsor's PETG snap) a side tighter. The ribs stand at the angle between the slices, so once both slices are on, a strip comes off neither. Nothing is in the gap or over the outer face. `--hold h` makes mqucifer/print-shop#166's H, with a flange over each outer face, to read against.
  - **Verdict:** `FAIL` where a strip would reach a pane's opening, past the lead and the lip, lies over the outer face (the H), has anything under two lines (0.8 mm, by rays square to its outline), stands on no more than two lines' width along its length, or as laid needs a support. Each strip's `prints` says what stands on the bed, its bridge, and any face in the air; `least_feature_mm` and `on_bed_mm2` the least of them.
  - **Second method:** each strip's angle and its rib, or its slot, read back from its own faces, and its volume against its section's area.
- **`pieces ring SLICE --thickness T [--border MM] [--depth MM] [--play MM] [--wall MM] [--hole MM]`** makes the rings: a hem ring, and a top ring in two parts, a seat and a cap, as a groove near a dome's top lies nearly level and one part's wall would print in the air. Each has a groove for every slice's edge. The hole through the top's two parts is a stated figure for the lamp's fitting.
  - **Verdict:** `FAIL` where an edge isn't held on every side, or a part as laid needs a support. A scalloped hem has no hem ring, and a note says so.
  - **Second method:** each slice's edge asked of the rings: solid just past its outer face, its inner and its end, and empty where the edge goes.
- **`pieces seam A B --frames FA FB [--gap MM] [--came FILE] [--press MM] [--rings FILE...]`** places two built neighbours and reads how they meet. Each frame is cut at its folds into flat rows, and each row is set where its slice's map puts it, bent by the folds and turned round the dome.
  - **The reply gives,** at each length: how far off parallel the two faces are, how far apart they stand against the gap they were built for, and what the rows share by OpenCascade's `common`. With `--came`, what of each strip lies over each slice's outer face and its inner, how it holds each slice (a rib in its groove, or a channel round its edge), how far it presses in, and where it lies over an opening. With `--rings`, whether each slice's hem and top edge is in a groove. It writes both slices as placed, for `render`.
  - **Verdict:** `OK`; `INTERFERE` where slices, strips or rings share more than the grip; `FAIL` where the faces stand another gap apart, a strip lies over an outer face, doesn't hold both slices or lies over an opening, or an edge isn't in a groove.
  - **Second method:** points, with no boolean: the faces' own corners, moved 0.02 mm into their own row, asked whether they're inside the other; and the nearest the rows come, by OpenCascade's distance between shapes. What lies over an outer face, by points just past it as well as by the strip's corners.
  - **It can't read** how a heated lead line bends: each row is placed as a rigid flat piece.
- **Measured, the seams with the H strips** of mqucifer/print-shop#166, before mqucifer/print-shop#190 (`~/print-shop/proofs/m20-seams/seams.txt`, made by `run.sh` beside it, in `print-shop/bench:0.15` on the Mac, 2026-11-18). On a test dome of the planned size with the planned lead of 1.4 mm and three bands, 12 slices of 4 rows:
  - `build --seam` makes a frame in 2 s, bevelled 14.77° at the hem's length to 6.32° at the top's, with 0.45 mm of lead left beside a pocket; `capture` (least lip 0.949 mm), `pause` and `thin --one-body` pass on it.
  - `came` makes 4 strips, 53.4 to 22.4 mm long, their channels 29.54° to 12.65° apart, each 2.88 mm wide with a slot of 3.1 mm, a mouth of 2.9 and a web of 0.4; each stands 2.97 mm tall on the bed, its web a bridge 3.21 mm long, with no face in the air.
  - `ring` makes the hem ring, the top's seat and its cap; all 36 points at the hem and 36 at the top are inside a ring, and each part prints with no support.
  - `seam` on slices 1 and 2 with the strips and the rings: the faces 0.6000 mm apart and 0.0000° off parallel, nothing shared, 4 of 4 strips round both edges, a press of 0.050 mm, no flange over an opening, and both slices' edges in the grooves, in 3 s.
  - The controls: two slices built with no seam are `INTERFERE`, 29.54° off parallel; a neighbour built with no gap is `FAIL`, 0.300 mm apart; a gap of 1.6 mm is refused at 8 places; a flange of 3.5 mm is refused by `came`, and made anyway it's found over openings at 264 places; strips for a 2.4 mm frame press 0.650 mm in, `INTERFERE`; rings for a 2 mm frame share 260.9 mm³, `INTERFERE`.
- **Measured, the seam held from the inside** (`~/print-shop/proofs/m20-came-inside/came-inside.txt`, made by `run.sh` beside it, in `print-shop/bench:0.15` on the Mac at 1760dd2, 2026-11-20). On the same three-band dome, 12 slices of 4 rows, and on the six-band dome below, of 7 rows:
  - `build --seam` makes each frame in 5 to 9 s with a groove along each of 4 lengths of both edges (7 on the six-band dome): 0.45 mm of lead beside a pocket as before (0.4475 on the six-band dome), 0.57 mm between a groove and the nearest opening, 0.4 mm of lip over it, and every point round every groove read back as cut. `capture` (least lip 0.949 mm), `pause` and `thin --one-body` pass on both slices of both domes.
  - `came` makes 4 strips, 53.4 to 22.4 mm long, their ribs 29.54° to 12.65° apart, each rib 0.8 mm in a groove of 0.6, the bar 3.31 mm wide and 0.8 mm proud of the inner face; nothing is under 0.8 mm, each lies on its bar (71.8 mm² at the least) with nothing in the air and no bridge, and its ribs read back within 0.0000°. On the six-band dome the 7 mm strip stands on 20.4 mm², where the H stood on 11.6.
  - `seam` on slices 1 and 2 with the strips and the rings: the faces 0.6000 mm apart, nothing shared, all 4 strips holding both slices by a rib in each groove, a press of 0.100 mm, nothing over either outer face by corners or by points, nothing over an opening, and the edges in the grooves; on the six-band dome all 7 strips, with the top ring only, as its hem is scalloped.
  - The controls: the H (`came --hold h`) is `FAIL`, 0.85 mm proud of the outer face and 0.19 mm at its narrowest, and `seam` finds all its strips over the outer face, 0.85 mm proud and over 1.16 mm of it; a lip of 0.9 is refused at 8 places (14 on the six-band dome), a groove 0.27 mm from an opening, with a lead of 1.7 mm needed; strips made for a gap of 1.0 press 0.300 mm in, `INTERFERE`; a bar of 3.5 mm is refused by `came`, and made anyway it's found over openings at 532 places.
- **Measured** on a dome of the planned size, 200 mm across and 130 tall with 12 repeats and one band of each pattern (`~/print-shop/proofs/m20-dome/six/six.md`, in `print-shop/bench:0.15` on the Mac): `dome` makes 228 pieces in 2 s, 85.19% glass on the dome by the outlines and 85.21% by the pixels. `flatten` cuts 12 slices in 2 s, each 49.9 by 159.0 mm in 7 rows, all the same map; its folds bend 4.3° to 16.0°; bent back, corners, flats and edges are within 0.00001 mm; the rows stand 4.17 mm from the curve at the furthest, in the hem's row. Slice 1 has 19 pieces: `check` passes in 2 s with no rule break; `build --border 0.7` makes one frame of 348 faces and 19 panes in 6 colours in 2 s; `capture` holds all 19 (least lip 0.949 mm), and `pause`, `thin --one-body`, `light` and `info` each answer in under 6 s.
- **Limits.** No model has run these yet. A map's panes are one thickness, and a round panel isn't traced. A dome's surface is the one profile, and its slices are flat across: more slices, or more lines round the dome, are what bring them nearer the curve. Bending a slice and the lamp's fitting are still to come: the top ring's hole is a stated figure until the fitting is chosen, and its seat and cap close the dome's top but for that hole. A scalloped hem has no hem ring yet. `seam` reads two slices, not all of a dome's.

**Light and pane thickness** are the stained-glass toolset's readings of what a person sees (`bench/glass.py`). In all three stained-glass runs light through each pane was measured by hand, outside the bench, and on two a floor went under the panes. `light` reads it along z, as the piece hangs flat to the light: a floor under a pane reads 0%, and a wide lip on a small cell reads low. The grid's band is a quarter of its spacing along every edge it crosses, as a straight edge between grid points misclassifies that much on average, and at worst all one way. `thickness` reads each pane's height, and rays through it, so a pane thicker in one place reads both. **Neither has a default limit:** a limit is the request's, `--min-open` or `--max` and `--min`, and with none each is a reading that passes and says so. How much light PETG lets through at a thickness isn't the bench's.
