`params` reads a number assigned once at the top level of a design's script (`BaseCircle = 20.0`), and runs the script in a scratch folder, so the design isn't touched. A parameter that moves nothing is `NO CHANGE`. On a Fusion copy, changing `BaseCircle` left the outer ring and motif in place while Fusion reported everything healthy (mqucifer/print-shop#15): this names each piece that moved and each that didn't. One that leaves pieces behind is `PARTIAL`, and `--max-left N` says how many may stay and still read `OK`. `--keep DIR` copies what the changed run wrote into a new folder, under the same names: the refinement pass checks a proposed change to a part on those files ([the rounds](rounds.md#the-refinement-pass)).

**Checks hold what a need says** (mqucifer/print-shop#24). Each of these can now say what a need says, and fails otherwise:

| A need's words | What used to pass | Now |
|---|---|---|
| A margin over a pane | No room at all: `--max-gap` is only a ceiling | `pause --min-room R`, and `least_room_over_pane_mm` is a number a need can limit |
| One connected solid | Several bodies | `thin --one-body` |
| Moves every piece | One piece moved of several | `params` is `PARTIAL` when pieces stay behind |
| So many panes | Fewer panes read than files | `capture --panes N` |

No command can tell what a need means, so these are asked for. With nothing asked, they read as they did, except `params`.

**A sheet shows what it asks about** (mqucifer/print-shop#26). On `cup-1` the renderer gave each file a colour from a list of 5, starting again at the 6th, so a pane was drawn in the frame's colour and didn't show as glass, nothing on a sheet said which colour was which file, and the model described the design it meant, not the picture.
- **`--look FILE=COLOUR[:LABEL]`** draws a file in its colour, a name (`blue`, `amber`, `clear`, perhaps `light` or `dark`) or `#rrggbb`, from `bench/colours.py`, which the harness reads too. A file with none takes the colour furthest from every other on the sheet. Each file is marked where it is on every picture, after the picture's check, by its name since mqucifer/print-shop#54 (below), and the legend under the sheet names each file, its label and its colour. `drawn:` lines say the same in text, and `numbers.files` in the record.
- **What each view shows** is counted: each view is drawn again unlit, one exact colour a file, and `numbers.seen_px` has each file's pixels. A file no view shows is said, "not seen in any view drawn", as on `cup-1`, where a pane's colour hid it.
- **`--cut`** is a view: every file cut by one plane, each in its colour, the largest first so what sits in an opening, such as an island of frame in a pane's hole, is drawn over it. `z=3` is level; `x=50` is upright, seen from +x; `y=40` from the front; and `x=50,y=20:40` draws only that stretch, so a frame 6 mm tall is more than a line and a pane can be judged in its pocket. Each file's area is checked against OCCT's section of its exact surface, and the painted area against the polygons. Two files whose sections overlap are said, by how much, which no other picture shows. A cut is a column beside the views and close-ups, in plain and variant sheets.

**A cut through any plane** (mqucifer/print-shop#352). A joint on a sloping seam, a rib down a dome's seam or a dovetail on a leg turned to a corner, shows in a cut square to the seam, through its pin or latch, and an axis cut slices it slantwise: one along x on a sloping seam came out 62° off square. Runs drew such sections by hand in scratch scripts, with labels on the wrong features.
- **`--cut at=X,Y,Z,normal=NX,NY,NZ`** is the plane through that point square to that direction, seen from the normal's side, drawn square to it and to scale, each file in its colour. Its own axes are `u`, the picture's right, and `v`, its up, in mm from the point, and both are drawn on the picture's sides with the point marked. `v` is `up=UX,UY,UZ` laid on the plane where given, else +z laid on it, or +y on a plane within 30° of level; `u` is `v` across the normal. So `normal=1,0,0`, `0,0,1` and `0,-1,0` draw as `x=`, `z=` and `y=` do: `y=` is seen from -y, as the front view. `normal=0,1,0` is seen from +y, with `u` along -x, the mirror of `y=`. A plane's figures may carry an exponent, `1e-3`. `u=FROM:TO` and `v=FROM:TO` draw only that stretch, as `x=50,y=20:40` does. The reply says the plane: its point, its unit normal, `u` and `v`, and how `v` was chosen; `numbers.cuts` has them as `plane`, and each file's box on the plane in `u` and `v` as `box_mm`. A plane on a face is `NO ANSWER` with a plane 0.05 mm along its normal to copy, as the axis form's is.
- **`--gaps`** marks on every cut each pair of files' least gap on the drawn stretch: a line where it lies, from the middle of the stretch of outline where it's least, and its figure. The figure is OCCT's, between its sections of the two files' exact surfaces on that stretch, where both are STEP, as `clearance` reads a gap, and otherwise the section's outlines'. Each is the other's second check, within the fine mesh's chords on both outlines (`2 x 0.002 + 0.0001` mm), and past that it's a FAIL of the picture. The reply says each pair's figure, where it lies on the plane and in the parts' frame, or that they touch; `numbers.cuts[...].gaps` has each pair's `gap_mm`, `gap_by`, `gap_outline_mm`, `gap_occt_mm`, `at_uv` and `at`. A gap on the cut is read in the plane: never less than `clearance`'s least gap in 3D, equal where the plane passes through where the parts are nearest square to both, as a cut square to a seam through its pin does, and more where it meets them slantwise, so a joint can look looser on a cut than it is. The reply says so beside the gaps, `--help` says so, and the picture says "gaps in-plane: never less than 3D".
- **On a slanting cut** a surface's chords move its outline by the chord over the sine of the angle between the surface's normal and the plane's, so the outlines' second check allows each file that, by the normal of its fine mesh's triangle nearest the gap's end (`gap_band_mm`), the sine taken as no less than 0.05. On a cylinder of radius 50 beside a flat 0.25 mm off it, cut 86° off square, OCCT reads 3.5839 mm and the outlines 3.5907, and the fixed band of 0.0041 mm failed a right picture.
- **A window's edge on a face** clips material beyond it to a line, which Shapely gives as a collection with no boundary, and the cut crashed, in the axis form as well: only the clipped area is kept now.
- It works with `--placed`, `--part`, each copy of a file an assembly names in several parts (mqucifer/print-shop#343), `--look` and `--variant`. The axis form, its pictures and its numbers are as they were, but for the crash on a window's edge.
- Tested on parts made in the test (`tests/test_cut_plane.py`): two plates 0.2 mm apart, each with two holes, and a pin in each hole with 0.25 mm of play, all turned 35° about x and 20° about z, cut square to their seam through both pins. Each outline lies where it should on the plane, each gap is `clearance`'s to 0.0001 mm, and each gap's ends, taken back to the plates' frame, lie on the hole's wall and the pin's.

**An island of frame inside an opening** (mqucifer/print-shop#27). On `cup-1` one pane had holes cut in it for others, so the frame's walls round those holes stood inside its opening, joined to the rest only through the lips above and below. Every command read that opening as one solid pane, and nothing said so: `capture` read the island's volume as a pane "too thick for its pocket", `pause` read it as a ceiling mid-pocket, and `render --section` drew the opening over it. Now an opening is the hole less any island inside it, so its pane has a hole round each, and:
- `capture`, `pause`, `panes` and `render --section` read it, and say "opening N has 1 island(s) of frame inside it". `capture` reads the lip round each hole of a pane as it does the outer edge, ring by ring.
- An island closer to the opening's edge than twice the play can't have pane between: the pane goes round it open, and `capture` says so with where. One a pane can't go round at all is named, with the opening's centre and size, and not read.
- `pause` reads what each layer leaves free of the whole opening, not the hole round one point of it, which could stand on an island.
- A section is drawn piece by piece, so an island shows. Its line counts the pieces and islands, and one that fails its own area check names each piece it didn't draw.
- `panes` writes a pane with holes as a STEP too, checked by its volume.

**`capture` says where each pane is.** Its numbers are its own, largest opening first, and the model read its "pane 1" as `pane_1.step` for 33 calls. Each pane is now said with its centre, its size, its holes and, where a file beside the frame has its outline, that file: the outlines' sections at the pocket's height share 90%, and OCCT finds a point inside the bench's pane inside the file's solid. Two files lying in one opening with no wall between are named. A pane that meets the frame where it sits says whether that's the ceiling (too thick), the floor (set too low), or frame standing in its place, with where.

**A curve stored as flat strips** (mqucifer/print-shop#54). 121 of the 226 STEP files in approved folders stored every curve as flat faces, one for each segment of a Shapely polygon extruded with `polyline`: 360 MB, against 7.9 MB for the other 105. `info` now reads `file_kb`, `flat_faces` and `small_flat_faces`, the flat faces under 1 mm across their plane, by OCCT's oriented box round each and again by the least rectangle round points along its edges. It's a reading, never a failure: a part with no curves can have small flat faces. With any, it says that a curve may be stored as strips, and names the skill that builds it as a curve. On an 80 mm ring 3 mm thick, built from polygons of 96 segments a quarter, it reads 768 of 770 flat faces and 2,989 kB; as two circles, 0 of 2 and 9.3 kB. **Strips come in runs** (mqucifer/print-shop#85): on `eval-pocket-2` `info` and the standing check said "36 of 63 flat faces under 1 mm across" of a lid built from boxes, one circle and fillets, the narrow sides of its clip beams and feet, and the model had to argue past a blocking check. A polygon's strips are neighbours along a chain, each turning a few degrees to the next, together following a curve; a beam's narrow side meets the next face square. So `info` joins flat faces that share an edge and turn between 0.01° and 30° to each other (a polygon of more than 12 sides), keeps each run of 3 faces or more with a narrow face in it, and counts the narrow faces in runs: `strip_faces`, and each run's length, `strip_runs`. The reply says "2 runs of strips (the longest 384 faces)", or "narrow flat faces only, no strips". Neighbours needn't be as wide as each other: a polygon whose points come in close pairs has strips that go wide, narrow, wide round its curve, and only the narrow ones are under 1 mm, so a rule asking for neighbours of about the same width read such a pane as no strips. A run with no narrow face isn't counted, so coarse faceting is read no more than it was. **Second check:** the same runs from points along each face's edges alone: two faces are neighbours where they share two points, the turn is between planes fitted to the points, and the width is the least rectangle's, and a difference is a note. On copies (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-bench8/curves.json`), `suncatcher-3`'s frame and both its panes still fail, and the `eval-pocket-2` lid and all 50 STEP files of batch 3's approved folders pass. A circle of 48 points through `polyline` is one run of 48; a lid with four 0.6 mm beams in slots, feet and fillets (`strips/beams`), 24 narrow faces and no strips. The harness's standing check counts `strip_faces` (`standing.strips_counted`), and fails at 20, as it failed at 20 small faces. An STL has no faces of its own, and says so. **Under 1 mm misses coarse faceting:** at Shapely's default of 16 segments a quarter, that ring's strips are 3 mm tall and 3.4 to 3.9 mm round it, and none reads small, though the ring is 0.048 mm off its circle. The skills say what that costs.

**`capture` with several plays** (mqucifer/print-shop#54). `--clearance` is one play for every pane. On `eval-choice-1` two cells had different plays, and nothing in the reply said so. A pane's own file is the one matched, or, where `--clearance` is so far from its play that none matches, the one file lying in its pocket: nine tenths of it inside, covering half the pocket or more. Its play is read from that file: the room between the pocket and the file's outline at `--pocket-z`, over the length round both, and as the second reading, the median distance from points round the file's edge to the pocket's wall. A pane whose play differs from `--clearance` by more than 0.02 mm, the coarse mesh's chords on both outlines, is named in `plays_differ`, with both readings, and the reply says one run can't judge panes of different plays, and the `--clearance` to run again at. Where every pane's file has the same other play, it says to run again at that. It judges nothing: the verdict is as it was at `--clearance`. On `eval-choice-1`'s approved frame and panes, at the need's 0.25 mm nothing is named; at 0.85, the play the run once gave its small pane, the bench's panes match neither file, and both are named with 0.25 mm by both readings (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-tools/evals/choice1/`).

**A sheet marks each file by its name** (mqucifer/print-shop#54). The approval sheet numbered files in order, so `pane_1.step` was marked "2" beside the model's own layout, where pane 1 was another. Each file is now marked on every picture by its name less `.step`, `pane_1`, or by its path where two files share a name, and the legend names each file, its label and its colour. Its words wrap within its entry, and nothing is cut: a legend once ended "none was giv". `numbers.files` keeps each file's `number` in order, and its `mark`.

**A height on a level face** is still refused, and the refusal now says the middle of the stretch either side, with how many openings each has.

**A gap range** (mqucifer/print-shop#60). `clearance --min-gap` alone let a lid too loose to stay on pass. `--max-gap G` fails a pair whose least gap is over G, `TOO LOOSE`, exit 1, so parts meant to mate are held from both ends, and a need on a lid's play carries both (`reports.BOTH_ENDS`). A pair that touches has a least gap of 0, never too loose, and a lid seated on its base touches it whatever its lip's play: with `--max-gap`, the reply says so, and to run again with `--lift`. A range the wrong way round is a usage error.

**A mated part's play, read on the printed file** (mqucifer/print-shop#65). The docs said to read a lid's play on a copy lifted off its seat, and `done` refuses a need on a file that isn't printed (mqucifer/print-shop#64): on `eval-lid-1` the model made the copy, `done` refused it, and the lid was saved 2 mm up instead, and on `eval-fit-2` the frame was built round the pane where its file sat. `--lift D` moves the last file named D mm along `--along` (z unless said, D signed) before measuring, and only there: its file stays where it is, and the reply's first line says what moved, by how much, and that every figure is with it moved (`numbers.lift`). The need is then on the printed lid. D must be more than `--min-gap` and `--max-gap`, or it's a usage error, as a lift short of the play would read as the gap. Where a pair's least gap still reads D, it's the faces that seat, so the play is at least D and isn't read: the reply says so. On the tray's lids seated, `--lift 0.5 --min-gap 0.1 --max-gap 0.3` is `CLEAR` at 0.2 mm of play, and `TOO LOOSE` at 0.6, where it reads the lift and says so, as the lifted copies did.

**The gap a verdict reads** (mqucifer/print-shop#65). On `eval-fit-2`, `clearance references/pane_1.step frame.step --min-gap 0.25` read `TOO CLOSE` where OCCT's exact distance was 0.2500 round the pane's curved corners: the verdict read the mesh, 0.24998, as its chords cut into the pocket's concave corner, and the allowance of 2 × 0.002 mm was on `--max-gap` only. The same allowance under `--min-gap` would pass a gap truly short by the chords, so instead the verdict on a STEP pair reads OCCT's exact distance, `gap_mm`, at both ends, to 1e-6 mm, the mesh's being its second check within the chords on both faces, and `DISAGREE` past them. On an STL pair it reads the mesh, which is the file's own surface. A need names `pairs.0.gap_mm`. Planted: a pane 20 × 14 rounded 3 mm in a pocket 0.25 mm wider all round, its corners 3.25: OCCT reads 0.24999999999999853, the mesh 0.2499806; `--min-gap 0.25 --max-gap 0.25` is `CLEAR`, where the old rule was `TOO CLOSE`, and 0.2501 is `TOO CLOSE` (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-bench/min-gap/`).

**A gap by place** (mqucifer/print-shop#72). A pair's least gap is its tightest face, so a mated pair with two plays reads only the smaller. On `eval-fit-3` a pane sat in its pocket with 0.25 mm of play round its edge, the request's, and 0.15 mm on its faces, the model's to choose so the pause works: `clearance` read 0.15, and the edge's need couldn't be read on the printed part. A section through the pocket was refused at `done`, as a need checks the printed part (mqucifer/print-shop#64), `rays --d` can't hold a rounded rectangle, and a size isn't the play. Five rounds went on it. On `eval-fan-1` the clip arms' side play couldn't be read apart from the seated plate's contact, and lifted, the barb overlapped. Now `clearance` takes places, as `edges` does:
- **`--box NAME=X0,Y0,Z0,X1,Y1,Z1`** reads, for each pair, the least gap between the parts' surfaces inside the box, on the files where they lie. Put it round the faces whose play it is and clear of the faces with another: for a pane's edge, its edge and the pocket's wall, between its two faces. It reads the parts' own material in the box: as a box is convex, the least distance between that is the least between their surfaces in it, never a section or a copy. On a STEP pair the verdict reads OCCT's exact distance between the parts cut by the box, and the second method Manifold's, between the meshes cut by it, within the chords on both; on an STL pair the mesh is the verdict, and points sampled on it the second method.
- **`--facing NAME=DIR`** reads, on a STEP pair, the least gap between faces whose outward normal stays within 60° of DIR all over, one of `+x -x +y -y +z -z`, and the other part's faces facing back, both ways round: DIR and its opposite read alike, as a gap has a face on each side. A rounded corner turns through 90° and faces neither way. Its second method samples points on those faces. An STL has no faces of its own, so it's refused there, saying to use `--box`.
- **Each place gives** `numbers.pairs.N.places.NAME`: `gap_mm` and `gap_by`, `gap_second_mm`, the points `from` and `to` it's between, and its `verdict`, so a need names `pairs.0.places.edge.gap_mm`. `numbers.places.NAME` says what each place is. `needs` refuses a place its command doesn't name.
- **With a place named, `--min-gap` and `--max-gap` hold each place's gap,** not the pair's least: a range is the play at one place, and a mated pair has several. The pair's least gap is read and said as before, and its overlap and its methods' agreement still count. The answer's verdict is the worst of the pairs' and the places'.
- **A box that catches no surface of one of the pair** says which lies outside it and which fills it with material, `NOT CAUGHT`, with no figure; one that catches neither part of any pair is `NO ANSWER`. A box round a contact reads 0, `TOUCHING`, as the pair does. On the overall least gap and `--lift`, nothing changes.
- **Planted** (`places/`): a pane 20 x 14 rounded 3 mm, 1.2 mm thick, in a pocket 0.25 mm wider round its edge and 0.15 mm over each face. A box round its edge reads 0.25, round the lip over a face 0.15, round a rounded corner 0.25, facing +z 0.15 and +x 0.25, OCCT and the mesh agreeing within 0.004 mm; with `--min-gap 0.25 --max-gap 0.25` the edge is `CLEAR` and the face `TOO CLOSE`, and at 0.2501 the edge is `TOO CLOSE`. As STLs, the edge and the corner read 0.25 by the mesh. A plate seated on a base between two arms 0.3 mm off its sides reads 0.3 in a box round an arm, the pair `TOUCHING`. With the fake model, a need on the edge's place passes at `done` at 0.25 and fails at 0.2501.

**A figure at its limit** (mqucifer/print-shop#72). On `eval-fit-3` `pause` said "0.400 mm above the pane at the pause, over 0.4 mm", and failed: the room was 0.4 by the file's figures and 0.40000000000000036 by the floats that add up the layers. `clearance` held its limits to a millionth of a millimetre, `pause` to nothing, and a need compared a figure the reply had rounded to four places. Now one rule holds every limit on the bench and every need's `at_least` and `at_most` (`bench/limits.py`):
- **A figure equal to its limit, to arithmetic's noise, passes.** Arithmetic's noise is `ARITHMETIC`, 1e-6 in the limit's unit, said once. It's a constant, not a band: no limit is loosened past it. Each comparison is `limits.under` or `limits.over`: `clearance`'s and each place's gap range, `pause --max-gap` and `--min-room`, `thin`'s nozzle and layer, `--min-body`, `light --min-open`, `thickness --max` and `--min`, `wall --min-wall`, `edges --min-radius`, `capture`'s lip and rim and share, `panes --min-area`, `layout --min-width`, `readback --min-share` and `--expect-delta`'s `--tol`, and `rays --d`'s and `--depth`'s `--tol`.
- **The figure a limit judges is the figure saved,** unrounded: `gap_mm`, `room_over_pane_mm`, `open_pct`, `least_wall_mm`, the least radii, `thickest_mm` and `thinnest_mm`, `width_mm`, `across_mm`'s ends, `floor_mm.min` and `change_mm3`. A need on it reads what the bench judged. The lines round them for print.
- **A figure said beside its limit** has as many places as it takes to tell it from the limit, so a reply never says a figure over its limit with the limit's own digits: "0.4001 mm above the pane at the pause, over 0.4 mm. Raise the pocket's floor by at least 0.0001 mm".
- **A reading's own error stays as it was,** each stated with its source, never widened, and held through the same rule (the operator's answer on mqucifer/print-shop#72): `wall --min-wall` 2 × the fine mesh's chord, 0.004 mm, as the sections are on the mesh; `edges --min-radius` 0.5% of R or 0.005 mm, OCCT's curvature on a spline; `thickness --max` and `--min` 0.004 mm, the chords; `capture`'s rim 0.01 mm and share 0.5 points, the chords on both outlines. A figure that passes only within one says so: "the 2.004 mm asked for, within the mesh's 0.004 mm". Reading these three against an exact figure, as `clearance` does, is for a later batch.
- **A face at 45° is at the overhang's limit, however the part is turned** (mqucifer/print-shop#239). The downward reading at a stop, which is the print loop's own (`setup/features.py`, `overhang_to_support`), compares a face's angle and the slope under each point with 45°, and a float32 mesh's points put a face drawn at exactly 45° up to some thousandths of a degree either side: one bar read 0, 340, 698 or 1057 mm² to support as it was turned. Both comparisons allow 0.0054°, that mesh's own rounding: two points of it are off by up to 5.3e-5 mm within 512 mm of the origin, over the 0.4 mm of height a slope is read over. 45.01° still needs support.
- **`thin` read a wall exactly the nozzle's width as thin:** its rays started 1e-5 mm inside the surface and didn't add that back, and the sections were opened by the nozzle's full width. A wall 0.4 mm wide at a 0.4 mm nozzle was `DISAGREE`; now it's `OK`, and 0.4001 `FAIL`.
- **Planted:** `frame.step` with a 1.6 mm pane has the room fit-3 had, 0.40000000000000036: `--max-gap 0.4 --min-room 0.4` is `OK`, where it was `FAIL`; 1.5999 is 0.4001 over and 1.6001 0.3999 under. Each other limit is run at its figure and just past it (`test_*_at_their_limit*`, `test_*_at_its_limit*`), and the before and after are in `~/print-shop/proofs/m18-gaps/`.

**Replies that say where** (mqucifer/print-shop#77). The learning loop's first class, from the Sponsor's session of 2026-11-09: tool replies that don't say enough. In 40 long calls read one at a time, a reply that left the model unsure was the main or second cause in 14, and on `pi5-case-1` the model wrote 24 measuring scripts of its own for what the bench had no command for. Each reply now says where, and says what its figures mean:
- **`clearance` leads with a file's bodies.** A file of more than one solid (or closed body, on an STL) is said first, in the verdict ("lid.step: 5 bodies; 15 pair(s): ...") and on a line naming each body's volume and box, with what to do if it's meant to be one piece. `numbers.bodies` has each. The second reading is the file's mesh, split where nothing joins it; a mesh that joins two solids where they touch is noted. On `pi5-case-1` the lid's clip legs were floating, 5 bodies, and five replies listed 15 pairs without saying so: the model found it on its own an hour later.
- **Each overlap says where it is.** Each separate piece of it, largest first, by the mesh's own intersection: its volume and the box it fills, and which named `--box` it lies inside, partly in, or "outside every named box" (`pairs.N.overlap_at`). OCCT's `common` gives its box too, in the second check (`overlap_occt_box`). The run's replies gave the volume and nothing else.
- **Two methods that both find an overlap say so.** Where OCCT's `common` and `cut` and the mesh all find an overlap and only the volumes differ, the verdict is `INTERFERE`, with "both methods find an overlap; volumes ..." and each method's figure (`overlap_by`, `volumes_differ`). `DISAGREE` stays for one method finding an overlap and another none, said in those words. A method's figure within its own tolerance of the mesh's is no disagreement, as before. On `pi5-case-1` OCCT read 4.000 mm³ and the mesh 3.274, and about 1.5 h went on a `DISAGREE` that was a volume. On the hinge turned 3e-5°, OCCT reads 1937.2 and the mesh 1933.5: `INTERFERE`; turned -3e-4° at y 0.6, OCCT reads none: `DISAGREE`. The check is as strong as it was: an overlap any method finds and every method agrees on is still a fail.
- **`clearance` doesn't answer on what it can't measure** (mqucifer/print-shop#244). A surface facing inward gave a negative overlap, which fell through "over the band" to a gap of 0: a part that reached far into a solid read `TOUCHING`.
  - **An overlap under nothing by more than its band is `DISAGREE`,** naming whose surface can't be trusted and the figure, for a pair, at a `--box` and along a `--slide`. The band is taken from the overlap's size, not its sign. One inside the band is still two faces that only meet.
  - **Each part is checked before it's compared, by `info`'s rule** (`shapes.cant_read`): a surface that isn't closed, in `info`'s own words, or a negative volume, by the mesh or by OCCT. A STEP is checked solid by solid, so two solids that touch in one file are still read; an STL as the file is, as `info` reads it, and body by body. So an STL with an open seam that's split and filled to be read still gives no quiet verdict.
  - **Such a part gives no `CLEAR`, `TOUCHING` or `HELD`:** its pair, its place and its slide are `NO ANSWER`, with the reason first and each method's figure beside it. `TOO CLOSE`, `TOO LOOSE` and `NOT HELD` aren't answers on it either. An overlap every method finds is still `INTERFERE`, saying the part can't be read: `NO ANSWER` ranks under it. A part is never repaired or turned right way out to get an answer.
  - **An STL's enclosed hollow** splits off as a body that faces inward, and is `DISAGREE`, as it was.
- **Many pairs shrink to what matters.** Past 3 pairs, every pair whose verdict and places' verdicts are `CLEAR`, `TOUCHING` or `NOT CAUGHT` is one counted line, with the least gap among them, and one second check; every other pair, and every place that isn't one of those, is said whole, as `pairs.N` so a need can name it. A box's own line is left out past 3 pairs, as the command gave it. The record keeps every pair. With `--box` on large references the run's replies ran to about 21k characters, 8 times, against a median of 445. On a lid of 5 bodies over a fan, 15 pairs with one overlap and two boxes, the reply is 1,896 characters, the overlap's pair whole.
- **`light`'s `DISAGREE` names its two figures,** the method that gave each, and where: the pane's footprint, or the narrowest band's two ends, and what to render to look. On `suncatcher-3` "the narrowest band is 1.500 mm ..., rays across it find 0.987 mm" was read as the leading being too thick. Planted: a plate 0.1 mm thick tilted 5° over a pane, which the sections cut into strips a layer apart and rays meet everywhere.
- **`thin` says where each thin place is.** Its spots, a layer at a time, are joined into places where one layer's meets the next's, and up to the first 10, thinnest first, are said with their box, how many layers they run through and their least width across by rays (`thin_places`). On `suncatcher-2` the reply gave 40 spots and the thinnest, the positions were in the record, and the model took a note about a wedge for where they were. The verdict counts the places and the spots.
- **A one-layer point at a hole's rim is noted, and isn't a thin wall** (mqucifer/print-shop#238, `bench/rim.py`). A bore at 45° to the layers that ends on a flat face square to it has one layer at each rim whose section grazes it and leaves a point: 0.053 mm across, in a plate 20 mm thick. It's noted as a cusp and a sharp point are, with how many there are and where, and kept in the numbers (`rim_points`, and `rim_points_at` with each one's box, width, rim and least wall square to it). `thin_spots`, `thin_places` and `thinnest_across_mm` are then the thin ones. A thin spot is such a point only where all three are true:
  - **it runs through one layer:** its place is one layer tall, and the layers weren't sampled (`--max-layers`);
  - **it lies at a hole's rim, where the hole meets a face:** every point of it is within the nozzle's width of a closed edge inside a flat face, past which the surface turns into the material square to the face or wider, and it lies on both;
  - **the wall there, read square to the surfaces and not in the layer's plane, is at least the nozzle:** the surfaces the point lies on; the hole's own surface, on from the rim as far as it doesn't turn a corner out of the hole, a nozzle apart all over; and what lies straight down the opening. So the hole's side and its end both count. Where the hole breaks out at a slant its edge there is no wall: a reading within a nozzle of an edge sharper than square that comes out through the face past that edge is the edge's, so the square rim at the other end keeps its note and the breakout's own point still fails. A reading that comes out anywhere else is a wall, however near an end.
  - **Still a `FAIL`:** a bore 0.3 mm from the plate's side, whose points stay with it; a 0.3 mm skin over a bore's end, though the skin is 0.424 mm in the layer's plane and the point at the other end is all the sections see of it; a rim sharper than square; a rib one layer tall at a rim; and every thin place through two layers or more, at a rim or anywhere else. Where a point at a rim stays thin for its hole's wall, the reply says that wall and where it is.
  - **Not changed:** the nozzle's width, the spot's least area, the pieces that vanish at it, and the rays' wall reading. That reading still counts rays that cross a square rim's corner level, so a plate with many such bores can be `DISAGREE` by the rays with every point noted: eight M5 bores are, at 6 rays.
  - **A known limit:** that `DISAGREE` over many such bores is a wrong reading, and it errs safe: it refuses a good part and passes no bad one. It turns on how many rays are sampled, not on the part. Left as it is, and parked (the side-table orchestrator, 2026-11-22).
- **`capture` says what it reads each pane against:** where its underside came from (`--pane-floor-z`, the pocket's floor, the level face facing up in its opening nearest under `--pocket-z`, or the part's bottom where there is none), its top, and the level faces over and under its opening that are its rim, with their heights and areas (`panes.N.read_against`). A pane that misses `--pocket-z` says where its underside came from, and to give `--pane-floor-z`. On `eval-unnamed-2` "runs from z=0.000 to z=1.000, which misses --pocket-z" didn't say where 0 came from, and the model guessed the bench moved the pane.
- **`faces FILE`** reads a STEP part's flat faces, a plane at a time, largest first: the way it faces (`+z`, or its normal), where (`z=7.5`), its area, its box and size, and how many faces are in it; and each full cylinder, its split faces joined: a hole (its surface facing its axis) or a boss, its centre half way along its axis, the axis, radius and depth, from where to where, and for a hole whether each end is open, by rays along its axis. A part of a cylinder, such as a rounding, is counted and left to `edges`. Second readings: each plane's area by the fine mesh's triangles in it, within the chords round its curved edges; each cylinder by the mesh cut square to its axis half way along, a circle fitted to the cut's points round it (centre and radius within 0.005 mm), and its depth from the mesh's points on it. An STL has no faces, and is told to use `slice`. It replaces the run's scripts on a bought part's faces, a fan's holes and a plug's ends and feet. On a fan block 30 x 30 x 7.5 with four 3.4 mm holes it reads six planes and the four holes at their centres, open at both ends.
- **`slice FILE --at z=H`** (and `x=`, `y=`, more than once) cuts the part by a plane: each separate piece, largest first, with its area, its box in the part's axes, and its outline, said as a rectangle (and how it's turned) or a circle where it is one, its points where it has 12 or fewer once simplified, and else their count; and each hole, its centre, size, area and, where round, its diameter. The second reading is OCCT's section of the exact surface, its area, pieces and holes, or on an STL its triangles cut one by one. A plane that misses the part says where the part runs. It replaces the run's 9 cross-section scripts. Through the fan block at mid-height it reads one 30 mm square with four 3.4 mm holes, and upright through two holes, three pieces.
- **`slice` reads each piece's least width,** and a need can hold what a section reads (mqucifer/print-shop#101). `cuts.N.pieces.M.least_width_mm` is the thickness of the piece's thinnest part across: a straight wall's thickness, or a tab's. It's read from the polygon's edges, for each two that face each other within 30° and share no corner, as the least distance along one's inward normal to the other where they overlap, through the piece, so a sharp tip, where two sides meet, isn't a width; and a piece with no two such edges, a triangle, has none. The second reading, `least_width_rays_mm`, is rays from points 0.1 mm apart along each edge, its ends among them, along its inward normal to the first edge they meet, counted between the same pairs; they agree within 0.037 mm (the chords, and the rays' half-step where two facing edges close at 30°), or it's `DISAGREE`. `cuts.N.pieces_count` and `cuts.N.holes` count a level's pieces and holes, and `cuts.N.pieces.M.size_mm` is a piece's size along the plane's two axes. **A place named** by `--box NAME=X0,Y0,Z0,X1,Y1,Z1` reads, at each level through it, the section inside the box, `cuts.N.places.NAME`: its `pieces`, their least width and size, and `whole_pieces`, how many of the level's pieces lie wholly inside it, so a need reads one wall or one tab after a rebuild changes the pieces' order. A tab cut free is one whole piece in a box round it; one fused to its wall is none. `needs` takes these as a `section` measure (`docs/rounds.md`). Evidence: `eval-edges-5` sent a 2 mm wall need on `slice` that was refused, so the wall was held only by the script's `WALL = 2`; and a design run's tabs were fused to their wall for their whole height while the model wrote that they stood free (lessons runs9-edges:5 and design-runs:34). Planted (`slice/`): an open box 50 x 35 x 20 with 2 mm walls reads one ring at z 10, `least_width_mm` 2.000 by both methods; a wall with four tabs 1.2 mm thick cut 0.5 mm free of it reads 5 pieces at z 10, between their root and hook, the ring at 2.000 and each tab at 1.200, a box round one tab 1 piece wholly inside it; the same tabs fused read 1 piece, and the box none wholly inside.
- Both are readings, with no limit and no `FAIL`: what's there, or `DISAGREE` where the two readings differ. A need can name their numbers (`holes.0.radius_mm`, `cuts.0.pieces`), but a check of the part goes through the command that judges it.

**`edges`** (mqucifer/print-shop#60) reads each outside edge of a STEP part: the Sponsor wants a case's edges, corners and lid softened, and nothing read an edge.
- **An edge** is read at points along it, every 2 mm, at least 3 and at most 25: the angle its two faces' normals turn through. Under 1° it's smooth, where a rounding meets a face. Over it, it's sharp, and outside if a point a little way into each face, and then between the two, is in the part; otherwise it's an inside corner, and isn't read. A chamfer's two edges are sharp, each turning 45°.
- **A rounded edge** is a curved face that meets a neighbour smoothly, a fillet or a corner where fillets meet, whatever it was built as. Its radius is the least it bends through convexly, read on a 9 by 9 grid over the face. A sharp edge is radius 0, so `least_radius_mm` is 0 where one is read, and `least_rounded_mm` is the least of the rounded ones, each with where.
- **Outside is what a finger reaches:** a ball 12 mm across, the test finger of IEC 60529 (IPXX-B) and IEC 61032 (test probe B), from outside every file named, and touching the edge from between its faces, not only flat-on to one. So the inside of a closed case isn't read when its lid is named, a seat flush with the lid isn't, nor the rim of a slot too narrow for a fingertip, and an edge round a plug's opening is. Each layer of every file is sliced onto a grid (0.25 mm, coarser past 8 million cells), Euclidean distance transforms find where a ball's centre fits and which of those places join the outside, and each place a ball touches an edge is held clear of every file by the mesh's exact distance. Every file named blocks the ball; one under `references/`, or named with `--with`, isn't read.
- **`--min-radius R`** fails a sharp edge or a rounding under R, naming each with where. A rounding drawn at R passes: it may read short by 0.5% of R or 0.005 mm. With no limit it's a reading, and passes.
- **A rounding stored as flat strips** reads as sharp edges, each turning a few degrees: one under 20° is counted in `faceted_edges`, and the reply says to build it with `fillet` or an arc. Rounded 3 mm as 16 strips a corner, a slab reads 68 such edges and no rounded face.
- **Second checks:** at each sharp edge read (150 at most, spread over them), the fine mesh cut by a plane square to it, and the angle its outline turns through at the edge, within 1° and each chord's lean; at each rounded face's least radius, the mesh cut along the direction it bends most, and a circle fitted to the outline's points on that face within half the radius, within 0.02 mm and 3% of it; and the ball's centre the grid found for each touch point, held clear by the mesh's distance. That the centre got there from outside is the grid's alone.
- **By group and by place** (mqucifer/print-shop#65). On `eval-edges-1` the need on the four upright corners read `rounded.19.radius_mm`, the 20th rounding in a list sorted by radius, which a rebuild can reorder, and three long calls went on picking corners out of the list. Each sharp edge and rounding read is now in one group, in the file's axes: `upright` where it runs within 30° of z (a rounding's way is square to its normal and to where it bends most); else `top` or `bottom` where its outward direction, the mean of its faces' normals, is more than 20° above or below level; and `side` for the rest. A rounding that faces up or down is a top's or a bottom's whichever way it runs, as a ball's corner bends alike both ways. `numbers.groups.NAME` has each group's counts, its least radius (0 with a sharp edge in it, none with nothing), where and on which file, and a need names `groups.upright.least_radius_mm`. `--box NAME=X0,Y0,Z0,X1,Y1,Z1` takes the edges with any point read inside the box, said at a point inside it, and `--facing NAME=DIR` those facing within 60° of one of `+x -x +y -y +z -z`, the edges round the face that faces it, each as `numbers.selected.NAME`. A rounding's radius is its own least, wherever on it that is, so a box never reads one looser than it is. Each group's and selection's least is among those the second method checks, past the 150 spread over the rest. An opening's edges in a side wall are grouped by the way they face (its top edge faces down, so `bottom`): read them with `--box`. On `mixed`, upright reads 3 mm, top 1.5, bottom 0 with its 8 sharp edges, and side none.
- **Planted:** a block rounded 2 mm all over (`OK` at 2, `FAIL` at 2.1); one with its bottom left sharp (8 sharp edges, all at its foot); an open tray (16 sharp edges a finger meets, 12 inside corners not read), the same tray with its lid flush on it (none), and with the lid as a reference (none, the lid not read). On a case 100 by 70 by 35 mm with 30 vent slots, a grille of holes, four bosses and a plug's opening, it takes 9 to 11 s: the opening's 4 edges are read, and the slots' and holes' rims are flat-on.

**`rays` from a point in air** (mqucifer/print-shop#65). On `eval-edges-1` rays from a rounded corner's centre, which lay in the box's hollow, read `across` 5.66 to 53.92 mm, and it took three long calls to see that wasn't the corner's radius. Where the centre is in air, each level now gives `nearest_wall_mm`, the nearest wall any ray meets, and a note says what `across` is: the open space's width through the point, from the wall one ray meets to the wall the opposite ray meets, a hole's diameter on its axis, and never an outside corner's radius, whose centre is always inside the part, in its material or its hollow, with `edges` to read it. From the tray's corner centre, 1 mm from two inside walls, the nearest wall reads 1.0 and the least across 2√2.

**`wall`** answers what `thin`'s `thinnest_flat_mm` was quoted for on `cup-1`: the hanging hole's wall. Any point inside the hole names it, and it reads the hole's whole length. The sections take the least distance from the hole's edge to any other edge of the material round it. The rays never touch a section, and meet them where the wall is least, as the least wall runs square to the hole's wall. Between two corners it isn't square to either, rays read it thicker, and that's `DISAGREE`, said why. Material beyond the hole's ends isn't counted. `least_wall_mm` is the number a need limits, or `--min-wall`, which holds a wall drawn at the limit within the mesh's chords.

**A hole that opens into a hollow** (mqucifer/print-shop#136). In an eval, screw holes through a 2 mm floor at the bottom of a hollow channel read the channel's 1.6 mm side wall, `FAIL` twice: the hollow is a closed hole in each section too, so `wall` followed the hole up into it. The model fell back on `slice --at z=... --box`, and on a test piece rather than the part (lessons runs14-neon-mount:3 and :11). Now:
- **`--z FROM,TO`** reads only the sections within that stretch of the hole, along its axis (z, or the `--axis` given): `--z 0,2` reads the floor round the hole. The point still names the hole, and needn't be in the stretch; a stretch the hole has no section in is `NO ANSWER`, saying where the hole runs. A note says what isn't read, `hole_read` has the stretch, and `hole_runs` says where the hole itself runs.
- **Where the hole's section more than doubles from one layer to the next** (`OPENS`, 2: a 45-degree countersink on a 3 mm hole grows 1.3 times a layer), it opens into a hollow, or a counterbore, there. When what's read reaches into it, the verdict's line says so, where the least wall is, and the stretch that reads the hole alone, at the level face between the two layers: "FAIL: the least wall round the hole is 1.600 mm, ..., under the 2 mm asked for, ...; the hole opens into a hollow at 2 along z, and this reads its whole run, the hollow too: the least wall is at 2.300, in the hollow. To read the wall round the hole alone, give --z 0,2". An `OPENS INTO A HOLLOW` line gives each opening's sections, and `hole_opens_at` and `hole_alone` have them. So the model learns the flag from the bench's reply.
- **Without `--z` the reading is as it was**, and a hole that opens into nothing is said word for word as before.
- Planted (`wall/hollow-floor`): a box 20 x 20 x 12, hollowed from z 2 up with 1.6 mm walls, a 3 mm hole through its floor 3.5 mm from the outside. With `--min-wall 2` and no range, `FAIL` at 1.600 mm, the opening named at 2 (7.07 to 282.24 mm² in one layer) and `--z 0,2` given; with `--z 0,2`, `OK` at 3.500 mm by the sections and 3.5003 by the rays; `--z 3,5`, in the hollow, reads 1.600 and says so (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-eval-fixes/`).

**A close-up** draws R mm all round a point along a view (`top` unless named), so a fine motif or a 3 mm hole can be judged: at 512 px and `R` of 4 that's 64 px per mm, where the first real run's 3 mm holes were 27 px. It looks along the view and doesn't cut anything, so what's inside a part needs `--section`. Nothing of the parts in the window is a `FAIL`, so one aimed wrongly doesn't read as a picture.

**A test piece** is drawn see-through in grey, and each picture says it isn't the part. It's whatever the one asking says it is, as nothing can tell by looking. So a script writes test pieces to files of their own, and a piece inside the part's file is drawn as the part.

**A pause height** (mqucifer/print-shop#203) means one thing on the bench, at the review and in the G-code: **the top of the last layer printed before the pause,** in mm above the part's underside as it prints. The pause comes after the layer that tops out there, so the next layer is the first to print over what was dropped in, or the first in the new colour.
- **`pause` says it as the review does:** "pause after layer 13, at z=2.6, before layer 14, at z=2.8". Type that z as `pause_at=2.6 for insert`, for parts dropped in (`pause_at=2.6` alone asks what the pause is for, mqucifer/print-shop#218). Where the file's underside isn't at z 0, the file's own height follows in brackets, and isn't the one to type. `pause_top_z` in the numbers stays the file's, as the other heights there are. `pieces build`'s `pause_z` is the same height: its frame's underside is at 0.
- **It holds for the pose and layers it was read at.** `pause` reads the file as it lies, at `--layer` and `--first-layer` (0.2 mm each unless given). Give it the process's own, and print the part as laid (`--as-laid`).
- **Orca takes another height,** the top of the first layer after the pause (`PauseAt.print_z`), and pauses before that layer. `setup.proposal.pause_z` turns the one into the other, and the refinement pass names its layer the same way. Don't give Orca a pause height.
- **The review reads where it landed from the G-code,** beside the height asked for, and says `PAUSE MISMATCH` with what the G-code has where they differ: no layer tops out at that height; supports with layers of their own (`independent_support_layer_height`) put one between it and the part's next layer; the part stands on a raft, whose height the G-code's z counts; or Orca wrote no pause, as in a print by object. Its layer numbers count every layer in the G-code, so with such supports they run ahead of `pause`'s, which counts the part's.
- **Before mqucifer/print-shop#203** a typed height was the first layer after the pause, so it paused a layer lower, and a pause to drop parts in had to be typed a layer up. Don't add a layer now. The review notes a pause that comes after the layer closing over a fit from the part's context. A fit's top alone doesn't say where its pocket closes, as the band may be the dropped part's with room planned over it, so the slice has to show it: that layer is the first from the band's foot to bridge or overhang, more than the layer after it, within 0.4 mm of the band's top. Where it can't tell, it says nothing. The print desk copies an older job's correction at the height that pauses where it did, and leaves it out, saying so, where that job's G-code doesn't say.

**`capture`** answers whether the frame's shape holds each pane. It's how the Sponsor prints them, like a lens in a pair of sunglasses without the snap: a pane is set in at a pause and printed over to lock it in, not to bond it. So nothing here reads a material or counts on adhesion, and the frame's material is free. For each face of each pane:
- **Can it slide out?** The pane is read at every place its play lets it sit, against everything beyond the pocket, by polygons and then as a solid swept out. Passing at any of them is `FAIL`, so a pane held only while it's centred isn't held.
- **Can it swing out?** A lip that lies all to one side of the pane's centre is `FAIL`, as it can pivot about that lip.
- **How much lip?** `least_lip_mm` is the narrowest round the edge, and `least_lip_with_play_mm` what's left once the pane has moved away from it. Under `--min-lip` after its play (0.4 mm, a nozzle's width, unless you say) is `FAIL`, with how much of the edge and where.
- **How thick?** A lip under a layer at its root is `FAIL`, and `air_mm` is how far the pane can rattle before it reaches it.
- **Edge with no lip** is reported and not judged, as in `panes`, if the rest goes round. Where a lip stops it falls away to nothing round a window's corner, which isn't counted as thin, and one that tapers over a long way is.
- **Why it isn't held** (mqucifer/print-shop#46). On `eval-tip-1` the pocket's cut ran on through the top face, and `capture` said only that the pane comes out, with no lip along all 59.3 mm of its edge. The model's own 2D check said the lip was there, it judged the bench wrong, and it spent about 1.5 hours and four calls of 21k–40k tokens' reasoning that ended without a tool call. Now a face that comes out, or holds the pane by less than the limit along half its edge or more, says what the frame has beyond that face, stretch by stretch, with where on the edge and the heights it read. It reads every section beyond the pane's face as one, along the line square to the pane's edge at each spot, and says one of:
  - the frame covers the pane by only so much, where the limit after play needs more;
  - nothing covers the edge, and the frame over the pane starts so far in from it;
  - the hole is as wide as the pocket, to 0.03 mm, so nothing overhangs the pane on that face and the pocket's cut may run through the face;
  - the hole is so much wider than the pocket, or narrower but stopping short of the pane, and the frame starts so far out from the pane's edge;
  - there's no frame within 10 mm, or nothing of the frame beyond the pane's face at all, with the pane's face and the frame's end.

  The pocket's wall is read on the same line, from the opening at `--pocket-z`. Frame met across a pane counts as over a spot only if it's no nearer the pane's edge anywhere else, so the far edge's lip isn't read as this one's. Each kind is said once, with how much of the edge it runs along, in how many stretches, where the longest runs, and the range of its figures. A single spot (a lip's falling end, a corner) isn't said, and every stretch is in each face's `over`. **Second check:** at a spot in each stretch, rays straight beyond the face, walked along the same line, and the pocket's wall by a level ray at `--pocket-z`. A different kind, or a figure more than 0.04 mm off, is `DISAGREE`. Rebuilt from `eval-tip-1`'s frame with its pocket cut through the top again, the reply is the run's own at call 87, then "Over it, the frame read from z=2.5 to its top at z=4: all round its edge, 59.3 mm, the hole is as wide as the pocket" (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-why/`).

**A rim sized to the visible share** (mqucifer/print-shop#37, criterion 6). On `suncatcher-2` the Sponsor ruled 1.2 mm for big panes and 0.55 mm for small ones, before play, and a need could only give one `--min-lip` for all, so nothing held the big panes at 1.2 mm. "Small" by name or by eye can't be checked: the Sponsor, 2026-11-04, it has to be a math check, not a matter of knowing. `--full-rim R --least-rim R2 --min-visible P` take the request's full rim and smallest rim, before play, from the pocket's wall to the opening's edge, and its least share of each pane visible. All three go together, with no default, and not with `--min-lip`.
- **The rim each pane needs:** the full rim if P% of the pane stays visible with it; if not, the widest rim that keeps P%, by insetting the pane's outline (true distance, round joins) by the rim less the play and bisecting; and if even the smallest rim can't keep P%, none, and the pane is to be removed. A long thin pane is judged by how much its rims cover: its width counts and its length doesn't.
- **The check:** the pane's lip, read per spot as for `--min-lip`, is at least the rim it needs all round, and its visible share on the frame as built, covered on neither face, is at least P%. The visible share is `light`'s own reading (`glass.visible`), one function for both.
- **Tolerances:** the lip may fall 0.01 mm short of the rim it needs, the mesh's chords on both edges, and the share 0.5 percentage points short. A rim drawn exactly at the need leaves exactly the share, so the share's tolerance is the same chords read as area.
- **What it says:** each pane's share, the share wanted, the rim it needs and the rim it has, and how that rim was chosen, in three figures (mqucifer/print-shop#61): the full rim, the share it leaves visible, and the smaller rim that keeps the share wanted, or that even the smallest rim doesn't. On `suncatcher-3` the reply gave the rim to set and not how, and two calls of long reasoning rebuilt the logic. `rim.chosen` is `full`, `smaller` or `remove`. A failing pane says "set this pane's rim to X mm before play", with where it's short, or "remove this pane", or, where its rim is right and too little shows, that the frame covers it elsewhere.
- **The rim to set is the rim needed, rounded down to 0.001 mm,** so a cell built at it passes in one step: rounded down, the share stays at least the one wanted, and the lip falls short of the need by under 0.001 mm, inside its tolerance. Rounded to 0.01 mm, a 2.71 mm pane needing 0.5554 mm would be told 0.56, which leaves 59.48% visible, and be told 0.56 again. On `suncatcher-2`'s ask-2 frame rebuilt with the skill's mitred offsets at each figure the reply gave, every pane passes at the next `capture` (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-verify/one-step-ask2/`).
- **Second checks:** the share by rays straight up through the frame from a grid in the pane, and the share at the rim needed by a grid of points at least that far from the pane's edge.
- **Which way the rim moves** (mqucifer/print-shop#65). On `eval-rims-4` the longest call, 31 minutes and 85,000 characters of reasoning, went on "its rim needs 1.2 mm before play, and has 1.0 mm at the least", working out which way a larger rim moves the visible share. Each pane's line now says what to change and what it leaves: "Widen its rim by 0.2 mm, from 1 to 1.2 mm before play: the opening's edge moves 0.2 mm in, over the pane, which covers more of it, and leaves 81.46% of it visible", or "Narrow its rim by 0.131 mm, from 1 to 0.869 mm before play: the opening's edge moves 0.131 mm out, off the pane, which shows more of it, and leaves 60.00% of it visible", then whether a wider rim passes too (a full rim: any rim at least it, while the share stays) or none does (a smaller rim: wider leaves too little visible, and narrower is under the rim it needs). A rim already right says "leave it". A failing pane's verdict says the same. `rims/both-at-1`, both panes at 1.0 mm as on `eval-rims-4`, reads those words (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-bench/replies/`).
- **Back-tested** on `suncatcher-2`'s ask-2 frame at 1.2 mm, 0.55 mm and 60%, against the operator's own inset of each pane file: the rim needed agrees to 0.001 mm and the share at the full rim to 0.00 points. The big panes need 1.2 mm, the legs 0.78, the triangles 0.86 and L1 and R1 0.99, and none is removed (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-verify/share-ask2/`).
- **Rims and lips:** a rim is a lip before play, from the pocket's wall. `least_lip_mm` is the lip after play, how far it covers the pane in the middle of its pocket. `--min-lip` takes the play off once more, for a pane slid to one side, as it always has, and keeps its 0.4 mm when given alone.

A pane too thick for its pocket is `FAIL`, as is a height that misses the pane. It doesn't read a pane forced past a lip that bends. Pair it with `pause`, which says when the pane goes in and whether the nozzle clears it.

**An argument's form is said with one to copy** (mqucifer/print-shop#191). A way is signed, `--slide +z`: `z` could be either way, and a slide the wrong way reads `NOT HELD`. An axis is lower case and unsigned, `--axis z`, for `rays` and `wall --axis`, `clearance --along` and `bend --layers`. A `--look` label is one word, as the command line splits one of several: `--look cup.step=blue:the cup` is refused with `--look cup.step=blue:the-cup`. Each is a usage error, exit 3, as before; only its words changed, which argparse's choices alone gave as a list.

## Never take a tool's word

The map's table, row by row. Each row that touches these commands is a planted case in `tests/test_bench.py`, named after it.

| What we saw | What the tool said | Here |
|---|---|---|
| Near-touching faces: results empty or disagreeing | Often zero overlap | `clearance`: 66 cases rerun on our own hinge, and a pair where OCCT reads zero is `DISAGREE` |
| A pocket that missed the body | Feature valid | `readback` is `NO CHANGE`, and `rays` finds the centre in material |
| A bearing seat mostly in mid-air | No error, watertight, one body | `rays` counts the rays that hit nothing, and `readback --cutter` the share of the cutter in the body |
| Mesh repair that kept 5% of the volume | "Watertight" | `readback` fails it, stated or not |
| Thin shelled surface: CAD volume swung, even negative | A plain number | `info` is `NO ANSWER` or `DISAGREE`, and names OCCT's number |
| A lofted part reached far into a solid, with a negative overlap | `TOUCHING` | `clearance` is `DISAGREE` on an overlap under nothing, and `NO ANSWER` on a part `info` can't read (mqucifer/print-shop#244) |
| A parameter that drives only part of a design | Everything healthy | `params` names which pieces moved |
| Four-bar, nothing grounded, clevis assemblies | Success, or rc -1 | Motion: not in this bench |
| Peak stress at a corner, contact with a missing constraint | A plain number, 0.0 MPa | Stress: not in this bench |

The Sponsor's way of printing panes adds three, each a planted case:

| What could go wrong | What would look right | Here |
|---|---|---|
| A pane standing above the next layer, where the nozzle hits it | The layer count alone | `pause` is `FAIL`, by both methods' layer, among several openings, and on the plate. `nozzle_over_pane_mm` is how far the tip clears it |
| A lip narrower than the pane's play | A lip in the section at one height | `capture` moves the pane by its play, and it comes out |
| A lip down one side | A lip, and no way to slide out | `capture` is `FAIL`: it can swing out |
| A pocket cut on through the top face (`eval-tip-1`) | A 2D check of the pocket and the window, which shows a lip | `capture` says the hole over the pane is the pocket's own outline, all round, by sections and by rays |
| A pane with a hole round an island of frame (`cup-1`) | "Too thick for its pocket", a ceiling mid-pocket, a section with no walls | `capture` holds it by its lips round every ring, `pause` agrees by both methods, and the section draws the island |
| A pane number taken for a file's (`cup-1`) | `pane 1` | each pane is said with where it is, and its file |
| A hole's wall quoted from the whole part's thinnest flat (`cup-1`) | `thinnest_flat_mm` 2.0 | `wall` reads that hole, and `FAIL`s under `--min-wall` |

Building this found more, each now handled and said in the code:
- **CadQuery's `tessellate` tolerance is relative,** a share of each edge's size, not the mm it looks like. On a real part that read as a 0.03 mm false gap. The bench meshes with absolute tolerances, through OCCT directly.
- **Manifold's `RayHit.distance` is a fraction of the ray,** not a length.
- **OCCT's adaptive volume moves with its accuracy setting** on a real file by far more than its own error estimate, so the bench reads its default, and checks it against the mesh.
- **A coarser mesh isn't always further from the truth,** so how far a figure can rest on the chords comes from the chord tolerance times the curved surface's area.

A second check has to ask the same question as the first, or it only makes noise. Running over our 51 reference parts showed that: rays along a surface's normal read a cone or a thread thinner than it prints, and the top of a cylinder on its side as thin when no layer prints it. So a wall's ray runs level, across the layer, and shallow slopes aren't read as walls. A sharp point, a feather edge, a cusp and a few stray rays are noted with where they are, and don't fail a part.

**What holds a bought part** (mqucifer/print-shop#76). In a design run the model put a fan's clip toes inside the fan's frame, an overlap at the face the fan was pushed against, and took the overlap for its catch: nothing was in the fan's way out, so nothing held it. It had reasoned for hours over its own picture of which way the fan goes in, and the Sponsor's view (2026-11-09) is that the bench should do that picturing, not a skill asking the model to hold it in its head. `clearance --lift` moves the last file one distance; a hold needs the whole way out. `--slide DIR`, one of `+x -x +y -y +z -z`, moves the last file named along DIR, from where it lies until it's clear of every other part (`bench/slide.py`), and says, in this order:
- **Where it lies.** An overlap of the moving part with another is said first, `OVERLAP WHERE IT LIES`, with its volume and box. An overlap is never a hold, so while one stands the answer is `NOT HELD`.
- **The hold.** The first distance at which the part meets another, `slide.first_mm`, which parts, and the box of each first contact, read 0.02 mm past it, with the named `--box` it lies in, or "outside every named box". It's `HELD` within `--hold D`, 1 mm unless given, held to `limits`' rule, so a contact at D holds; it's `NOT HELD` if the part leaves without meeting anything, or only meets something further than D, and the reply says how far it moves and to bring what holds it within D.
- **The path.** Every part the slide meets before it's clear: its box, each piece of it (each hook), and `reach_mm`, how far in it reaches from the edge of the moving part's outline, seen along the slide. Read backwards, that is everything the part has to pass on its way in, so it is what has to bend, or be fitted afterwards: the reply says so in those words. **`reach_mm` isn't how far anything bends,** and the reply says so: the bench doesn't know which way an arm bends. It's the bend only where the catch sits at the outline, as hooks under a fan's bottom face do (1.000 mm). An arm across the fan's path sliding sideways reads 3.75, half the fan's height; a lid sliding into its tray reads 15.0, about half the lid; a snap's ledge inside a lid's footprint reads its distance in from the lid's edge. Where the catch is, read it from its box. (yt-design-guides read it as the bend, on mqucifer/print-shop#82.)
- **The way in, blocked** (mqucifer/print-shop#100). A piece on the path that isn't one of the first contacts (its box doesn't meet a first contact's) and reaches further in than every one of them, by more than 0.1 mm, blocks the way in: the part can't be fitted that way unless it bends or that piece is fitted afterwards. The answer's first line says so after the hold, "HELD ... but the way in is blocked: lid.step's piece at (-15, -15, -3) to (-11.25, 15, -2) reaches 3.75 mm into the path, past the catch's 0.50; fan.step can't be fitted this way unless it bends or is fitted afterwards", a `BLOCKED WAY IN` line follows it, and the JSON has `slide.catch_mm`, the first contacts' deepest reach, and `slide.blocked`, each blocking piece with its part, box, `reach_mm` and `reach_rays_mm`. The verdict stays the slide's: the part is held, and the way in is a second fact. **A need on `HELD` isn't met while `slide.blocked` holds a piece:** `done` says which and how far in. A need may accept it by naming `slide.blocked`, such as `slide.blocked.0.reach_mm` with `at_most`, for a piece the request says is fitted afterwards, and its `what` says so, or `needs` refuses it. Each catch piece and each blocking piece is read again from the rays' points in its box and held to its section's reach as the path's is; a difference is `DISAGREE`. Each step's path pieces are now read by place, as the first contacts are, so a part's sides on its pocket's walls, a sliver with no area seen along the slide, don't join a bump on one wall to a wall across the way in on another into one piece. Evidence: a design run's fan read `HELD` on a 0.5 mm bump and its need passed, while a wall of the same part, added after the need, lay across its whole way in at `reach_mm` 3.75, and nothing read it (lesson design-runs:33). Planted (`hold/bump`, `hold/bump-walled`): a fan block in a pocket, its sides on the walls, a bump 0.2 mm under it reaching 0.5 mm in, and a wall from one side 3.75 mm in, z -3 to -2: sliding down, `HELD` after 0.2000 mm by both methods, `catch_mm` 0.4999, and the wall blocking at 3.7499 by the sections and 3.75 by the rays; without the wall, `HELD`, nothing blocking.
- **Blocked means no room to set the part in before the piece** (the operator's review of mqucifer/print-shop#100): a piece blocks only where the slide meets it before the part has gone its own length along the slide past the catch (`slide.length_mm`, within 0.0041 mm, `ROOM_TOL`); one met later is in `slide.room`, and a `room:` line says the part can be set in before it, as a fan 30 mm long in an upside-down lid whose far wall it meets after 44.9 mm can. Each path piece has `met_mm`, by the step and halving on the part's material in its box, and `met_rays_mm`, `DISAGREE` where they differ for a piece past the catch. Planted (`hold/bump-roomy`): the same wall 8 mm down, past the block's 7.5 mm from the bump at 0.2: `HELD`, nothing blocking, the wall met after 8.0000 mm by both methods and said to leave room; `hold/bump-walled`'s wall, met after 2.0000, still blocks.
- **A hold by a separate body** (mqucifer/print-shop#122). Where a first contact within `--hold` is one of the bodies of a file of more than one, joined to nothing else in it, the verdict is `HELD BY A SEPARATE BODY`, exit 1, not `HELD`: the file doesn't hold the part as one piece. The answer's first line says it after the hold ("..., within --hold 1 mm, but channel.step#2 is a separate body, one of channel.step's 2, joined to nothing else in it"), a `HELD BY A SEPARATE BODY` line names each such body, its file and its least gap to the nearest other body there, as `pairs.N.gap_mm`, the pair that reads it by its two methods, and says to join it to the body it belongs to; `slide.separate` has each. **A need on `HELD` isn't met by it**, and `needs` refuses a need asking for it, as one asking for `FAIL`: a part meant to be apart is its own file, and a file of one body reads as before. Past `--hold` it's `NOT HELD`, as before. Evidence: in an eval a ledge built in a channel, off the wall it should have joined, stayed a second body through every rebuild; `--slide` read `HELD` six times while the reply's lead said the channel was 2 bodies, the need on `HELD` passed, and about five hours went on that hold (lessons runs13-neon:8 and :9). Planted (`bodies/`): a block in a channel, its way out up, a ledge 0.2 mm over it and 2 mm off the wall: in one file with the channel, as a STEP of 2 solids and as an STL of 2 bodies, `HELD BY A SEPARATE BODY` after 0.2000 mm, the gap to the channel 2.0000 mm (both `HELD` on 0.12); the ledge run out to the wall, one body, `HELD`, its reply word for word as 0.12 gave it; the channel and the ledge as two files, `HELD` by the ledge (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-bench-bodies/`).
- **A mesh's bodies are split** (mqucifer/print-shop#122). trimesh fills a body's open seam, such as a T-junction a CAD export leaves, as it splits a mesh into bodies, and it does that with networkx, which `print-shop/bench:0.12` lacked: `clearance --slide` on a file of 2 bodies gave no answer, twice, in an eval, and `Trimesh.section(...).polygons_full` failed the same way. 0.13 has networkx 3.7, pinned and hashed with the rest. A body read with a hole filled is said in a note, as the file still has it. Planted (`bodies/channel-seam.stl`, `bodies/channel-tee.stl`): the 2-body channel as one mesh, one triangle of the ledge's top face left out, or that face meshed with a T-junction, each 3 edges with one face: on 0.12 both `NO ANSWER: ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'networkx'`. On 0.13 the first splits into its 2 bodies, its hole filled and noted, and reads `HELD BY A SEPARATE BODY`; the T-junction's hole has no area, nothing fills it, and it's `NO ANSWER: channel-tee.stl#2 isn't a closed surface`, as a one-body mesh that isn't closed always was.
- **To meet** is for the overlap to grow past what it is where the part lies, by more than `measure_pair`'s band, so a part on its seat, or sliding along a wall at no play, meets nothing. The step is 0.1 mm, a third of a 0.3 mm catch, so none is stepped over, or the parts' gap where it's larger, as no contact is nearer than that; each contact is halved to 0.0001 mm, then the gap left closes the rest, so a contact head on reads the mesh's own figure. The path is read on the coarse mesh, a piece at one step and one at the next with boxes that meet being one piece.
- **Second method:** rays, from the moving part's mesh vertices, points every 0.05 mm along its sharp edges and 6,000 points over its faces, along the slide into each other part, and from that part's points back along it, each counting only where it enters a face and is inside the part 0.01 mm past it, so a ray running along a face, and meeting an edge there, isn't a contact. A vertex on a face reads exactly, two edges crossing within 0.05 mm. They agree with the step and halving within the chords and 0.0001 mm below, and 0.05 mm more above; each reach is read again from the points the rays found on the path, which may be short of it by their spacing.
- **Where the parts touch already** (mqucifer/print-shop#84), as a fan's sides on its pocket's walls, the gap is 0 all the way and can't close a contact. Their overlap has no volume, but its faces give the band its chords, and a contact over a small area takes a while to pass that band: on `eval-pocket-2`'s lid the step and halving read 0.1533 where the rays read 0.1000, the feet's top 0.1 mm under the fan (mqucifer/print-shop#84). Where the parts touch, the contact is now read back along the overlap's growth, over 0.02 mm past where it passed the band, to where it began: head on that's exact, and where the contact widens as it goes in, it's short of where it passed the band but never before the contact. The band isn't changed. Each first contact's place is read from what the overlap covers seen along the slide, opened by 0.0001 mm, and its box from the overlap inside that place: the walls' sliver has no area seen along the slide. On CI's x86 it had a little volume, joined each foot's piece and ran its box up the wall. Planted with walls 1e-6 mm into the block, the old reading was one box from z -0.12 to 7.38, and it's now the four feet. Planted (`hold/feet`, `hold/holed-fan`): a fan block with four 3.4 mm holes, its sides on its pocket's walls, and four feet 8 mm wide reaching 0.5 mm in, 0.1 mm under it: the holes' 320 mm² at 0.002 mm make a band of 0.64 mm³, which the feet's 16 mm² take 0.04 mm to pass. The step and halving read 0.1400 before, and both now read 0.1000, `HELD` (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-bench8/`).
- **A contact that widens is followed back by its curve** (mqucifer/print-shop#143). The slope above reads a contact late where it widens as it goes in, and by more the larger the band. In an eval a catch ring 0.2 mm over a strip's top edge, all round a letter, its underside sloped and the strip's sides on the channel's walls, read 0.2263 mm by the step and halving against the rays' 0.2000, past the tie band, at a first spot no ray first met: `DISAGREE`, which cost the run two more stops (lessons runs13-neon:12 and :15). A flat face and a sloped one together grow as a square of the distance. So a growth whose slope rises (by more than a thousandth of itself each mm) is read at three distances, where it passed the band, 0.01 mm on and 0.02 mm on, no further than the slope was read, and followed back along the square through them; then again from where that lands, until it moves under 0.0001 mm or the growth left is under 0.001 mm³. It stops where a move isn't under half the one before, and where it lands with no growth at all (to 1e-6 mm³) the start is halved for between there and the move before, so it's never before the contact. An even growth is followed by its slope, as before, and reads as it did. No band or tolerance is changed: the step and halving reads nearer what the rays do, so the two agree within the chords and their places aren't in question. Planted (`catch/`): a band 1 mm thick round a 30 x 20 mm outline in a groove it fills, a catch 0.2 mm over it sloping in at 45 degrees from the outer wall, cut by six 0.3 mm gaps into three long stretches and three teeth 0.03 mm long that no ray first meets. Main read 0.2626 and `DISAGREE` at a tooth; it's now `HELD` at 0.2003 by the step and halving and 0.2000 by the rays. Read 0.05 mm late (`bench_cases/planted_tie.py`), it's `DISAGREE` still. On the eval's own pair: 0.2263 and `DISAGREE` before, 0.2000 and `HELD` now (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-batch-15/slide-143/`). The ring of mqucifer/print-shop#135 meets a slope too, and now reads 0.8004 where it read 0.8053, so its planted cases plant the eval's 0.0094 mm of lateness to reach the tie.
- **When it's `DISAGREE`:** one method meets a part and the other doesn't; the two read a different reach on the path; or they read the first contact's distance differently (past the band above) and also differ on the verdict, `HELD` against `NOT HELD`, or on where the first contact is: the rays first meet a different part, or a point outside every box of the step's first contacts, or a box has no first point of the rays'. Where they read the distance differently and agree on the verdict and the places, the verdict stands, with both figures: "both methods find the first contact at east, north, south, west; 0.2000 mm by the rays and 0.2500 by the step and halving, and both read HELD against --hold 1 mm, so the verdict stands: their distances differ, not their answer". Each check still runs and no figure is loosened: this changes what an agreement on the verdict is called. A `DISAGREE` whose methods both said the part was held gave a model nothing to act on. Planted by making one method read otherwise in the process (`bench_cases/planted_slide.py`): 0.05 mm late within `--hold`, `HELD`; with `--hold 0.22` between the two, rays that meet nothing, or rays first meeting the hooks away from every contact, `DISAGREE`.
- **A tie round the part** (mqucifer/print-shop#135). A sloped ledge meets a strip's top edge all round its outline at one height, so the first contact is a tie along the whole ring, and any patch of it is as first as another. In an eval `clearance CHANNEL STRIP --slide +z` read `DISAGREE` four times, though both methods caught the strip, the rays after 0.8000 mm and the step and halving after 0.8094: the step and halving's first contacts held a 0.03 mm patch that no ray's first point fell in (lesson runs13-neon:11). Now contacts met within `TIE`, 0.02 mm (`PROBE`, the depth each first contact's box is read at), of the earlier of the two methods' first distances are one first contact. Where the two first distances lie within it, a contact of the step's that no ray first met is still a first contact if the rays meet it there within that band, by every ray, not only the first ones (`_rays_met`, the box opened by the coarse mesh's chords), and the verdict is the hold's: "both methods find the first contact at one height round strip.step: a tie, its 2 places on channel.step each met within 0.02 mm of the first by both methods, so any is as first as another; 0.8000 mm by the rays and 0.8053 by the step and halving, and both read HELD ...", with `slide.tie` true. It's still `DISAGREE` where the rays meet that contact only past the band ("... and the rays meet it there only after 1.0062 mm, past the 0.02 mm tie band from 0.8000") or nowhere, and where the two distances are further apart than the band. Nothing else is loosened: a ray's first point outside every box of the step's is `DISAGREE` as before. **A first contact that closes round the part,** seen along the slide, is said on its own line, "the first contact with channel.step runs round strip.step: seen along z it closes round, as a ledge meeting strip.step's edge all round at one height does", and `slide.round` names each such part; a `DISAGREE` doesn't say it. A clean ring reads one box and was `HELD` already: 26 rings and strips, square and round, at 45 degrees and steep and shallow, the strip clear of the bore or flush on its walls, as STEP, as STL at 0.002 mm chords and round ones at 0.01, 0.05 and 0.1 mm, all read `HELD` on 0.13. So the eval's patch is planted on the ring (`bench_cases/planted_tie.py`, `ring/`): on 0.13 the patch reads the eval's `DISAGREE`, "where the rays find no first contact"; on 0.14, `HELD`, a tie; a patch up the slope, met by the rays after 1.0062 mm, and the step and halving 0.05 mm late, `DISAGREE` on both.
- **A hold straddling `--hold`** (mqucifer/print-shop#135). The same eval hit a hold one method read `HELD` at 1.0000 and the other `NOT HELD` at 1.0155, against `--hold 1`: a contact at the limit. It stays `DISAGREE`: `limits` holds a figure to its limit to arithmetic's noise, and neither reading is loosened to agree with the other. Where the two are within the tie band, the reply says what to do: "... The two are within 0.02 mm of each other, one first contact, and it lies on --hold within the readings' tie band: bring what holds it more than 0.02 mm inside --hold 1 mm, and both read HELD". Planted (`ring/strip-at-hold`): the strip 1.0 mm under the ring, `DISAGREE` at 1.0053 by the step and halving and 1.0000 by the rays, with those words; at `--hold 1.03`, `HELD`.
- **Two distances within the chords, with `--hold` between them, are a disagreement too** (mqucifer/print-shop#143).
