The two methods' places and answers were compared only where their distances differed by more than the chords, so a hold at the limit read to the chords, 1.0004 against 1.0000 at `--hold 1`, would have read `NOT HELD` on the step's word alone. It's judged as any two that differ are, with the words above. `ring/strip-at-hold` reads `DISAGREE` at 1.0004 and 1.0000.
- **A first contact in stretches runs round too** (mqucifer/print-shop#143). A catch round a letter is in stretches, at its corners: the eval's was twelve places that closed round nothing. Where a first contact lies along 90% or more of one outline of the moving part, seen along the slide and within the coarse mesh's chords, and covers under half of what the part covers, the reply says it runs round, with the share: "the first contact with channel.step runs round strip.stl: seen along z its 6 places lie along 98% of strip.stl's outline, ...". `slide.round` names the part and `slide.round_along` gives the share, 1 where it closes round. A face met whole lies along all its outline and isn't a ring, so it isn't said.
- **`--slide` reads a hold, not a play:** with `--min-gap`, `--max-gap` or `--lift` it's a usage error, saying to run each in a call of its own, and a `--box` with it only names where a contact lies. `--facing` is refused, `--hold` without `--slide` too, and `--slide -z` is read as written, by the parser `needs` reads a command with too. The answer's verdict is the slide's, unless a reading disagrees or two parts that don't move overlap.
- **The path names `fit-on`** (mqucifer/print-shop#142). Where the slide meets a part, the path is what the moving part passes on its way in, and how far that has to bend is `fit-on`'s reading, not this one's. The reply gives the command, with the files as this one was given them: "`fit-on snap/lid.step --onto snap/box.step --along +z --from 8`". With an STL among them it says nothing, as `fit-on` reads STEP.
- **The bench doesn't model friction**, and a `NOT HELD` says so: a lid held only by its lip's fit slides out.
- **A need names it:** `HELD` is a verdict `needs` takes for `clearance`, and `slide.first_mm`, `slide.path.N.reach_mm` and the rest are numbers it walks (`reports.SLIDE`). With the fake model, a need on `clearance lid.step fan.step --slide -z` with `HELD` and `slide.first_mm` at most 1 is met at `done` with hooks under the fan, and fails `NOT HELD` with a plain plate. `designing-enclosures` has one line: name the way each bought part goes in, then run `clearance <part> <bought part> --slide <the way out>`.
- **Planted** (`hold/`): a fan block 30 x 30 x 7.5 under a plate. Hooks 0.2 mm under its bottom face, reaching 1 mm in: sliding down, `HELD` after 0.2000 mm by both methods, at the four hooks, each 1.000 mm into the path; at `--hold 0.1`, `NOT HELD`. Toes 1 mm into the frame at its top face: `OVERLAP WHERE IT LIES`, 12.000 mm³, then `NOT HELD`. A plain plate: `NOT HELD`. The tray's lid sliding up: `NOT HELD`, and the friction said. A lid held by snaps, its hooks 0.2 mm under ledges 2 mm inside its footprint and catching 0.8 mm: sliding up, `HELD` after 0.2000 mm, each ledge reaching 3.000 mm in from the lid's edge. Each under 6 s run alone (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-hold/slide-76/`).

**`info` and `readback` on an STL: what the second check compares** (mqucifer/print-shop#144). The mesh's volume is the divergence theorem over its triangles. The second is 128 plane sections, each one's area by its polygons times the height it stands for. The two agree within 0.5% of the mesh's volume, the stack's own error over a slope or a curve (`STACK_SHARE`), or 0.001 mm³; past it, `DISAGREE`. A section at a step's middle stands for the whole step, which is wrong where the section changes all at once: at a level face inside the step. On a given channel letter, a tray 25 mm tall with a 2 mm floor, the floor's top lay 0.047 mm into a step, which read the walls' area where it was still floor: 59683.300 mm³ against the mesh's 60135.386, 0.75% apart, `DISAGREE` on a plain watertight body, and every need on `info` or `readback` for a part cut from it failed (lesson runs14-neon-mount:14). So the sections are stacked between level faces: each stretch between two heights the mesh has a level face at gets its share of the 128, one at least, and no step holds a level face. A stretch with upright sides is then exact, and the tolerance is for what's left. The reply says so: "second check: volume by sections: 11171.2693 mm³, 128 sections stacked between its level faces, within 0.5% of the mesh's (55.856 mm³)". A body with no level face inside it, such as a sphere, reads as before. Planted (`given/`): a tray like that channel, `DISAGREE` before at 11076.130 against 11171.269, now `OK` with both 11171.269; and two cubes in one mesh, one into the other, watertight, whose mesh counts their shared 350 mm³ twice: 2000.000 against the sections' 1650.000, `DISAGREE` before and now. The eval's channel reads 60135.386 by both (`~/print-shop/proofs/m18-batch-15/info-144/`). An open mesh is `NO ANSWER`, as before.

## The way on, and the bend

(mqucifer/print-shop#142; M18's "physics as projects need it".) A design run approved a snap-on lid that could never be fitted. `clearance --slide` read it held once seated, and nothing read the way on, where each clip had to bend several times what printed PETG takes across its layers. The Sponsor caught it by eye before printing, and said (2026-11-15): "We need to use tools at our disposal and not do everything with a single shot harness". FreeCAD, tried headless on that lid (mqucifer/print-shop#15), read both, and Fusion's joint and interference agreed with it step for step on the way on. So these two commands call FreeCAD 1.1.3, with gmsh 4.15.2 and CalculiX 2.23, in an image of its own under the bench's rules, and report as the others do. No need has to name them. **The harness runs both itself when a run says it's done** (mqucifer/print-shop#150, [`docs/rounds.md`](rounds.md#when-a-run-says-its-done-the-way-on-and-the-bend-mquciferprint-shop150)): each held part's way on, and the arm at each place it passes, found from the place by `bench/arm.py`.

**When to use which.**

| The question | The command |
|---|---|
| Is the part held once it's on, and by what? | `clearance HOLDER PART --slide <its way out>` |
| Can it get on, and how far does what's in its way have to move? | `fit-on PART --onto HOLDER --along <its way out> --from MM` |
| Does the arm that has to move survive it? | `bend HOLDER --arm BOX --root PLANE --push <fit-on's depth> --toward DIR` |

**`fit-on`** (`bench/fit_on.py`). MOVING is the part's file where it sits once on. `--along` is its way out, as `--slide` takes it (`z` is `+z`), and `--from` how far out it starts: far enough to be clear. `--from clear` is as far as it takes for its box to be past every other file's along the way, and a step more, as a whole number of steps (`from_clear`, mqucifer/print-shop#150): 8.25 mm for the planted lid, whose skirt's foot is 8 mm under the box's rim. It's placed at each `--step` (0.25 mm) from there to where it sits, and at each step intersected with each `--onto` file.
- **Each piece of overlap** has its volume, its box, `height_mm` along the way, and its size seen along the way: `depth_mm`, its least width across, the direction that's along (`across`), and `along_mm`, its length the other way. Across a wall the part passes, the depth is how far the wall, or the arm on it, has to move aside: the bend. **The bench doesn't know which way anything bends,** so it gives the direction, and a piece narrower along its wall than it's deep reads the narrower way.
- **Places.** Pieces that lie over each other from step to step are one place, said once, deepest first (`places.N`): the step where it's deepest, and the stretch of the way it lasts (`first_mm` to `last_mm`). Its deepest step is then looked for between the steps, halving down to 0.02 mm, so a hook with a sloped face isn't read only where a step happens to land.
- **Verdict:** `CLEAR` when nothing overlaps at any step, exit 0. `BLOCKED` otherwise, exit 1, naming the deepest place, with `depth_mm` the deepest of all. A snap fit is meant to be `BLOCKED`: the reply gives the `bend` command with that depth as its `--push`. A need on one names the verdict, `["BLOCKED"]`, and holds `depth_mm` with `at_most`.
- **An overlap where the part sits** (`seated`) is said as that: no bend clears it, and `clearance` reads it.
- **Second method:** rays along the way on, through a mesh of each part at 0.005 mm, with no boolean. The part only moves along the rays, so one set reads every step: each ray's stretches inside the moving part slide by the step, and what they share with its stretches inside the other part is that step's overlap. Rays 0.1 mm apart over the whole outline (further apart on a part too large for 400,000 of them) look at every step for an overlap two rays or more across that `common` didn't find. Rays 0.02 mm apart over each place read its depth again, as the least width of the rays inside both parts. A depth more than twice the rays' spacing and 0.01 mm apart, a place the rays don't find, or an overlap only the rays find is `DISAGREE`.
- **Planted** (`tests/cae_cases/make_fixtures.py`, `snap/`): an open box 60 x 40 x 30 with one clip inside its front wall, an arm 8 mm wide and 1.2 mm thick, free for 16 mm, its hook 2 mm tall and 0.8 mm out; and a lid whose skirt comes down 0.2 mm clear of the arm's face, with a window the hook snaps into. The band under the window passes the hook from 4 mm out down to 0.4: `BLOCKED`, 0.600 mm deep across y by `common` and 0.600 by the rays, 8.000 mm along, at 14 of 25 readings, 3.75 mm out down to 0.5. With the clip 0.8 mm further back, `CLEAR`. With the hook a wedge, read at 5, 2.5 and 0 mm out, the one step that meets it reads 0.4 mm and the deepest, 0.600, is found at 1.25 mm out. With the walls 0.5 mm too tall, the lid's plate is in the rim where it sits, 192 mm³, and the reply names no push. Each ran in under 2 s alone.

**`bend`** (`bench/bend.py`). `--arm` is a box round the arm, hook and all, one side of it on the arm's root, where it joins the rest, and clear of the wall beside it. The arm is the file's material in the box. It's held at `--root` (`z=10`), and one edge is pushed `--push` mm `--toward` a way across it.
- **An edge, not a face.** What pushes a hook meets it along a line and leaves it free to tilt. Holding a whole face square stops it tilting and doubles the strain at the root (the trial, mqucifer/print-shop#15). The edges a push can meet are those along the arm's extreme on the side the push comes from, as a hook's flat face or its crest has. `--at far` is the one furthest from the root, `near` the nearest, and `both`, the default, pushes each in turn and judges the worse: the nearer edge is the shorter arm, which the usual formula's length misses. A rounded hook has no such edge, and is `NO ANSWER`.
- **It's linear and static,** so the strain is in proportion to the push whatever the material's stiffness: only Poisson's ratio, 0.38, is the material's. The mesh is second-order tetrahedra of at most half the arm's thickness at its root, on one thread, so the same arm gives the same figure every time.
- **What's judged** is the strain across the layers, the stretch along `--layers` (z unless said), which is what pulls one printed layer off the next: its peak anywhere on the arm but within half a thickness of the pushed edge, where a push along a line has no finite strain in any mesh (`pushes.far.peak_pct`, and `across_layers_pct` for the worse push). The reply also has it on the root and within a thickness above it, the largest stretch any way, and where each is.
- **The limit is the material's across its layers,** not its bulk strength (decided with the Sponsor, 2026-11-15). `--material petg`, the only one so far, is 1%: Prusament PETG's datasheet (Prusa Polymers, version 1.1, 16-02-2022) gives "Interlayer Adhesion [MPa] 18 ± 4" and a tensile modulus of 1.5 ± 0.1 GPa, so layers part at about 18 / 1500 = 1.2% strain, 0.9% at the low end of both; in the plane of the layers the same sheet gives 5.1% at yield. Another material is added with its source, and `--limit PCT` gives the request's own. Nothing here has been compared with a print.
- **The room behind the arm.** `back_moves_mm` is how far the arm's back moves toward the push, and `room_behind_mm` how far behind it the rest of the part is, read by sweeping the back faces along the push into what the box left. An arm that meets what's behind it before the push is done can't bend as this reads it: `FAIL`, saying both figures.
- **Verdict:** `OK`, or `FAIL` over the limit or short of room.
- **What would pass** (`would_pass`, mqucifer/print-shop#150). A strip's strain is ε = 1.5·t·y/L², in proportion to its thickness and its push and to one over its length squared. So from the reading's own peak, each with the other two as they are: at this push, the most thickness at the root (`thickness_mm`, t × limit / peak) and the least length from the root to the pushed edge (`length_mm`, L × √(peak / limit)); and at this arm, the most push (`push_mm`, y × limit / peak). The reply says all three beside what the arm has, and the room it would need where it's short of that. The push is exact, as the solve is linear: the short clip reads 2.98% pushed 0.6 mm, `would_pass.push_mm` is 0.201, and pushed that it reads 0.998%. The thickness and the length are the strip's law applied to an arm that isn't quite a strip, so a changed arm is read again. With `DISAGREE` there's no one figure to scale, and none is given.
- **Second method:** a beam, by hand. The arm is cut into 40 slabs from its root to the pushed edge, each slab's area moment and outer fibre from OCCT's volume properties; the tip's force comes from the push by summing each slab's give, and a slab's strain from its moment. On a plain strip that's ε = 1.5·t·y/L², which the reply says too, with t and L read off the arm. The two are held together half way along the arm, by the strain along it, where neither has the root's corner or the push in it. Past 20% apart it's `DISAGREE`. A wide arm bends partly as a plate, up to 1/(1 − ν²), 17%, stiffer where it can't curl across its width, and a beam has no shear: on the planted clips the two are 7 to 11% apart, and the solve's peak is 0.99 to 1.08 times the beam's root figure.
- **Planted:** the clip above, pushed 0.6 mm at its hook's top edge, 16 mm from its root: 0.455% by the solve, where ε = 1.5·t·y/L² is 0.422%, 8% apart, at a 0.6 mm mesh. At its foot, 14 mm from the root, 0.590% against 0.551%, and that's the one judged: `OK` within 1%, with 1.000 mm behind it. Both pushes take under 6 s alone. Pushed 1.6 mm, 1.21%: `FAIL`. Half as long, 1.757% at its top edge: `FAIL`. In a box with 0.45 mm behind it, `FAIL` on the room, its strain the same. Pushed sideways, 8 mm thick and 16 long, the two are 24 and 34% apart: `DISAGREE`. At a 0.3 mm mesh the judged figure is 0.61%, against 0.59% at 0.6.

**`bench/arm.py`** isn't a command: it's the harness's, run in the second image, and finds the arm at a place `fit-on` read, as a box, a root plane and a push for `bend` (mqucifer/print-shop#150; how, and when it finds none, is in [`docs/rounds.md`](rounds.md#when-a-run-says-its-done-the-way-on-and-the-bend-mquciferprint-shop150)). On the planted box it gives the box the tests give `bend` by hand, `-4.1,-17.1,10` to `4.1,-14.9,26.1`, root `z=10`, toward `-y`; on the lid's band, held at both ends, none.

**What neither reads:** friction, how hard the part has to be pushed, how well a snug fit or a snap holds, a way on that isn't one straight line, an arm that yields or bends so far its shape changes the load (the solve is the first, linear answer, with no contact), and how the print's direction weakens a part other than through the one limit across its layers. Something thinner along the way than a step, passing something as thin, can be stepped over: give a smaller `--step`. Every figure above is in `~/print-shop/proofs/m18-fit-on/planted/`.

## Joined parts under a weight, and each part against the bed

(mqucifer/print-shop#156, mqucifer/print-shop#168 and mqucifer/print-shop#170; M20's side table, in mqucifer/project-print `ROADMAP.md`.) A side table is bigger than the bed, so it's printed as parts and joined, with bought bolts, nuts and washers where it takes load: printed parts are held mechanically, never by glue, by through-bolts (decided with the Sponsor, 2026-11-18). A leg is taller than the bed, so it's split too, and its pieces join with a tapered dovetail and a printed click latch; glue goes in channels in the flanks as a second hold only (the Sponsor, the same day: "We can glue, it just can't be the primary hold"). `bend` reads one arm pushed aside. Nothing read several parts joined together with a weight on them, or a set of parts against the bed. The table's load is read with FreeCAD (decided with the Sponsor at M20's planning session, 2026-11-17), as M18 reads the way on and the bend: a tool we have, not the harness. `load` runs in the second image as it is, `print-shop/cae:0.1`, with no new package, and `fit-bed` in the first.

**When to use which.**

| The question | The command |
|---|---|
| Does each part of a split fit the bed, and on which side? | `fit-bed assembly.json`, or `fit-bed a.step b.step` |
| Do the joined parts hold the weight, the way each is printed? | `load assembly.json --weight KG --at X,Y,Z` |
| How far does the top move under it? | the same reply's `moves_mm` |
| What does each joint have to carry? | the same reply's `joints.NAME` |
| Does a through-bolt hold, and what gives first: the bolt, the plastic under a washer or the nut, or the plastic round the hole? | the same reply's `joints.NAME.gives_first` and `nearest_share` |
| Does each nut's pocket fit its nut? | `fit-bed assembly.json`, `pockets.NAME` |
| Does a split leg's dovetail hold with no glue counted: which way does the load drive its tail, and what holds it? | `load`'s reply, `joints.NAME.drive_n`, `faces` and `latch` |
| Lifted by its top, does anything drop off? | `load assembly.json --lift top` |
| Can a dovetail be put together the way the file says, and does its latch bend far enough to get on? | `load`'s reply, `ways_in.NAME`, read before anything is solved (mqucifer/print-shop#184 and mqucifer/print-shop#185); `clearance SOCKET TAIL --slide <way>`, `fit-on` and `bend` read the same by hand |

### The assembly file

Both read one file, JSON, written by whoever designs the parts (`bench/assembly.py`):

```json
{"parts": [
   {"name": "top", "file": "top.step", "at": [0, 0, 500], "material": "petg", "layers": "z"},
   {"name": "leg1", "file": "leg.step", "at": [10, 10, 0], "turn": [["z", 90]], "layers": "x"}],
 "joints": [
   {"between": ["top", "leg1"], "kind": "through", "bolt": "m5-8.8", "nut": "m5-nut",
    "washer": "m5-washer", "at": [[30, 30, 500]], "pocket": 0.2}],
 "held": [{"plane": "z=0", "how": "standing"}]}
```

- **`parts`**, each one solid in a STEP file beside the assembly file:
  - `name`, its own, which the reply and a need's path use (`parts.top.stretch_pct`), and `file`;
  - `at`, where it sits, `[x, y, z]` in mm, after each of `turn`: `[axis, degrees]` about the origin's axes, in order. A part drawn where it sits gives neither;
  - `material`, `petg` unless given, the only one so far;
  - `layers`, the axis of its own file its layers are stacked along: the way up on the bed. It turns with the part. There's no default: a printed part is weaker across its layers, so the file says how each is printed.
  - `with`, other files placed by its `turn` and `at`, each a STEP or STL file beside the assembly file, such as a frame's panes (mqucifer/print-shop#201). They carry no load: `load` and `fit-bed` read the part alone, and `--placed` reads them where it sits ([a part built flat](#a-part-built-flat-read-where-it-sits)).
  - `band`, a glass frame's lead band (mqucifer/print-shop#295): a STEP file beside the assembly file, in the part's own axes, holding what of the part is the panel's leads and lips, such as the panel's outline inside its border drawn through the frame's thickness. `load` reads the part as it is; only where the whole assembly can't be read, as it meets the container's memory or no mesh of it solves, it reads it again with each part's band left out, a lower bound (below).
  - `print`, how it's printed (mqucifer/print-shop#337, `bench/printed.py`): its walls, line width, top and bottom layers, infill density, pattern and angle, and layer height, with regions of their own. `load` reads a part that has it as a skin round an infill core ([a part as printed](#a-part-as-printed)), and one without it solid, as before.
- **`joints`**, each `between` two parts that meet face to face, with its `kind`:
  - `through` (mqucifer/print-shop#168): a bolt right through both parts. A `bolt`, a `nut` and a `washer` from the tables below, all one thread, or the file is refused; and `at`, where each bolt is, a point on its axis. The bolt's head, on a washer, is on the first part named, and its nut on the second: on a washer too, or, with `pocket`, in a pocket there that holds it by its flats, with no washer under it. `pocket` is the play between each of the nut's flats and its pocket's wall, in mm. With `channel` (mqucifer/print-shop#302), the bolt lies in a drop-in channel: `{"axis": "x", "open": "+z", "head_wall": "x=12", "nut_wall": "x=28"}`, the assembly's axis it runs along, the way it's open, square to that, and the planes of the walls the head and the nut bear on, each on a washer, so it takes no `pocket`. Each is in the assembly's axes and coordinates, as `slides` is. A joint with no `channel` is read as before, and nothing undeclared is ever read as one ([below](#a-drop-in-channel)). It takes no `insert` and no `pull_n`: what it holds is read;
  - `dovetail` (mqucifer/print-shop#170): a tail on the first part named, in a socket in the second. `slides` is the way the tail goes into its socket, toward its seat, one of `+x -x +y -y +z -z` in the assembly's axes. `load` reads it against the parts themselves before anything else, and a joint the parts can't take that way fails, naming the way they do (mqucifer/print-shop#185). `latch`, where it has one, is the flexing tab that holds it there: `{"on": PART, "arm": [[x0, y0, z0], [x1, y1, z1]], "root": "z=30"}`, the part the tab is on, a box round the tab, hook and all, with one side on its root, and the root's plane, as `bend` takes an arm, in the assembly's coordinates. With `"frame": "part"` they're in the tab's part's own file's coordinates instead, and the latch is carried with the part by its `turn` and `at` (mqucifer/print-shop#295): a leg made in its own frame and placed at 45° at a table's corner has its latch named as its file has it, and four legs each turned a quarter turn have theirs named alike. The box is then turned with the part, every test of what's in it is made in its own axes, and `bend` reads the tab there, on the part as its file has it, with the way it's pushed in those axes. **It takes nothing else.** Glue channels are the parts' own shape and count as nothing: no field gives glue, a bolt or the joint a figure, and a file that gives one is refused. `slides` and `latch` on any other kind are refused too, so `bonded` can't stand in for a dovetail;
  - `bonded`: glued or welded over the faces that meet. Printed parts aren't held this way (the Sponsor, 2026-11-18: "It needs some mechanical hold"), and `load` reads a bonded joint that carries anything as `UNREAD`;
  - `bolted`: a `bolt` and an `insert` from the tables below, and `at`, where each bolt is, a point on its axis. Its own figure for what each bolt holds is `pull_n`, in N, with its `source`, the words that say where it's from;
  - `touching`: held by nothing. It can push, and can't pull.
- **`held`**, where the assembly stands or is fixed: each a `plane` (`z=0`) and `how`, `standing` on it, held square to the plane and free to slide, or `fixed` to it, as a bracket is to a wall; and `part`, to hold one part's faces only. With none, it stands on its lowest level.
- **Parts meet face to face.** Two that pass through each other, two that meet with no joint named, a joint whose parts don't meet, and a held plane with no flat face in it are each `NO ANSWER`, saying where and by how much, and nothing is solved.

**The bought parts**, each figure with where it's from (`assembly.BOLTS`, `assembly.NUTS`, `assembly.WASHERS`, `assembly.INSERTS`):

| Name | What it holds | From |
|---|---|---|
| `m3-8.8`, `m4-8.8`, `m5-8.8`, `m6-8.8`, `m8-8.8` | its proof load, the most pull with no lasting stretch: 580 MPa over the thread's stress area, 5.03, 8.78, 14.2, 20.1 and 36.6 mm², so 2917, 5092, 8236, 11658 and 21228 N | ISO 898-1: a class 8.8 bolt's proof stress up to M16, and each coarse thread's nominal stress area |
| `m3-a2-70` to `m8-a2-70` | 450 MPa over the same areas: 2264, 3951, 6390, 9045 and 16470 N | ISO 3506-1: the 0.2% proof stress of stainless A2-70 |
| `m3-heat-set` to `m8-heat-set` | no figure | none yet: see below |

Nuts and washers (mqucifer/print-shop#168), in mm. A nut's figures are its size across its flats at most, s, which is its nominal size; across its corners at least, e; its height at most, m; and the least diameter of the face it bears on, dw. A washer's are its hole, d1, its outside diameter, d2, and its thickness, h, each nominal.

| Name | Across flats | Across corners | Height | Bearing face | From |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `m3-nut` | 5.5 | 6.01 | 2.4 | 4.6 | ISO 4032:2012, hexagon regular nuts (style 1), product grades A and B, table 1 "Preferred threads": s max, e min, m max and dw min. The 1999 edition's table 1 gives the same |
| `m4-nut` | 7 | 7.66 | 3.2 | 5.9 | the same table |
| `m5-nut` | 8 | 8.79 | 4.7 | 6.9 | the same table |
| `m6-nut` | 10 | 11.05 | 5.2 | 8.9 | the same table |
| `m8-nut` | 13 | 14.38 | 6.8 | 11.6 | the same table |

| Name | Hole | Outside | Thickness | From |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `m3-washer` | 3.2 | 7 | 0.5 | ISO 7089:2000, plain washers, normal series, product grade A: d1 nominal (its least), d2 nominal (its most) and h nominal |
| `m4-washer` | 4.3 | 9 | 0.8 | the same table |
| `m5-washer` | 5.3 | 10 | 1 | the same table |
| `m6-washer` | 6.4 | 12 | 1.6 | the same table |
| `m8-washer` | 8.4 | 16 | 1.6 | the same table |

- **How they were read** (2026-11-18): ISO's own text is sold, so each table was read from copies of it, two at least that agree on every figure here, saved with their addresses in `~/print-shop/proofs/m20-through/sources/`. ISO 4032: a copy of the 2012 edition, the 1999 edition's preview from iTeh, and globalfastener.com's two pages for it. ISO 7089: globalfastener.com's page for the 2000 edition and Westfield Fasteners' table. Two other pages each differed from those in one figure not used here (one nut's least height, one nut's height), and aren't relied on.
- **A bolt's width** is its thread's name: M5 is 5 mm (`assembly.DIAMETER`).
- **A nut's own thread isn't judged.** ISO 898-2 pairs a class 8 nut with a class 8.8 bolt so that the pair can be tightened to the bolt's proof load without the thread stripping (its clause on the design of bolt and nut assemblies, for M5 to M39), and a bolt here is held to its proof load. M3 and M4 are outside that clause.

**What printed PETG takes pressed** (`assembly.PRESS`, mqucifer/print-shop#168). Under a washer, a nut or a bolt's shank the plastic is pressed, and Prusament PETG's datasheet (version 1.1, 16-02-2022) has no compressive figure: the word isn't on it. So a pressure is held to the same sheet's figures for a pull, which stand in for it, and every reply says so:
- **pressed across its layers,** which pushes them together: 45 MPa, the low end of "Tensile Yield Strength [MPa] 47 ± 2" for a specimen printed flat (ISO 527-1);
- **pressed along its layers,** which spreads them sideways and so pulls one off the next: 14 MPa, the low end of "Interlayer Adhesion [MPa] 18 ± 4", the figure `bend`'s 1% across the layers comes from.
- **Why a pull's figure stands in on the safe side** (the operator's ruling on mqucifer/print-shop#169): a thermoplastic yields pressed at no less than it yields pulled, so holding a pressure to the pull's figure doesn't pass what the plastic wouldn't take. That's the reasoning the stand-in rests on, with no figure of its own from a source, and both figures are taken at the low end of the sheet's range besides.
- A push is across the layers where it's nearer the way they're stacked than their plane. Nothing here has been compared with a print, and a crush test on the Sponsor's own PETG would be the figure to trust.

**PETG's density** (`assembly.DENSITY`, mqucifer/print-shop#170): 1.27 g/cm³. Prusament PETG's datasheet (Prusa Polymers, version 1.1, last update 16-02-2022), in its table "Typical material properties": "Density [g/cm3] 1.27", by ISO 1183. The sheet, as fetched on 2026-11-18 from the address `~/print-shop/proofs/m20-through/sources/README.md` gives, and its text are in `~/print-shop/proofs/m20-dovetail/sources/`. A part's own weight is its solid volume at that. A printed part has walls and infill and weighs less, so the solid weight is the careful one to hang from a latch.

**An insert's pull-out has no source yet.** A brass insert melted into printed PETG pulls out of its plastic long before a steel bolt stretches, so the insert's figure is the one that matters, and none is in the table. The figures found on 2026-11-17 were for other plastics (a test in nylon), an average across several plastics with no figure for PETG, or a claim with no method, and the two pages that might have given more couldn't be read. **So a bolted joint with no figure is `UNREAD`, never `OK` on its bolt alone** (the operator's ruling on mqucifer/print-shop#160): where a bolt has any pull to hold, and neither its insert nor its joint has a figure, the answer names the joint and the insert as unread, exit 2, and no need passes on it. A joint gives its own `pull_n` and `source`, from the request or a pull test, and is then read against the least of that and its bolt. A pull test on the Sponsor's own PETG and inserts is the figure to trust, and is the operator's to put to the Sponsor when the table's run is near.

### `load`

(`bench/load.py`.) `--weight` kg is shared equally between the places `--at` names, each on the assembly's surface, within 0.5 mm, and each spread over the surface within `--over` mm of it (10 unless given: a cup's foot, not a point). It pulls `--toward -z`, down, unless given.

- **`--lift PART`** is a load case of its own (mqucifer/print-shop#170), in place of `--weight` and `--at`: the assembly lifted by that part, as a table is by its top. It's held `fixed` by the flat faces at that part's highest level, against `--toward`, and by nothing else: the file's own `held` is set aside, and the reply says so. Every other part hangs by its own weight, `--toward` the way down: its solid's volume at its material's density, in N by standard gravity, 9.80665 m/s². It's put on every node of that part's own elements, a fifth of each element's share on each mid-side node less a twentieth on each corner, which is a uniform pull on a second-order tetrahedron, scaled so the part's total is its solid's and not its mesh's. `loads.N` has each part's `volume_mm3` and `grams`, `force_n` is the total, and `lift` the part named. The lifted part's own weight is in the hand, and isn't put on.

- **In a run, its `layers` are held to `fit-bed`'s side** (mqucifer/print-shop#192, `rounds/print_sides.py`). The bench reads each part's strain against the `layers` the assembly file gives, and can't know which side the part will print on: `fit-bed` runs in the other image. So where a run reads `load`, the harness holds each part of the assembly it read to the side the run's latest `fit-bed` laid that part's file on, and where the axis differs, the reply leads with a line saying so: "`load` read the part lower with its layers stacked along x, as `assembly.json` says, but `fit-bed` laid `lower.step` on its -z side at call 1, where they'd stack along z: each strain across its layers here is read for x. Print it on a side square to x, or give it `"layers": "z"` and read `load` again." The reply's detail has it as `layers_against_fit_bed`. Given the assembly file, `fit-bed` reads only the two sides square to `layers`, so they part where it was given the STEP files, or the assembly changed since. *Evidence: a run read every strain against the wrong layers until a stand-in caught it (lesson runs19-joint:16).*
- **One body, solved once.** The parts are put in their places and joined by FreeCAD's `generalFuse`, so the faces that meet are shared and gmsh meshes both sides of each joint with the same nodes. CalculiX solves it linear and static through the FEM workbench, on second-order tetrahedra of at most half the thinnest part, and no finer than leaves about 40,000 of them, on one thread. Where a curved side would turn an element inside out, as on a bolt's hole far smaller than the mesh, gmsh straightens it (its high-order optimisation, mqucifer/print-shop#168): without that CalculiX solved nothing for a table-sized top with 5.5 mm holes at a 10 mm mesh. So the same parts don't always give the same figures to the last digit (mqucifer/print-shop#348): that optimisation orders the elements it moves by where they lie in the process's memory, which varies from run to run, so between two runs a few hundred of the curved mesh's nodes can move by up to 2e-3 mm, and every figure after them by about 1e-5 of itself, a joint's moment in its 4th decimal. That was 1 stool in 12 on CI and about 1 mesh in 40 on the Mac, on one thread or two. No gmsh option orders those elements, and the container can't turn off the address randomisation that varies the layout. On the same mesh, every solve and every figure is the same every time. The bench's own bounds are far wider than that spread: the second method's 5% (`AGREE`), and a part held below its limit by the same share. Where gmsh can't curve every element's sides onto the parts' faces without leaving one inside out, as on a traced glass frame's many small curves and sharp corners, the mesh is made again second-order still, ten nodes to each tetrahedron, with its elements' sides straight, their mid-side nodes halfway along them, so that none is inside out and each curved face is read as their chords; only gmsh's high-order optimisation is left out (mqucifer/print-shop#295). The reply says so, with gmsh's own words for why: FreeCAD keeps no mesh where gmsh ends with an error, and `load` used to say only "gmsh made no mesh of the joined parts: None". A mesh that curves without trouble is as it was. Where the straight one still has an element turned inside out, the reply says so and to give a finer `--mesh`. CalculiX runs on one thread too, for the whole and for every solve of a part round a bolt, a dovetail or a latch's tab, and for `bend` (mqucifer/print-shop#295): FreeCAD starts it on as many as the host has CPUs, 8 on the Mac and 4 on CI, not the container's 2, and its solver's memory grows with them. The container's `/tmp` is memory, held against the same 3 GiB, so each mesh's and each solve's files are deleted once they've been read. Where the solve meets the container's memory, the reply leads with what to do: name a glass frame's `band`, or read fewer parts at once (or give a coarser `--mesh`, where the mesh is mostly its own size). Then it says what it met: it's said to meet the limit only where the kernel stopped a process for memory while that solve ran, by the container's own count (`oom_kill` in its cgroup's `memory.events`), not where what it held at the most, which counts the files it has read, reached the limit. It says with the mesh's elements and nodes, how many the mesh size alone would give and how many are finer round small features, which gmsh sizes by their curvature and not by `--mesh`, and that nothing is read. The weight is put on as a force on each node of the surface's triangles in the patch, and the reply says the patch's area and its middle, which is where the weight acts: a patch cut off by an edge isn't round.
- **Standing** holds each face in the plane square to it only, so a table's feet are free to spread, as on a floor: that's the more careful reading of the joints at the tops of its legs. Two nodes stop the rest sliding and turning, and carry nothing under a load square to the plane; a load along a standing plane is refused, saying to hold a plane `fixed`.
- **Each part** (`parts.NAME`):
  - `across_layers_pct`, the most it's pulled across its layers, the stretch along the way its layers are stacked, and `across_at`, where. It's held to the material's limit across its layers. A push across the layers parts nothing, so only a pull counts: a leg printed standing and pushed straight down passes.
  - `stretch_pct`, its largest stretch any way, a pull or a push (`stretch_is`), `stretch_at`, and `stretch_from_layers_deg`, how far out of its layers' plane it points: 0° is along them and 90° straight across. It's held to the material's limit in its layers' plane.
- **The limits are the material's, and the reply names their source,** as `bend` does. `petg`: Prusament PETG's datasheet (Prusa Polymers, version 1.1, 16-02-2022). Across the layers, 1%, as `bend` takes it: "Interlayer Adhesion [MPa] 18 ± 4" over a tensile modulus of 1.5 ± 0.1 GPa is about 1.2% strain, 0.9% at the low end of both. In the layers' plane, 5.1%, the same sheet's elongation at yield for a part printed flat. The stiffness is that modulus, 1500 MPa, and Poisson's ratio 0.38, `bend`'s. How far anything moves is in proportion to one over the stiffness, and the strains are too, as the load is a force: a sparse part is softer than this.
- **Each joint** (`joints.NAME`, the two parts' names joined by `+`): the force and the moment the second part puts on the first, about the middle of the faces that meet, as `force_n` and `moment_nmm`; and of those, `pull_n` square to the faces (a pull is more than 0, a push less), `shear_n` along them, `bending_nmm` and `twist_nmm`.
  - **It's read from the nodes' own balance,** not the surface's stress (`load.element_forces`): at each node of the faces that meet, the force the first part's own elements carry, each element's stiffness times how far its nodes moved, less what's put on that part there from outside. CalculiX balances exactly that sum at every node, so a part's joints add up to what's on it. The first reading summed the stress on the faces, and at a sharp inside corner, where a leg meets the underside of a top, it was up to 32% low: four legs carried 20.1 N of a 29.4 N weight between them. They now carry 7.34, 7.37, 7.34 and 7.36 N, 29.4 in all. Each element's ten nodes are taken in FreeCAD's own order, its corners and then its mid-side nodes side by side (mqucifer/print-shop#320): the one pass used to find each mid-side node by where it lies, the one nearest its side's straight midpoint, and on a small curved element on a bolt's bore that took one side's node for another's, so the element's forces went twice to one node and never to its sixth. The order is checked before anything is read from it. Each element's ten nodes are distinct; each mid-side node is its side's in every element with that side, and no other side's or corner; and each lies near its own side, in the middle half of the side along its chord and no further off it than half its length. That last doesn't tell two sides of one element apart, as a neighbouring side's midpoint lies within it too: the shared sides do, wherever another element has the side. And in one pass the elements must balance as CalculiX solved them: at every node not held, what they carry is the load put on it, with each node moved as CalculiX prints it to seven figures (`*NODE PRINT`, beside the .frd's six that FreeCAD reads), within what those figures can leave there: each element's stiffness, each term taken as its size, times half a unit in the seventh figure of each of its nodes' movements, summed over the elements at the node, on each axis, and a hundred-millionth of the load. That can't be passed in the right order, whatever the shape. Every reading is still of the .frd's movements, as it was. An element whose nodes aren't in the order CalculiX took them carries other forces, and leaves the rest at its nodes, so it's caught wherever that's more than the figures account for. A bound as a share of the load alone wouldn't do: a cantilever 115 mm long, read in the right order from the .frd's six figures, leaves 4% of its load at one node, from the rounding of its 2 mm movement. Where any of these fails, the reply is `NO ANSWER`, naming the parts the elements are in, from the parts' elements as they're reached without crossing a joint, whatever their mid-side nodes are.
  - **Every joint is solved as one piece,** the faces tied all over, as a glued joint is and as a bolted one is while its bolts hold. What differs is how each is judged.
  - **Bolted:** what each bolt has to hold (`bolts`, and `bolt_pull_n` for the worst). The joint's pull is shared between its bolts. Its bending moment, less what a push through the faces' middle takes off it, is taken about the faces' edge on the pushed side, each bolt in proportion to its distance from that edge. The worst is held to `holds_n`: the least of the bolt's proof load, the insert's pull-out where one has a source, and the joint's own `pull_n` (`holds_from` says which). With neither an insert's figure nor the joint's, and a pull to hold, the joint is unread (`insert_unread`). The shear is shared between the bolts and said, and isn't judged: where an insert bears sideways on its plastic has no figure. A bolted joint's faces are in one plane.
  - **Through** (mqucifer/print-shop#168, `bench/load_through.py`): a bolt right through both parts, with nothing that depends on an insert. Its bolts' pulls are found as a bolted joint's are, and it's judged by the worst of three, for every bolt, in both parts:
    - **the bolt,** its pull against its proof load (`bolt_pull_n`, `holds_n`);
    - **the plastic pressed:** under each washer, or under the nut where it sits in a pocket, the bolt's pull over what bears there (`bolts.N.ends.K.pressure_mpa`, `bears_mm2`), and where the shank leans on the hole's wall, the shear over the bolt's width times the hole's length (`wall_pressure_mpa`, over `wall_bears_mm2`; less at a peaked bore, below), each against what the plastic takes pressed that way to its layers. What bears is the ring from the hole, or the washer's own hole where that's wider, out to the washer's outside, or to the nut's bearing face, cut to the part's own flat faces at the hole's end. A washer that isn't wholly on the part fails, saying how much of it is, and at a peaked bore wholly is that ring less the peak's cut;
    - **the plastic round the hole,** read by the FEM as strain, as any part is (`bolts.N.ends.K.round`). The part alone is cut off two diameters of what bears on it from the bolt's axis and held where it's cut. The bolt's pull is spread over what the washer or the nut bears on, toward the other part, and its shear is put on the half of the hole's wall the shank leans on, most where it leans square, between where the parts meet and the seat (below). It's solved on a mesh of its own, and read within three quarters of the cut, as the hold strains what's beside it: pulled across the layers against the material's limit there, and the largest stretch any way against its limit in their plane. A hole near an edge leaves a thin bridge of plastic that the shank stretches, and that's where it tears out.
    - **Which wall takes the shear** (mqucifer/print-shop#245): the bore's own stretch at that end, from where the parts meet to the seat, the one its pressure is read over, and nothing past either. A cylinder face is the bore's where it's on the axis and of its radius, as before, so a counterbore isn't wall; of one that runs across either level, the part between is loaded. Where the bore carries on past a nut's slot for the bolt's tip, the shank doesn't lean there: spread over that too, the plastic's strain read a quarter low and a tear could read `OK`. At a peaked bore (below) it's that stretch's round less the peak, as a peak's flat faces aren't cylinder faces. `bolts.N.ends.K.sheared_mm2` is the wall that took it, beside `hole_length_mm`.
    - **The reply names what's nearest its limit** (`gives_first`), and by what share (`nearest_share`, 1 at the limit): it's never `OK` on the bolt alone. A joint whose bolts have nothing to hold, pushed together and not sheared, says so.
    - **Before anything is solved,** each bolt's way through is read from the parts' own faces (`bench/through.py`): a round or peaked hole in each part along the bolt's axis, within 0.05 mm of the place named, no narrower than the bolt, the two meeting end to end where the parts meet, each ending on a flat face square to the bolt. One without is `NO ANSWER`, saying which part and why.
    - **A peaked bore is a bore** (mqucifer/print-shop#237). A round bore that lies level on the bed reads as needing support, and rightly: its roof looks down past 45°. It's printed peaked, and `load`, `fit-bed` and `rays` read one shape as that: one round face about the axis and, where that face is missing, two flat faces tangent to it that rise to a point no flatter than 45°, covering all the way from the round to the point, along the whole stretch. Each says that it's peaked, its diameter and which way its point is (`peak`: `toward`, `rises_deg` from level with the point up, and `point_mm` from the axis). What's read on it:
      - **its seat:** the ring under the washer or the nut less the peak's cut, and nothing else (`seat_mm2`, beside the whole `ring_mm2`). The pressure is the pull over `bears_mm2`, the real area, against the same limit. The seat is worked out by arithmetic and read from the part's own faces as well: less on the faces is a seat cut by something else, which fails as an edge or a step does, and more is `DISAGREE`;
      - **where the shank leans:** on what of the round's half is still there. Toward the point, a peak whose flats rise at h leaves 1 - sin h of the bolt's width times the hole's length, 26.9% at 47°; leaning square to that it leaves (1 + cos h) / 2, and away from it all. The flat faces aren't counted. The reply says the share and the area (`wall_bears_mm2`), and reads the area again from the mesh's triangles on the stretch: the two either side of the pressure's limit is `DISAGREE`;
      - **in `rays`,** given a STEP: each point of the wall as the diameter it says, twice its distance from the axis on the round and twice its flank's plane's on the peak, every one within `--tol` of `--d`. `across_mm` still runs up to the point, so `bore_d_mm` is the diameter. An STL has no faces to say it's peaked, and reads as before;
      - **what isn't one,** and is refused as before: a slot's end, whose flats never meet, a D, an open groove, a peak cut short of its point, and a peak flatter than 45°. The downward reading doesn't change: a round bore level on the bed still needs support.
    - <a id="a-drop-in-channel"></a>**A drop-in channel** (mqucifer/print-shop#302): a U with a round bottom, open on one side, running across the join, that the bolt drops into from that side, its head bearing on one part's wall and its nut on the other's. `load` and `fit-bed` refuse it as a slot's end, and rightly, wherever along it the bolt's place is put, unless the joint declares it (`channel`, above). Declared, it's read in each part from the part's own faces (`bench/through.py`): one round face about the declared axis whose one gap, the same along the whole stretch, is centred on the declared open side and no more than half the round; and its walls where the channel ends, the head's in the first part named and the nut's in the second. What isn't that is `NO ANSWER`, saying what it is: a round hole, a round open another way or along another axis, less than half a round, open in two places, or closed along some of the stretch. A declared wall that isn't where the channel ends is `NO ANSWER` in `load`, which has no seat to solve, and `FAIL` in `fit-bed`, naming it. Each says it's a channel, its round's diameter, which way it's open and how wide where the round leaves off (`channel`: `toward`, `round_deg`, `open_mm`). What `load` reads on it:
      - **the clamp,** as any through joint's: the bolt's pull against its proof load, and each washer's pressure on its wall over what it bears on, the ring less the channel's cut, the round and the walls straight up from where it leaves off: by arithmetic, π(R²_out − R²_in) less 2∫ r·asin(w/r) dr from the ring's inside to its outside, w half the channel's width, and from the wall's own faces. Less on the faces is a wall too narrow for the washer, which fails naming the wall; more is `DISAGREE`;
      - **the shank's lean** on the round only, as a peak's: none toward the open side, half square to it, all away from it;
      - **the open side carries nothing,** and the reply says so for each part (`toward_open_n`). What pushes the shank toward a part's open side lifts that part off the bolt: it fails, saying by how much, and it's what gives first. Only the rest leans on the round. So a channel carries no shear along its open way, ever: one of the two parts is always pushed toward its own open side.
    - **A bore's round is read stretch by stretch** (mqucifer/print-shop#237): at each level of the stretch the bolt's place is at, the round faces there must close, or leave the one gap a peak fills. Two arcs apart along the axis, or one after the other, never add up to a hole. A round face that closes anywhere along its own length is taken as closed along all of it, so a bore that's round for some of its length and peaked for the rest, in one face, is read as round, and its seat at the peaked end then fails.
    - **Second methods:** each bolt's pull on the coarser mesh and by statics, and the pressures from them, `DISAGREE` where one lands on the other side of a limit; and the plastic round each hole again on a mesh half as coarse again.
    - **What a through joint's reading leaves out:** it's read with its bolts snug and no more, so a bolt tightened hard, which presses its washers on the plastic by its own preload, isn't in it, and nor is PETG creeping under that; the pressure is taken as even under each washer; all the shear is taken by the shanks, none by friction, and a twist about the faces' normal by none; the bolt's length and its nut's thread aren't checked.
  - **Dovetail** (mqucifer/print-shop#170, `bench/dovetail.py` and `bench/load_dovetail.py`): a tail slid into a socket, held by its own shape.
    - **Whether it can be put together** (`ways_in.NAME`, `bench/way_in.py`, mqucifer/print-shop#184 and mqucifer/print-shop#185). eval-table-1's leg split read `OK` slid in along z, and its plan called it verified, though the socket's mouth was narrower than the tail's tip and the only way in was sideways. Its latch, a solid stub named as the latch's arm, read as holding the lifted leg, though nothing could bend it past the socket's wall. So before anything is solved, after the parts are joined as the solve joins them, each dovetail is taken apart the way it went together:
      - **The way out.** The tail's part is slid out of its socket against `slides` until its box is past the socket's part's. Each of its faces is drawn out along that way into a prism, and each prism is cut with the socket's part (OCCT's `common`), so what a face passes on the way out is what it has to pass on the way in, with no step to fall between. Faces that only run along the way draw nothing. What the latch's box holds is the latch's: each face is cut at the box, as a tab's face can run on into its part, and where the tab is on the socket's part, that part is cut there too. Anything else in the way, and the joint **fails**: it can't be put together that way, whatever the load, and nothing is solved. The same sweep along each of the six ways says which ways the parts come apart past nothing but the latch (`comes_out`), so which way the tail does go in (`goes_in`), or that none does.
      - **The latch going on.** What the latch's tab passes on the way, seen along it, is how far the tab has to move to let the tail by: its least width across (`latch.depth_mm`), as `fit-on` reads a place, and the way that's across (`latch.across`). `bend` then reads the tab as anyone would call it: the tab's part where the assembly puts it, the latch's `arm` and `root`, pushed that far, away from what it passes, with the part's own layers (`latch.bend`, with the command). The latch is credited with holding only where `bend` reads `OK`. Where `bend` reads it over the material's limit, or can't read it pushed that way at all, as a stub whose push runs along it from its root, the joint **fails**, with `bend`'s words, and nothing is solved: a latch that can't get on holds nothing, lifted or pulled. A tab that passes nothing on the way in has nothing to bend.
      - **Second method:** rays along the way out through a mesh of each part, as `fit-on`'s second method reads a way on, at every 0.25 mm: an overlap two rays or more across outside the latch's box, seen along the way, blocks it. The sweep and the rays apart on whether the way out is blocked is `DISAGREE`, and so is the tab's depth by the sweep and by rays 0.02 mm apart over what it passes, more than twice their spacing and 0.01 mm apart. The tab's strain has `bend`'s own second method, its beam, said in the reply.
      - **What it leaves out:** a way in that isn't one straight line; friction and how hard the tail is pushed; play, as the parts are read where the file puts them, home; and a hook so thin along the way that a ray at each 0.25 mm step passes it, which only the second method can miss. The solve ties its faces all over, as every joint's, and tied faces pull as well as they push; a real flank only pushes. So what the solve gives is what the joint carries, and that's put back on its faces as pushes alone.
    - **Which way the load drives the tail** (`drive_n`): the joint's force along `slides`, more than 0 into its seat and less than 0 out of it, the way it came in. It's read on the coarser mesh and by statics too.
    - **Each face by the way the tail slides in** (`faces`, the most pushed first): a face of the tail whose way out of it is nearer the slide than square to it stops the tail, a **seat**; any other runs along the slide, a **flank**, and a taper's flank leans into it. A face that leans against the way in is `NO ANSWER` before anything is solved: the tail could never have gone in that way, so `slides`, or which part is named first, is wrong.
    - **The pushes.** The tail is taken as stiff, bedded on its faces: it shifts and turns a little as one body, and each bit of each face pushes back square to itself, in proportion to how far the tail has pressed into it there, and not at all where the tail has come away. That's a footing's bearing pressure with no tension, on faces that lie every way. The shift and the turn that balance what the joint carries are found by Newton's method on a sum that's convex, so there's one answer. Each face has its `push_n`, how much of it bears (`bears_mm2`) and its most pressure (`pressure_mpa`), held to what the weaker of the two parts takes pressed that way to its layers.
    - **Play** (mqucifer/print-shop#188, `load_dovetail.play`). A printed tail is a slip fit, its flanks a few tenths of a mm clear of its socket, and the file draws it so. Read with only the faces drawn touching, a slip-fit tail lifted by a latch off its middle was held by that latch and the seat beside it pulling against each other, for many times what hung from it, and a sideways push landed on whatever small faces were drawn touching. So it's read the way a print takes the load. Before anything is solved, each flat face of the tail's part and a flat face of the socket's part that faces it square, more than 0 and at most 2 mm off, and what the two share seen that way, is a face too, with that much play, in pieces no wider than 0.5 mm; and the latch's catch has its own, its hook under its ledge (`play_mm`). Each piece pushes back only once the tail has moved its play into it, so the tail shifts and turns as one body until its play closes, and the faces that then touch carry the load. That needs the faces' own stiffness, a block of the plastic as deep as the joint reaches from its middle, pressed square: the modulus over that reach. All it decides is how far the faces that bear are pressed, a few hundredths of a mm, and so whether a face with play closes. So a weight down the slide stays on a seat, which stops the tail far short of a flank's play, and the play closes under what turns the tail or pushes it sideways. Each face has its `play_mm` and `closed_mm`, how much of that the move closes; the joint has its `shift_mm`, its `turn_deg` about its middle, and `faces_move_mm`, the most any of its faces moves, and the reply says which faces close and, of the rest, how near the nearest came. Where its faces move more than the 2 mm of play read, it says faces further apart may close first. With every face drawn touching, the pushes are what they were, whatever the stiffness. **The 2 mm is a judgement, not a sourced figure:** twice the slide check's 1 mm `--hold`, past which a part isn't held, so a printed slip fit's few tenths and a loose fit's mm are both read. Faces further apart aren't counted, so a load only they could hold is left held by nothing and the joint opens, `FAIL`, saying faces up to 2 mm apart were counted: wider play is never read as holding.
    - **The latch's tab is the latch's:** none of the faces in its box bears for the tail, as none is read in the neck or the walls. A hook drawn touching its window's sides took a sideways push at over 2000 MPa before.
    - **What no face pushes back against is left over** (`left_n`, `left_nmm`, `opens`), and the joint fails, saying it opens. Three loads do it. One that draws the tail back out: no face pushes that way. One a face would have to pull to carry. And a sideways pull on a taper: a tapered flank's push has a share back up the slide, so a load that levers the tail off its socket squeezes it out, unless the weight seats it harder or a latch holds it. **Friction isn't counted,** by the same decision as glue: on a slope of 1 in 20 real friction would hold, and the reading doesn't lean on it.
    - **An unseating force is held by the latch only.** With no latch it's `FAIL`, naming the force and saying only friction or glue would hold it. With one, the latch's catch is one more face, on the tail's part, that pushes it home once its own play has closed, and `latch.holds_n` is what the catch then carries: what draws the tail out, and what the flanks add as they're pushed. Where that's more than 1.5 times what draws the tail out, the reply says why: the tail turns on the faces beside the latch before any further off closes, and the latch and those faces pull against each other, as a lever's ends do.
    - **The latch** (`latch`): before anything is solved, its catch is read from the two parts' own faces: a flat face of the tab, inside its `arm`, that faces the way the tail would leave, with a flat face of the other part over it within 1 mm, the slide check's own `--hold`, and what the two share seen along the slide (`catch_mm2`, `catch_at`, `play_mm`). A latch that catches on nothing is `NO ANSWER`. What it holds is then judged twice: the pressure on the catch, and the tab, solved by itself: the part inside `arm`, held at `root`, with that pull on its hook's face the way the ledge pushes it, read as strain across its layers and any way (`latch.tab`). **Its way on is read first,** as above: what the latch holds is only read once `bend` has read it getting on.
    - **The tail's neck and the socket's walls** (`neck`, `walls`): each part alone, cut off round the joint, once the joint's width across the slide from the joint's own box, and held where it's cut, with the faces' pushes on it, worked out again on that mesh's own triangles so they balance exactly. Read as any part is, further than a quarter of that from the cut: pulled across its layers, and its largest stretch any way. They aren't read under a load the joint doesn't hold.
    - **The joint's verdict is the worst of these, named** (`gives_first`, `nearest_share`).
    - **Second methods:** the pushes again from the coarser mesh's reading and from statics, `DISAGREE` where one balances and another doesn't, or where one drives the tail out and another in; and the tab, the neck and the walls each on a mesh half as coarse again.
    - **What a dovetail's reading leaves out:** glue, wholly; friction; play on a curved face, which bears only where it's drawn touching, and between faces more than 2 mm apart; a turn that isn't small, as the tail's move is read as a small one; how a tab's own give shares what its catch holds with a seat beside it, as the latch is read as stiff as any face; how far a taper slides down as its faces are pressed, which turns on their stiffness, taken as a block of the plastic as deep as the joint's reach, and decides whether a seat with a little play under a wedge closes (the planted wedge slides 0.26 mm of its tip's 1 mm); how a seat and a taper drawn touching share a weight in a real print, where the bedding shares it by how square each is to the slide; and the pushes are a reading of the joint's balance, not a contact solve.
  - **Touching:** `FAIL` where it's pulled apart; where it carries shear or bending with no push to hold it; and where its push, which acts at `push_at`, falls outside the faces that meet (`push_off_faces_mm`), which tips it open. The friction it needs to stay put, its shear over its push, is `friction_needed`. **That has no default limit:** `--friction MU` holds it, and without it it's a reading that says so. A touching joint whose faces aren't in one plane isn't judged, and a note says how much of it the solve pulls on.
  - **Bonded:** its force is read, and where it carries anything to speak of the joint is unread (`glue_unread`) and the answer is `UNREAD`, exit 2, as for an insert with no figure (mqucifer/print-shop#170, the operator's ruling on mqucifer/print-shop#171). Glue is a second hold only, and no glue line in printed PETG has a figure with a source, so nothing says it holds, and `bonded` can't stand in for a dovetail or a bolt. Before this it read `OK` with nothing judged.
- **How far it moves:** each loaded place, along the load (`loads.N.moves_mm`), the most of those (`moves_mm`), and the most any point moves and where (`most_moves_mm`). A need holds `moves_mm` with `at_most`.
- **Verdict:** `OK`; `FAIL`, naming each part over a limit, each bolt over what it holds, each through-bolt's washer, nut or hole whose plastic is over its limit, and each touching joint that comes away, with what to change; or `UNREAD`, where nothing read fails and a bolted joint with a pull to hold has no figure for its insert, or a bonded joint carries load. A need never passes on `UNREAD`, whatever verdicts it names.
- **Second methods:**
  - **The whole solve again on a mesh half as coarse again,** and every judged figure on both. Two readings of one figure on opposite sides of its limit are `DISAGREE`; on the same side, the gap is said and the finer mesh's figure stands. A sharp inside corner has no one strain in any mesh, so that's where they part.
  - **The held faces' reaction,** which CalculiX prints, against the weight: past 1% apart the load didn't go on as asked, and it's `NO ANSWER`.
  - **Statics,** for a joint with every held face on one side of it: what crosses it is the load beyond it, and that load's moment about the joint, whatever the mesh. A bolt's pull is worked out from it again, and one on the other side of what it holds is `DISAGREE`. Where the parts are held or joined on both sides of a joint, as a table's legs are through the floor, statics alone doesn't give it, and the reply says so.
- **What it can't read:**
  - a part's walls and infill, where the assembly file gives it no `print`: such a part is solid here, so a sparse part is weaker and bends more than this. One that has it is read as printed ([below](#a-part-as-printed));
  - a printed part's stiffness the other way: one modulus every way, with the layers only in the limits;
  - creep under a weight left on for months, a knock, someone sitting down, and buckling;
  - a bolt's preload, a joint that opens, and contact: it's the first, linear answer;
  - shear where an insert bears on its plastic, a glue's strength, a nut's thread and a bolt's length;
  - the parts' own weight under a `--weight`: `--lift` is the case that reads it, alone;
  - a dovetail's glue and its friction, neither of which is counted.
  - Nothing here has been compared with a print.
- **Planted** (`tests/cae_cases/make_fixtures.py`, `load/`), each figure in `~/print-shop/proofs/m20-load/planted/`:
  - **A beam** 120 x 20 x 10, held at one end, 1 kg on its top 115 mm out: its largest stretch is 0.217% at the root, where ε = 6FL/(Ebh²) is 0.226%, and the loaded place moves 1.870 mm, where FL³/(3EI) is 1.99 mm for a weight at 115 mm and the patch's middle is at 113.2. 1.9 s.
  - **The same beam as two halves bolted at its middle:** 0.217% and 1.870 mm again, the same to three places. The joint carries 9.80 N along its faces and 522.0 N·mm, and statics says 9.81 N and 522.0. Its one M4 bolt at the faces' middle holds 104.4 N, 522 over the 5 mm to the bottom edge. With the joint's own figure at 500 N, made up for the test, `OK`; at 60 N, `FAIL`; and with none, `UNREAD`, where the bolt's own 5092 N would have passed it.
  - **Laid across its layers:** under 5 kg the beam's root is at 1.085%. Printed flat, that's along its layers, within 5.1%, and 0.599% across them: `OK`. Printed standing on its end, 1.081% is across its layers, over 1%: `FAIL`, saying to lay it so its layers run along the pull.
  - **A block on a slab,** touching, 2 kg on its top: pushed together by 19.61 N through the faces' middle, needing no friction. Pulled up, or pushed sideways, `FAIL`.

Its bolts hold 44.6 N at most, and it reads `UNREAD`: its inserts have no figure.

Both printed flat, layers along z, so the tab's pull is across its layers.

Now the lower drops by the latch's 0.2 mm, its seats come away, and it turns 1.26° on the latch until all four flanks close their 0.15 mm: the latch holds 1.487 N, the weight, by the solve, on the coarser mesh and by statics, and its tab is at 0.25%: `OK`. 31 s.

Now they're the latch's, and the tail shifts and turns 1.02° until its flanks close: the +x flank takes 36.2 N, 1.87 MPa of 14, the nearest anything is to its limit, and the -x flank 16.6 N: `OK`, the latch holding nothing. 22 s.

The latch holds 8.61 N, 5.9 times the weight, and the reply says it's levered; its tab is pulled across its layers by 1.47%, 1.40% on the coarser mesh: `FAIL`, as before, when it held 9.35 N at 1.59%. Its faces move 3.2 mm at most, which the reply says is more than the 2 mm of play read. 31 s.

Before, it failed too, opening by 0.48 N, the hook's sides having taken the rest. 27 s.

Now the seat stops the slide within hundredths of a mm and keeps 47.18 N of the 49.03; the weight's turn, 0.81°, closes both flanks, which take 17.8 N each at 1.35 MPa, and their slope the rest of the weight: `OK`, the seat's 2.12 MPa the nearest its limit. 27 s.

- **A whole furniture assembly** (mqucifer/print-shop#295; the side table's assembly run couldn't read its cases). Each figure here, and every command and reply, is in `~/print-shop/proofs/ux-295/`.
  - **A glass frame.** A frame `pieces build` made from a traced picture, with sharp V corners and lips sized piece by piece by `--min-visible`, read "gmsh made no mesh of the joined parts: None". gmsh couldn't curve every second-order element onto its many small curves without leaving some inside out ("Optimization failed (some measures below critical value)", 108 elements with a negative Jacobian), ended with an error, and FreeCAD kept no mesh and said nothing. A mesh that did get through was CalculiX's element "turned inside out". Now it's meshed again straight-sided and the reply says why. The same frame then met the container's 3 GiB: FreeCAD started CalculiX on as many threads as the host has CPUs, its files in `/tmp` held memory, and CalculiX alone needs 2.5 GB for it. On one thread, with the files deleted once read, it reads whole.
  - **Read as a lower bound.** Where the whole still can't be read, a part that names its `band` is read again without it: the reply's first line says `read as a lower bound`, names the parts and gives why, and `lower_bound` has both. The rest of each part is read with no stiffness for its band, as it would be with the band gone, never with a stiffness made up for it, and the band itself is unread. **Which figures are a bound:** how far the loaded places move along the load, taken together, is: with less in it the assembly gives more under the same weight, never less. Each part's strain, and each joint's and bolt's share of the load, can be more or less than with the band in, and isn't one; the reply says so. **A lift keeps the band's weight** (mqucifer/print-shop#375): lifted, each part hangs by its own weight, and a part without its band would hang by less, so every strain would be read under less than it carries. So the band's weight is kept, its volume of the part at the part's density, hung from the faces where the band met the rest of the part (each a face of the part read without its band that the band's solids lie on, the middle of its largest triangle within 0.02 mm of them), each of their triangles taking its share by its area, as a weight on a place is; its stiffness is left out, never made up. The reply's first line says `read as a bound`, and the verdict's that the band is unread. Every part still hangs by its whole weight with less of it to carry it, so how far what hangs moves along its weight, taken together, is an upper bound under the full weight. **A share is the full weight's only where statics fixes its joint**, everything beyond it hanging from it alone: the reply names those joints (`lower_bound.statics_fixed`), whose loads and bolts' shares are as with the band in. Any other joint, in a loop of two ways, can carry more or less, as the band's stiffness moves load between them, and so can its bolts: the reply says so and names them (`lower_bound.shares_not_bound`). **No part's strain is a bound:** a banded part's is read on the rest of it, and its band, where its worst may be, is unread; and any part's, at a corner where parts meet, reads by the mesh there (the operator's review, mqucifer/print-shop#376). The hanging part's line gives the band's grams and the area they hang from, and `lower_bound.weight_kept_g` each band's weight. A band that meets none of its part's faces is `NO ANSWER`: its weight would have nowhere to go.
  - **A latch at any angle**, named in its tab's part's own axes (`"frame": "part"`, in [the assembly file](#the-assembly-file)), is carried with the part. A part turned off the assembly's axes is cut round its dovetail in its own axes: a box of the assembly's round its joint took in all of a post at 45° and cut it nowhere ("too small round the dovetail joint").
  - **A large assembly** can be past what the container solves: gmsh sizes each bore by its curvature, 12 elements a turn, whatever `--mesh` is, and a table's bolts are dozens of bores. The reply then says which limit it met, from the container's own `memory.peak`, at which mesh, with how many elements are finer than `--mesh` asks, and that nothing is read. No limit was raised, and no bore is meshed coarser to fit: a coarse bore reads the strain beside it low. It's read in two passes, below.
  - **Planted** (`tests/cae_cases/make_fixtures.py`, `turned/`, `large/`; `glass_panel.py` and `glass_joined.py` for the frame):
    - **The latched post of `play/edge.json`, its latch named in its own axes** (`turned/own.json`): lifted, every figure is the same as with the latch named in the assembly's; only `bend`'s command names the part's own file.
    - **Four posts at quarter turns round a middle** (`turned/quarter.json`), 100 mm out, each latch named once in the post's own axes, each post pulled up by 0.15 kg at its top: `OK`. Each latch holds 1.47 N, its hook passes 0.600 mm deep by the sweep and by rays, and `bend` reads its tab pushed that far +y in the post's own axes at 0.22% at its nearer edge, 0.222% by the strip formula; the tab is pulled across its layers by 0.25 to 0.26%. 105 s. **The same from 45°** (`turned/eighth.json`): `OK`, the same 1.47 N and 0.22%; the rays read the hook 0.614 mm deep on a grid square to the assembly, where the sweep reads 0.600, within their two spacings; each neck is cut 16.9 mm from its joint in its post's own axes. 105 s.
    - **A table of 24 parts and 56 M5 through-bolts** (`large/table.json`: a top of four tiles on aprons, legs in two pieces spliced by a plate, ribs, all through-bolted; round figures only), 20 kg over its top: 358,875 elements and 618,849 nodes at the 10.459 mm the mesh chose, of about 42,000 the mesh size alone would give. `NO ANSWER`: it met the container's 3 GiB, memory.peak 3221225472, after 156 s. At `--mesh 30` it's still 208,926 elements. At 6 elements a turn round each bore, 155,910, CalculiX alone ran out of the 3 GiB; its iterative solver ran past the 600 s. One corner alone, 6 parts and 11 bolts, is 91,537 elements at 10.459 mm and runs out too. The whole is unread here.
    - **A bolt left out** (`large/stool.json`, `large/stool-one-out.json`): a top on four legs, each held by one M5 through-bolt with its nut in a slot, its feet fixed, pushed +x at the top by 5 kg: `OK`, the loaded place moving 0.128 mm, 83 s and 1.2 GB at the most. With leg2's bolt left out its joint is `touching`, and it tips open, its push 52.89 mm outside the faces that meet: `FAIL`, naming it. The loaded place moves 0.128 mm in both: every joint is solved tied, as a bolted one is while its bolts hold, so a bolt left out changes what gives first, not how far anything moves.
    - **A glass frame** (`glass_panel.py`, drawn in code: six smooth lines crossing at shallow angles, traced at 160 mm, its map fixed three times as `pieces build` says; built 6 mm thick round 3 mm panes, lips 1.2 and 0.6 by `--min-visible 50`, play 0.25, a 12 mm border). Alone, held by its -x edge, 1 kg on its +x border: gmsh fails to curve it, and straight-sided it reads whole, `OK`. At `--mesh 4`, as the test reads it: 92,121 elements, the frame pulled across its layers by 0.323% and 0.319% on the coarser mesh, 272 s, 2.39 GB at the most. At the default 3 mm: 121,213 elements, 0.332%, 316 s, and 2.95 GB, within 60 MB of the limit, too near for a test to rely on. A 120 mm panel of the same picture meshes curved and doesn't show the failure. **The control for the lower bound:** the same frame alone at `--mesh 4`, read without its band though it reads whole (`band_forced.py`): its loaded place moves 27.349 mm, where whole it moves 16.123, further as the bound says; its strain across its layers reads 0.376%, where whole 0.323%, higher here, though that isn't a bound. Bolted down through its border to a block by three M5 through-bolts (`glass_joined.py`): whole it's 136,733 elements and meets the 3 GiB; with its band left out, the panel inside its border, it reads as a lower bound, `OK`, the frame at 0.349% across its layers and 0.338% on the coarser mesh, each bolt holding 47.6 N by the solve and by statics, the joint 9.81 N, the weight. 221 s.
    - **A band under `--parts` and a lift** (mqucifer/print-shop#375, `-m bands`; the same picture traced at 120 mm, as at 80 `pieces build` leaves a piece no pane, bolted to a block by `glass_joined.py`; and `band_plate.py`, a plain 6 mm border round a 3 mm panel 60 mm wide, its panel named as its band). **Read whole where two passes can:** the plain frame, `--parts`, 1 kg on its border: two passes read it whole, `OK`, no bound, 89 s on the Mac. Two passes refuse the traced frame by their own checks (pass 2's evening-out moved 37% of the frame's push, past the 20%; at `--mesh 4`, elements without six mid-side nodes of their own), which are neither time nor memory, and are said as they are. Lifted by the block, two passes refuse the plain frame too, the block on its own 0.046 N out of balance in pass 2, the same on main: a lift two passes can't balance is parked, not this issue's. **Memory, meshing:** with the first pass's mesher a program that takes memory until it's stopped (`mesh_memory_forced.py`), under the 3 GiB, the kernel stops it, not `load`, and the traced frame reads its band's bound, under the weight and lifted, about 75 s each. **Time:** two passes left two seconds (`reserve_forced.py` with `--parts`) stop before their first pass, and the band's lower bound reads, 75 s. **The lift's control,** the traced frame lifted by the block in one pass, whole and with the band's stiffness left out and its weight kept (`band_forced.py`, `band_outside.py`): the same 55.40 g hangs, 16.08 g of it the band's, hung from the 2,327 mm² where it met the border; the joint carries the same 0.5432 N, each bolt 0.916 N against 0.911 to 0.915 whole; the frame's middle moves 0.077 mm, where whole 0.040, and at the most 0.235 against 0.150. On the rest of the frame, outside its band, it strains more without the band: 0.0072% across its layers against 0.0044%, and 0.021% any way against 0.010%. But read whole, the frame's worst, 0.0083% across its layers, is in the band's own leads, which the bound leaves unread, and the block, with no band, reads 0.0037% against 0.0040% at a corner where the two meet, by the mesh there: so no part's strain is called a bound. 247 s.
    - **Straight-sided against curved,** on frames that mesh curved without trouble, every mesh made straight-sided by hand (`~/print-shop/proofs/ux-295/straight-vs-curved/`): the hills panel traced at 200 mm, held by its edge with 0.5 kg on its far border, reads 0.494% across its layers straight and 0.496% curved, 1.650% and 1.645% any way, and moves 21.416 and 21.417 mm, on the same 71,356 nodes: 0.4% apart at most, where its own coarser mesh reads 8.0% apart. A frame of straight lines reads the same to every figure.
    - **What reads today reads the same:** 22 `load` cases the tests run, on main at 359cc1a and on this, word for word but for their times. One figure, the sheared area round a peaked bore's bolt, reads 82.62 or 82.60 mm² from one run to the next on main as it is, and the same on this.

- **Two passes** (mqucifer/print-shop#301, `bench/load_parts.py`). Where the whole meets the container's memory, or where `--parts` asks, `load` reads it again in two passes, inside the container's limits as they are. An assembly that fits reads in one pass, as it always has, with no word of two.
  - **When:** `--parts`; or the one pass's solve met the container's memory, by the kernel's own count of what it stopped for memory (the reply's `two_passes.whole_met` says what it met).
