Only where two passes can't read it either is a part that names its `band` read as a lower bound, as above, and the lower bound's `why` says what stopped the two passes. **With `--parts` too** (mqucifer/print-shop#375): where two passes asked for meet the container's time or its memory, or no mesh of the whole reads, each part that names its band is read with it left out, as after the one pass's two passes, and its `why` begins `read in two passes, as --parts asks:`. With no one pass's try to time it by, two passes keep back 1.25 times a one pass's 600 s for the band's read (`ONE_PASS`), and the band's clock takes its read to take 600 s at the most. Any other refusal of two passes, as a part whose loads don't balance, is said as it is, and a whole that reads is read whole. **Memory while meshing:** gmsh, for the joined parts, is started as CalculiX is, the first the kernel stops where the container runs out (`cae.gmsh(first=True)`): FreeCAD starts it through Qt, which can't be told so, so it's run as FreeCAD's own run does, the same program and input, and its mesh read back the same way. Where it made no mesh and the cgroup's `oom_kill` count rose while it ran, the whole is unread for the container's memory, never meshed again straight-sided, which takes as much, and two passes or the band's read follow; before, the kernel stopped `load` itself there and the container went with it. **Its files on disk** (mqucifer/print-shop#376): a design run's assembly was still killed at its straight-sided mesh's start. `/tmp` is the container's memory, and gmsh's input, the joined parts' shape, and its mesh were written there: a file in it is no process's, so the kernel stopped the largest process, `load`. Now gmsh's files and CalculiX's for the joined parts are written on /work's disk, each mesh's and solve's in a folder of its own in the dot-folder `.bench-scratch` (`cae.scratch`), so a run's tools pass it by. Each is removed as it's read, and every one the command made at its end, whatever stopped it but a kill. A folder a killed command left, named `gmsh-` or `load-` and older than two hours, past any command's limit, is removed at the next `load`'s or `optimise`'s start (`optimise` solves with `load`'s model, and clears its own the same way); nothing else there is touched. Until then a design round never sees it: its walk of the working folder leaves `.bench-scratch` out (`rounds.paths.BENCH_SCRATCH`), so it's never the run's geometry, state, texts or size, and no copy of the folder, clean or not, takes it. Each file is held to the bench's 2 GB `fsize`, as every file a command writes is. And the mesh says each part of it as a step of its own (writing gmsh's input, gmsh meshing, and reading its mesh back), so a trace says which held what. **`load`'s own process:** with its files on disk, the same assembly's gmsh got through, and the kill came in `load` itself, as it read the mesh back and set it up. A failed allocation in FreeCAD's own code aborts the process, never a Python error (tried in the image under a lowered data limit: the mesh reader, the nodes on a face and the elements' nodes each ended it; only reading the nodes into Python raised `MemoryError`), so a limit on `load`'s process would only make the abort certain. Instead, the two big steps after gmsh are held against the container's room before they're begun (`cae.room`: its limit, less what it holds, plus the file pages the kernel drops first, less 64 MiB). Reading the mesh back takes 2.37 to 2.40 times its file, measured on three meshes of 8 to 207 MB. Setting it up takes 374 to 430 bytes a node, and in two passes, past the nodes, 735 an element at its peak, each element's faces and neighbours (646,197 elements). **Where a lower bound can follow,** a part naming its band and this not the band's own read, each need is held a half above the most measured (3.6 times the file, 650 bytes a node, 1,110 an element; `cae.READ_BACK_HIGH`, `load.SET_UP_NODE_HIGH`, `SET_UP_FACES_HIGH`), and the file pages aren't counted on as room. That's the operator's ruling on a design run's assembly, whose set-up a tenth under the least let through, and was killed: a step refused that would just have fitted costs a lower bound, said, and the reply says it "may not fit", while one let through that doesn't fit costs the container. **Anywhere else,** where the try is all there is, each is held a tenth under the least (2.3 times the file, 335 bytes a node, 660 an element), so a step is refused only where even that won't fit. Setting the mesh up is a step of its own, giving its elements and nodes. **Writing CalculiX's input and reading its results back** are held the same way, as the same assembly, its set-up checked, was then killed writing its input: FreeCAD's writer, with the loads put in, took 3,100 to 3,130 bytes a node, or 4,360 an element, at its peak on two meshes of 356,359 and 531,542 elements that solved, and its reader of the results 3,390 to 3,520 a node, or 4,720 to 4,940 an element (`load.WRITE_NODE` and the rest). Both meshes had the same nodes to an element, so each need is taken both ways: where a lower bound can follow, a half above the more; anywhere else, a tenth under the less. Each is a step of its own, with the mesh's counts. CalculiX itself is first to go, and where it meets the memory, two passes and a glass band's own read, which nothing follows, solve it again iteratively and corrected, so it isn't held beforehand; the reply says which solver read each mesh (`lower_bound.solvers` for the band's), and where every try meets the memory, how long each ran. Planted on the plain frame, the band's read by the iterative solver, its first direct solve made to meet the memory, reads the frame's movement within 1e-4 of the direct solver's and each strain within 1e-3. Where the band's own read can't be read, the reply says the lower bound couldn't be read either, why the whole couldn't, and what the band's read met; and where the bands are named, it never says to name them. Where one won't, the whole is unread for memory, saying what it needs and what's left, and two passes or the band's read follow. A `MemoryError` in a step is answered the same way only where the room is short of it: what numpy asked for, where it says, is within the container's limit and past what's left, or, where it doesn't say, nothing's left. Anything else, as an array no container could hold, is a plain error. **Not covered:** any other allocation in `load`'s own process past the room, as the readings after the solve, the nodes on each face that meets another and each part's balance, still ends the command, and the host says where. Those are each a few hundred MB on the meshes measured, against the writer's and the reader's GB. Where a part names its band, two passes are given the container's time less 1.25 times what the one pass's try took (`RESERVE`), held back for the band's read, which meshes less than that try did (on the joined glass frame it took 0.41 of it on CI's amd64 runner, 70.9 s of 173.5, and 0.37 on the Mac): where they'd run past that, they stop and the band is read, so a lower bound is never lost to two passes that can't end in time. The band's read has its own clock, and where it runs out it's `NO ANSWER`, saying so. `lower_bound.tried_s` and `band_s` give both times. With no band, two passes that run out of time are `NO ANSWER`, saying where. The traced glass frame bolted to a block is one: with its band in, gmsh gives the block no element of its own, which is now refused, saying so, where before it would have failed reading the block (`the mesh gives block no element of its own`).
  - **Pass 1, the whole:** every part, joint and bolt, solved as one pass solves them, each joint's faces tied all over, but each bolt's bore stood in by a square one (`squared`): the same axis and length, each side the bore's width and 0.1 mm more, so it holds the round one, set by the axis alone, so where a bore passes from one part into the next the two squares are one. Only a bore is: a round face on a bolt's axis that's a hole, the part's outside toward the axis, from the bolt's width to 1.5 times it across. A boss or collar round a bolt, or a counterbore wider than that, is left round, as one pass meshes it, and the reply counts them: cut away, a boss would make its joint softer and its bolt's readings lower. Its flat sides are meshed at `--mesh`, not 12 elements a turn. No bolt is put in the bore in either pass: one pass ties the faces and leaves the bore empty, and a connector stiffer than that would move the loaded places less. It gives each joint's force and moment, which every joint's reading takes as one pass's (each bolt's pull, the washers, the nut, the plastic round each hole solved by itself, a dovetail's faces); each loaded place's movement; and what each part's neighbours put on it, face by face.
  - **Pass 2, each part on its own,** at the mesh one pass reads it at, its bores meshed round by their curvature. Its loads are its own: the places `--at` names on it, or its own weight when lifted. What its neighbours put on it in pass 1 is put on the faces it meets them by: on each triangle, the push per mm² pass 1 found on the nearest of its own, its total then made pass 1's force and moment exactly. Each of pass 2's meshes takes pass 1's at the same size, and each triangle takes pass 1's push at four points across it, averaged. What evening out moves is held to 20% of what's put on (`EVENED`; on either mesh it moved 14.5% at the most on the stool, 10.4% on its control and 10.9% on the table), or nothing of the part is read; and a part that meets others but was given nothing by them isn't read either, as what holds it would balance whatever's put on it. It's held by the planes that hold it in the assembly; a part held by nothing but its joints is pinned at three nodes against moving as a body, and nothing more. It gives each part's worst pull across its layers and its largest stretch, against its material's limits, on its mesh and the coarser one.
  - **Memory:** where CalculiX's direct solver meets the container's memory in either pass, that mesh is solved by its iterative solver and corrected, each time for what's left unbalanced at each node, until that's under 1e-7 of the load. The reply names each solve that was. On the stool, the same solve forced that way reads every part's strain within 2.7e-4 of the direct solver on the same mesh, the movement within 3.4e-7, and each joint's force and moment within 2.6e-3, inside the 5% the two meshes are held to. Every other solve is the direct solver's.
  - **Where it was, and what it holds between steps** (mqucifer/print-shop#336). A design run's whole assembly, 37 parts, 36 through joints and 4 latched dovetails on parts turned 45°, was killed at the 3 GiB in every case, even at `--mesh 30`, and the reply was only that it was killed. Now `load` says each step as it begins, and the container's memory every 5 s, on stderr (`bench/progress.py`): the cgroup's current and peak, its processes' own and `/tmp`'s, and what `load`'s own process has in use and has freed but kept. The host and a design round's `Box` keep the last of each and don't pass them on, and a killed command's reply says where it was and the most the container had held by then: `verdict: NO ANSWER: it was killed, most likely at the 3g memory limit. Where it was: the second pass's part post-upper0 on its own, at 30 mm, from 1210.4 s in. The container's memory: at most 2.90 GiB of its 3g by 1215.4 s in, when it last said, 2.50 GiB then, 0.50 GiB of it files in /tmp` (as `tests/test_progress.py` has it). `scripts/load_trace.py` runs a `load` that way and prints only steps, seconds and memory, each part and joint by its number and every figure in a step's words as `#`; `scripts/load_ways_probe.py` does the same for the bolts' ways. On the real assembly they found:
    - **FreeCAD caches what it reads of each face on the shape**, and the parts as placed are kept for the whole read: reading their faces once, as the bolts' ways do, took 1.6 GiB (parts of 1,600 to 2,565 faces each, mostly the leading's cylinders), held to the end. Each step's end now lets the cache of the shapes `load` keeps go (`clearCache`); it's read again as it's asked.
    - **The C library keeps what the process frees**, which gmsh and CalculiX, processes of their own, can't use: joining the synthetic table's parts with their bores squared freed 0.87 GiB it went on holding. It's given back to the kernel (`malloc_trim`) as each step ends and before each gmsh mesh and CalculiX solve. What it can't give back, in pieces among what's still in use, it still holds.
    - **Each join took 1,000 to 1,200 s** of the hour, and two passes join the parts as placed twice (the way in, the second pass) and with their bores squared twice (the mesh, the coarser one). Each set is now fused once a read. And the faces two parts share are found by their hash, each confirmed by `isSame` as before, not by asking every face of each part of every face of each other, some 250 million asks there. Six plates of 2,310 faces each: 590 s a join before, 340 s and then 9 s now.
    - On a synthetic of the same shape, `large/table.json`'s 24 parts and 56 bolts with `turned/eighth.json`'s four latched posts beside them, 4 kg `--toward +z` on the top and the posts, `--parts`: `DISAGREE` before and after, every reason and every line the same, each figure within the spread two runs of the same code already give (the bolts' own readings round each hole, up to 0.24% of a strain of 0.001%); its peak 2.20 GiB before, 1.40 GiB now. No check is changed.
    - **The real table, then, read under 6g:** 682 s, never killed, where before it was killed after 2,583 s; its joins 70 to 111 s each. Its peak was meshing pass 1's whole, whose leading's thousands of small round faces gmsh sizes by their curvature, not by `--mesh`: 3.32 GiB with the elements' sides curved, `load`'s own process 1.0 GiB of it, and 3.66 GiB meshing it again straight-sided. The straight try ran inside the curved one's `except`, whose traceback held the curved try's mesh: it's let go first now, in pass 1's whole and in each part on its own. So `load --parts`, as a design run asks for its whole assembly, is given 4g (`host.TWO_PASSES_MEMORY`), as it's given the hour: 0.34 GiB over the most measured, 3.66 GiB, with the curved try's mesh still held, and 0.68 GiB over the curved try's own. Read so, under 4g, it ran to the end in 618 s, not killed: its peak 3.36 GiB in the curved mesh, and the straight-sided one's 2.82 GiB. Docker here has about 12.5 GB and at most two design runs read at once, 8 GB of it. A plain `load`, and every other command, keeps the 3 GiB, so what each proves of it stands. That table's whole is then `NO ANSWER` for its own reason, gmsh's boundary mesh of it, which is not this one's.
  - **An hour** (mqucifer/print-shop#325, the Sponsor's "Yes an hour", 2026-11-25). Two passes run under 3,600 s, as `optimise` does, where every other `load` keeps 600 s: the table of 24 parts and 56 bolts below takes about 1,750 s, 1,047 s of it the bolts' own readings, the same as in one pass, so 600 s could never read it. With `--parts` the host gives the hour from the start. Without it the host can't know before the run whether the whole will fit, or how long it will take, so it gives `load` 600 s and offers it the hour (`BENCH_TIMEOUT_TWO_PASSES`); `load` takes it up only as two passes start, by one line on its stderr, which the host watches for and doesn't pass on (`host.TAKEN`). **The hour is taken up** where the one pass can't read the assembly inside the container's limits, and two passes can:
    - where the whole met the container's memory (mqucifer/print-shop#325);
    - where an assembly's one pass wouldn't end inside its 600 s (mqucifer/print-shop#331): a design run's furniture assembly fitted the memory but ran past 600 s, round after round, and was stopped there, with nothing to say `--parts` would be given the hour. Now an assembly of more than one part, offered the hour, runs its one pass on a clock (`load._OnePass`). Like two passes' clock, it asks before each step it can foresee and never interrupts one, but for CalculiX, a process of its own:
      - before a reading round a bolt, whether one as long as the longest so far would end inside what's left;
      - before the whole's coarser mesh, whether what the mesh's whole took before its solve and after it (meshing it, and reading the solve), which the coarser's is no longer than, would; gmsh isn't stopped mid-mesh;
      - CalculiX's solve of the whole is given what's left, less what the whole's reading after its solve took, and is stopped there.

      Each way it takes up the hour at once, before anything is tidied, goes on to two passes without being asked, and the reply says why: `read in two passes (the one pass wouldn't end inside the container's 600 s: it had run 412 s, before the reading round …)`, the same in `two_passes.one_pass_late`. The hour counts from the command's start, so the one pass's try is in it. Two passes' clock takes the one pass's whole as what a whole takes at the most: its time, or, where its solve was stopped, what it had taken by then.
    - **What's left** is the limit less 2 s (`load.ONE_PASS_MARGIN`), the hand-off to the host: from the stop to the host reading `TAKEN`, which counts the container's start after the host's clock began. Measured on the planted stool in the cae image, stopped in CalculiX's solve at a limit given (`~/print-shop/proofs/ux-331/handoff.py`): 0.16 to 0.28 s over nine runs, so 2 s is about seven times that. **The trade-off:** a one pass that would have ended in its last 2 s goes to two passes, and so does one whose next bolt, or coarser mesh, the clock's estimate says wouldn't end in time though it might have. Two passes can then refuse on what they need of the geometry (a bore too near an edge, a part given more than `EVENED` moved, a part on its own that doesn't balance), and answer `NO ANSWER` where one pass would have given a verdict; each says why. A hand-off that ever takes longer than 2 s is stopped by the host, and the reply names `--parts`, below.

    Until then the host stops it at 600 s, as before, so a one-pass read that ends inside it, a single part, or a glass band read after a whole that failed for another reason, never runs past 600 s, and reads as it did. Past what the one pass's clock foresees, as a mesh that runs past the limit, the host stops it at 600 s; where the one pass said first that two passes could read its assembly (`host.ABLE`, a line the host doesn't pass on either), the reply says what may: `NO ANSWER: it ran past the 600 s limit and was stopped, in one pass. Two passes may read this assembly, and are given the hour, 3600 s: run it again with --parts, which asks for them from the start`. The host reads whether it was such a one pass at the moment it stops it, so `TAKEN` landing as it's stopped changes nothing. A single part's, and two passes' that ran out of the hour, are as before: two passes say where they stopped. A script, and a caller that gives its own limit, are offered nothing. A design round's calls go the same way through `rounds/box.py`: its `load` in two passes may take up to an hour of one call, where the round's own `command_seconds` holds every other (`docs/rounds.md`). Once taken up, the hour is the container's limit for the rest of the command: the clock below, what's kept for a glass band's lower bound (which comes out of the hour, and stays 1.25 times the one pass's try), and the reply's `ran:` line. Memory, CPUs and the rest of the container are as for every command.
  - **Time:** the host gives the container its limit (`BENCH_TIMEOUT`), and two passes stop before a solve, or a reading round a bolt, that wouldn't end inside it, at the longest of its kind so far: a whole as long as the one pass's try took, where there was one, and a part on its own as long as pass 1's whole, until one's been timed. They say which pass they were in and what was read, and nothing is judged on part of an assembly. They're asked before each step, never interrupted in one: a signal while FreeCAD meshes ends the process with nothing said. Past what the checks foresee, the host stops the command at its limit and says so.
  - **The reply** says `read in two passes`, and why, then `pass 1, the whole:` with its meshes, elements, nodes, seconds and the container's memory by its end (which counts the one pass's try, where there was one), and `pass 2, each part on its own:` the same. Each part's line starts `pass 2, on its own:`; each joint's, each loaded place's and the movement's, `pass 1, the whole:`. `two_passes` holds both passes' figures.
  - **Never weaker.** How far each place moves is taken on from pass 1's two meshes to a finer one, the larger of the two and of where they point to (Richardson's, at an error in proportion to the mesh's size squared), as a coarser mesh reads a part stiffer: so it's no less than one pass, whose bores' small elements make its mesh finer round them. `loads.*.mesh_moves_mm` is the mesh's own. A part's strain is read on its own mesh, which differs from its share of one pass's at the faces it meets others by, so a strain within 5% below its limit (`load_parts.BAND`) is `DISAGREE`, never `OK`: read 5% low, it would be at its limit or over. Each joint's readings take pass 1's forces, so each joint's nearest figure to its limit (its `nearest_share`, or a bolted joint's worst bolt over what it holds) is held the same 5% below it (`JOINT_BAND`); on the stool each joint's force in two passes is within 0.40% of one pass's. A `touching` joint's readings are where its push falls and which way it's pulled, not shares of a limit, and aren't banded. Both bands rest on the stool alone, the one assembly small enough to read both ways here.
  - **Its own second checks:** pass 1 at two meshes, each joint's force on the coarser within 5% of the finer (`AGREE`), or of 0.1% of the load where that's more, or the reply names it and it's `DISAGREE`. 5% is 250 times `load`'s own spread from run to run, about 2e-4 of a figure (mqucifer/print-shop#302), and the same share a part is held below its limit. That spread is most likely gmsh's high-order optimisation's, not CalculiX's: on an identical mesh, every CalculiX solve was bit-identical in mqucifer/print-shop#348's probes. And each part on its own: what holds it carries what's put on it, within 1%, or its pins carry next to nothing, or nothing is read.
  - **Planted** (`large/`; every command and reply in `~/print-shop/proofs/ux-301/`):
    - **The stool both ways** (`large/stool.json`, pushed +x at its top by 5 kg): `OK` both ways. Two passes read each part's worst strain across its layers 0.978 to 1.000 times one pass's, and any way 0.982 to 1.130; the loaded place moves 0.1289 mm, one pass 0.1284 (pass 1's mesh alone 0.1282, 0.2% less, and its coarser 0.1273: what taking it on to a finer mesh is for). Each joint's force within 0.4% of one pass's, each worst bolt's pull 1.002 to 1.013 times. Pass 1 squares 8 bores and is 27,568 elements; pass 2's largest part is 16,166. 75 s, against one pass's 63.
    - **The control** (`large/stool-one-out.json`): `FAIL` both ways, naming `top+leg2`, which tips open, its push 52.89 mm outside the faces that meet in one pass and 53.29 in two.
    - **The table of 24 parts and 56 bolts** (`large/table.json`, 20 kg on four places): its one pass meets the 3 GiB, and the kernel stops CalculiX, not `load`, so two passes start. Pass 1 squares 112 bores in 18 s and is 45,916 elements and 86,850 nodes at 10.459 mm (25,677 at 15.688), which CalculiX's direct solver holds, 1.92 GiB at the most: 82 s for both meshes. Pass 2's largest part is tile0, 35,415 elements; its 48 solves take 347 s. Then the bolts' own readings, unchanged from one pass, solve the plastic round each of the 112 holes at two meshes: 224 solves, 1,047 s. So the table can't be read inside the container's 600 s: at 600 s two passes stop themselves before the step their clock says would run past it (after 564 s on the Mac, before pass 2's tile3 on its coarser mesh; 411 s on CI's runner, a part not yet timed being estimated by pass 1's whole), and the reply is `NO ANSWER`, saying so and what was read. With the limit lifted, in a proof run only, it reads to the end in 1,748 s, one pass's try and all (two passes alone 1,498 s and 2.09 GiB at the most): `FAIL`, each leg's two pieces, `touching` end to end, tipping open, their push 2.25 to 2.84 mm outside the faces that meet. The Sponsor gave two passes an hour (mqucifer/print-shop#325, above): under it, as `load` runs it from the bench, it reads to the end: `FAIL`, as with the limit lifted, in 1,981 s of its 3,600 s (`ran:`), pass 1 128 s and pass 2 437 s, on the Mac with other builders' work beside it (`~/print-shop/proofs/ux-325/`). Its memory.peak is the 3 GiB, as it counts the one pass's try; two passes alone held 2.09 GiB at the most in mqucifer/print-shop#301's stage probe. The 600 s reading is kept as a test, under a limit given, and the hour's as a proof run by hand (`-m proof`), as it's longer than CI's job.

### A part as printed

(mqucifer/print-shop#337, `bench/as_printed.py` and `bench/infill.py`.) `load` read every part as solid, so the print side could only trade time against grams. A part the assembly file gives a `print` is read as it's printed: a solid skin, its walls and its top and bottom shells as thick as its settings make them, round an infill core whose stiffness and strain are set by its pattern, its density and the way its lines run. A part without one reads solid, as before, and the solid read stays the careful one. The format is the print side's too (`bench/printed.py`, agreed with mqucifer/print-shop#338 through the operator), so what's sliced and what's read are one thing:

```json
{"name": "leg", "file": "leg.step", "layers": "z",
 "print": {"walls": 2, "line_width": 0.45, "top": 5, "bottom": 3, "infill": 20,
           "pattern": "grid", "infill_angle": 0, "layer_height": 0.2, "up": "+z",
           "regions": [{"name": "root", "box": [[0, 0, 0], [30, 20, 10]], "infill": 60, "walls": 4,
                        "why": "denser where it's bent most"}]}}
```

- **Every setting is given at the part's own level.** `load` runs with no profile and guesses none: a part without any of `walls`, `line_width`, `top`, `bottom`, `infill`, `pattern`, `infill_angle` and `layer_height` is `NO ANSWER`, naming what's missing (the operator's ruling, 2026-11-26). The print side fills them from the Orca profile it slices with.
- **`up` is the way the part stands as it prints**: its own layers axis with its sign, `+z` or `-z` for layers along z. Its top shells are under its faces that look that way, and its bottom shells over those that look the other. The assembly's `layers` has no sign, and the print side poses a part upside down where that's better (`ingest.orientation.AXIS_ALIGNED` has a turn of 180° about x), so a part whose top and bottom shells differ and that has no `up` is `NO ANSWER`, naming it. The print side writes it from the pose it slices with (`slicer.regions.Pose.up`, in `RegionsSlice.for_load`). Read the other way up, 5 top and 3 bottom layers put 1.0 mm on the face Orca prints at 0.6, and where that face is pulled, it reads stronger than printed. A wall, top or bottom of 0 is `NO ANSWER` too: its core would reach the outside, which isn't read yet.
- **`infill_angle` is in the part's own axes**: with its layers along its own axis a, degrees from its own axis a+1 toward a+2 (`printed.own_way`), so `load` needs nothing of the pose. The print side turns it onto the plate by the pose it slices with.
- **The skin and the core, as solids.** The core is whatever of the part is at least its walls from its outline in its own layer's plane, its top shells (`top` × `layer_height`) below each face that looks `up`, and its bottom shells over each that looks the other way. Its walls are as many beads' spacing (`as_printed.wall_thickness`), by Orca 2.4.2's own flow (`src/libslic3r/Flow.cpp`): a bead's spacing is "`auto out = width - height * float(1. - 0.25 * PI);`" (line 202), and its plastic per mm is its height times that, "`float(m_height * (m_width - m_height * (1. - 0.25 * PI)))`" (line 225). Its loops are laid that far apart (`PerimeterGenerator.cpp:1303`, "`coord_t distance = (i == 1) ? ext_perimeter_spacing2 : perimeter_spacing;`"), and the infill is cut half of one inside the last ("`perimeter_spacing / 2`", line 1638). So 2 walls at 0.45 mm lines and 0.2 mm layers are 0.814 mm of plastic, not 0.9, and 4 are 1.628. Orca's overlap of the infill onto the walls (`infill_wall_overlap`) adds infill there, not wall. Arachne, Orca's other wall generator, starts its beads at the same spacing ("`coord_t bead_width_0 = ext_perimeter_spacing;`", line 2129), and varies them only where whole beads don't fit. The store's profile has `"wall_generator": "classic"`. The order walls are laid in (`wall_sequence`) doesn't change how thick they are. Where a slice exists, mqucifer/print-shop#341's reader measures the wall band's width, the part's and each region's with walls of its own, as a second reading (`slicer.regions.band`, mqucifer/print-shop#339): on the bar of `tests/test_slice_regions.py` it lies 0.07 to 0.09 mm past this, a bead's width past its spacing, 0.901 mm for 2 walls and 1.697 for 4. Its shells are the part's common with 2 copies of itself, its top shells down and its bottom shells up. Its walls (mqucifer/print-shop#356, `as_printed.cut_core`) are OCCT's offset of the part stretched ten times along its layers, and the stretch undone: an ellipsoid's offset, its walls across the layers' plane and a tenth of them along it, round at a concave edge. Where OCCT can't offset a part, they're its common with 16 copies of itself moved its walls round the layers' plane. That common is stepped at each concave edge, a face of each copy a hundredth to three tenths of a millimetre across, and on a curved part those steps by its B-spline faces turned gmsh's elements inside out, with their sides curved and straight. So where two flat faces meet concave, the steps are cut out to one mitred corner, the two moved faces' line (`as_printed.mitres`). Each errs a little, and which way matters: a core too small, its skin too thick, reads the part stronger and stiffer than printed, the unsafe side. The offset errs that way, by at most about 1.4% of the walls on a face they set, and so does the mitre, a square for a quarter disc at a concave corner, 21% of the walls' square at a right angle; the 16 copies err the other way, a wall between two of them read at least cos 11.25°, 98%, as thick as it is. Each cut is held to the core each layer has as Orca slices it (`as_printed.checked`): the part's section, each face of it offset in by its walls on its own with OCCT's 2D offset, so a face thinner than its walls is gone and the rest kept, and cut by its sections its shells above and below. That's read at 7 of its layers' planes spread over its height and at its own features' heights: just off each of its faces in its layers' plane, and at each concave edge's and cylinder's middle. It's compared as shapes, so a miss isn't made up by an excess beside it: the core may be short of a layer's by no more than 2% of the walls along the outline (`SHORT`), the unsafe side, and over it by no more than 5% (`AGREE`), with 80% of the planes, and at least 3, read. It may leave no face of the core narrower than a bead's width by a curved face (`as_printed.slivers`), unless OCCT can merge it into the faces round it, after which it's held to the part's shells and checked again. Where neither way holds, each is tried again on the part with its own faces under a bead merged first, its core kept within the part's own shells and held to its own layers; and where none holds, `load` says so, NO ANSWER, "the skin couldn't be cut round the part P's curved faces", and why each way didn't, and never shows CalculiX's inside-out elements for a geometry the bench made. The reply says how each part's walls were cut and how far the cut was from its layers (`printed.P[k].cut`, `cut_planes`, `cut_planes_read`, `cut_short_walls_pct`, `cut_over_walls_pct`). `scripts/as_printed_probe.py` says, of an assembly of one's own, where its cut leaves slivers and whether its mesh turns them inside out, in counts and kinds of face only. The cores are fused into the joined parts, so the mesh has a face where each skin meets its core: every element is the skin's or a core's, never half of each. That matters. A 5 mm element can't tell a 0.9 mm wall from the infill behind it: with the skin smeared over the elements it lies in, a bar 10 mm deep with a 0.84 mm skin and a core a fifth as stiff bends 19 to 38% too easily by its section's own arithmetic, at a 2.5 and a 5 mm mesh. Fused, a bar with 2 walls, 5 and 3 shells and a core a tenth as stiff met the sandwich beam formula to 0.6% at the usual 5 mm mesh, 3,000 elements: gmsh meshes the thin skin with flat elements, and they hold. Each part's solid is then its skin, which has every face the part has: the faces it meets other parts by, is held by and is loaded on.
- **Regions** cut the part into cells, each with the settings every region round it gives: a `layers` range's first, then each volume's, a `box`, a `cylinder` or a `mesh`, over it in order, the later over the earlier, as Orca 2.4.2 applies them (the print side's reading of its slices, mqucifer/print-shop#338). Each cell's walls follow the part's own outline and holes, as many as its settings give. A volume with walls of its own is an island: one ring of wall each side of its shape's faces where they cut the part, besides its own count round the outline and holes inside it. A `layers` range adds no walls. Every cell's shells are from the part's own faces. Each cell's core is its own material.
- **Which pattern lays which lines** is Orca 2.4.2's own code, at its tag `v2.4.2` (`src/libslic3r/Fill/`, read 2026-11-26):
  - every pattern's default turns 90° each layer: `FillBase.hpp:211`, "`virtual float _layer_angle(size_t idx) const { return fixed_angle ? 0.f : (idx & 1) ? float(M_PI/2.) : 0.f; }`". Rectilinear, zig zag (`FillZigZag`, which keeps it) and line keep it: one set of parallel lines in each layer, half along `infill_angle` and half square to it, and a line meets the next layer's only where they cross, so no wall builds up through the layers. Crosshatch turns every few layers and is read as rectilinear;
  - grid, triangles and aligned rectilinear don't turn: `FillRectilinear.hpp:46` and `:77`, "`return 0.f;`", under "The grid fill will keep the angle constant between the layers". Grid lays both directions in every layer, "`{ { 0.f, 0.f }, { float(M_PI / 2.), 0.f } }`", its density shared between them, "`params.density /= double(sweep_params.size());`" (`FillRectilinear.cpp:3000`), and triangles three, "`{ { 0.f, 0.f }, { float(M_PI / 3.), 0.f }, { float(2. * M_PI / 3.), 0. } }`". So each set of lines lies on the same lines each layer and builds a wall through the layers;
  - a set of lines takes ρ of the area across its layer: "`coord_t line_spacing = coord_t(scale_(this->spacing) / params.density);`" (`FillRectilinear.cpp:2768`).
  - gyroid turns its wave with the height and isn't lines: it's read as bending alone, the same every way.
- **The core's stiffness by its lines** (`infill.lines_stiffness`): a set of lines holding a share s of the core, at density ρ, is stiff as s·ρ·E along its run, Ashby's stretch-dominated struts (M. F. Ashby, "The properties of foams and lattices", *Phil. Trans. R. Soc. A* 364:15–30, 2006, eq. 5.1, "Ẽ/Es ≈ (1/3)(ρ̃/ρs)", there for struts every way, here with each set's own share); a wall is the same plastic in plane stress in the plane of its lines and the layers. Every pattern bends between its lines besides, as ρ²·E every way: his eq. 3.3, bending-dominated, "Ẽ/Es ∝ (ρ̃/ρs)²", where "we expect the constant of proportionality to be close to unity". It's a full anisotropic stiffness, turned into the assembly's axes by the part's own turns and `infill_angle`. FreeCAD writes its one material for every element, as for any assembly; each core's elements, found by the faces where it meets its skin, are then a set of their own in the CalculiX input, with the core's constants as `*ELASTIC, TYPE=ANISO`. (FreeCAD's own sets, one material to several solids, left 225 of 3,307 elements of the planted halves out of every one.) Each element's own stiffness is taken in the nodes' balance that a joint's forces are read from (`element_forces`), and in the check that the elements balance as CalculiX solved them, so both hold as they do for one material. A printed part's skin is thin, flat elements, stiff enough that the .frd's six figures of each movement leave up to 1.2 N at a node of the planted halves, and their joint read 3.9% over the weight. So where any part is printed, the elements' forces are read from the seven figures CalculiX prints (`*NODE PRINT`), as the balance check already is: the joint is then within 0.3% of statics, 0.22% on the planted halves. A part read solid is read as it was.
- **Published tests set its figures** (`infill.TESTS`), and the least share any of them reached is the one taken, each pattern naming the test that set it (`infill.set_by`). The cleanest open tests of a core alone are **Ben hadj Hassine, Chatti, Louhichi and Seibi, "Experimental Study of the Tensile Behavior of Structures Obtained by FDM 3D Printing Process", *Polymers* 16(11):1562, 2024, doi:10.3390/polym16111562** (read as full text from Europe PMC, PMC11174567): PLA, its Table 1 "Top and bottom solid layer 0 | Shell number 0", "Extrusion width 0.4 mm", ASTM D638 type 1 with an extensometer, each printed three times; its Table 2's levels "Honeycomb Gyroid Grid Triangles", "40 50 60" percent and cell orientation "0 45 90" degrees; and its Table 3, "Yield Strength (MPa)" and "Elastic Modulus (MPa)" for each, such as grid at 40% and 0°, "1 −1 −1 | 1.84 ± 0.09 | 344.5 ± 7.5", and at 45°, "1 −1 0 | 1.28 ± 0.04 | 78.0 ± 2". Its plastic: Polymaker PolyTerra PLA, "tensile strength of 20.9 MPa, tensile modulus of 1882 Mpa". Each figure is taken as a share of that plastic's, as Gibson and Ashby take a lattice's, its stiffness over its solid's by its density, and put on PETG's own.
  - **Its stiffness:** against the tests the lines read stiffer, by up to 4.6 times: a coupon with no walls has every line cut open at its sides. So each pattern's core is held to the least share of its lines' own stiffness any test of it reached, at the test's density and angle to its lines (`infill.scale`), and names the test that set it: grid 0.218, Ben hadj Hassine's 40% at 45° (78.0 MPa against its lines' 357.3); gyroid 0.370, its 40% at 45°; and triangles 0.396, its 50% at 0°. It's never read stiffer than a test of it.
  - **Its strain:** each test's yield over its modulus, as a share of its plastic's 20.9 MPa over 1882, at the least (`infill.strain_share`): grid 0.481 (40% at 0°, 1.84 over 344.5 is 0.534%, against 1.11%), gyroid 0.553, triangles 0.323. A core takes that share of PETG's own (no other test sets a lower one): in the layers' plane, of Prusament PETG's "Tensile Yield Strength [MPa] 47 ± 2" over its 1.5 GPa, 3.13%, as the tests' are figures of yield over modulus too; across the layers, of the 1% the skin takes. So a grid core is held to 1.51% any way and 0.48% across its layers, where the skin takes 5.1% and 1%.
  - Rectilinear, zig zag, line, crosshatch and aligned rectilinear have no test of a core alone here, and take grid's: the same straight lines, crossed. Honeycomb, cubic and the rest aren't read yet, and are `NO ANSWER`, naming those that are.
  - **A second test of a core alone, in PETG**: Alabdli, Jalal, Alqarni, Basalah and Damiati, "Influence of Infill Density on Bacterial Colonization and Mechanical Performance of 3D-Printed PETG Scaffolds", *Polymers* 18:2299, 2026, doi:10.3390/polym18182299 (PMC13611749): its Fig. 3 shows "the internal grid structure", and its Table 1 gives "20 | 1.27 ± 0.15 | 18.5 ± 3.7", "40 | 2.79 ± 0.28 | 47.9 ± 8.1", "60 | 5.72 ± 0.46 | 133.6 ± 18.7" and "100 | 12.50 ± 0.75 | 418.0 ± 41.8" (UTS and Young's modulus, MPa). Its strain is "from the crosshead displacement, corrected for machine compliance", its stress over "the original gross cross-sectional area", and its walls, shells and lines' angle aren't said. So each is read as a share of its own 100%, against grid's lines the way they're stiffest: 0.27 at 20% and 40%, 0.42 at 60%. That's above Ben hadj Hassine's least, which stands. PETG and PLA part here by a fifth, and the careful one is kept (the operator's ruling on mqucifer/print-shop#342, 2026-11-26): this PETG test's walls aren't said, its own solid reads 418 MPa against Prusament's 1.5 GPa, and its strain is the crosshead's. A PETG test takes the place of a PLA one only where it's as well stated. And a careful reading that the coupons (mqucifer/print-shop#340) can raise is better than one 24% stiffer that they might have to lower. Its strength over its modulus is above its solid's at every density, so it sets no strain.
  - **Tests of a skin round a core are upper bounds only** (`infill.BOUNDS`, `infill.backed_out`). The core is backed out of a specimen's apparent modulus as its gross area's stiffness less its skin's, taken at the same study's own 100%. That reads the core too stiff, as the 100% coupon's solid infill lies at its pattern's angle, softer than walls running along the pull: Racz and Dudescu's ABS at 40% and Rismalia's PLA at 25% back out stiffer than ρ·E, which no lattice of that density can be. So they bound the core from above, and every core here is read under each:
    - Racz and Dudescu, "Numerical Investigation of the Infill Rate upon Mechanical Proprieties of 3D-Printed Materials", *Polymers* 14:2022, 2022, doi:10.3390/polym14102022 (PMC9147800): ABS, rectilinear "0° orientation of the pattern with respect to the tensile direction", "A uniaxial extensometer was used", "(4 mm thickness, 10 mm width; ... cross-section area of 40 mm2)", its Table 4's experiment "20% | 982.0", "40% | 1177.5", "100% | 1521.8" MPa, and its Table 2's material in the section "20% | 20.50" and "40% | 23.85" mm², so a core of (23.85 − 20.50)/0.2 = 16.75 mm². Its core backs out at 0.153 of its solid at 20% and 0.460 at 40%; a rectilinear core is read at 0.032 and 0.082 there;
    - Rismalia et al., "Infill pattern and density effects on the tensile properties of 3D printed PLA material", *J. Phys.: Conf. Ser.* 1402:044041, 2019, doi:10.1088/1742-6596/1402/4/044041: PLA, "Wall Thickness 1.2 mm", "Top/Bottom Thickness 0.6 mm", strain gauges, grid at "45 degrees angle with respect to the axial axis", "Grid 25 % 2.76 ± 0.38" GPa and "grid pattern with 100% infill density ... 3.6 GPa", on ASTM D638 Type I, 13 mm wide and its thickness not said: 3.2 mm, which reads the lower bound of the two it might be. It backs out at 0.542; a grid core is read at 0.017.
  - **So what's checked, and from where** (`infill.checked_from`): grid and the line patterns from 20%, by Alabdli's PETG and the bounds; gyroid and triangles from 40%, by Ben hadj Hassine only. Below that, the core is its lines' stiffness at the same share, which nothing checks, and the reply says so for each core. Past 60% it's `NO ANSWER`: leave out the part's `print`, and it's read solid. No test gives the cores' Poisson's ratio, so PETG's 0.38, `bend`'s, is the core's.
  - **What was searched and not used** (2026-11-26, MDPI, PMC through Europe PMC's full text, arXiv, IOP, journals' and universities' open pages): no open test has a PETG core alone with its walls and shells said. Mazlan et al., *Materials* 16:695, 2023 (PLA grid, 2 walls) printed its 20% three times, at "876.75", "719.99" and "566.78" MPa, a spread more than its core's whole share, the last below its own 0% with walls alone. Mazlan et al., *J. Mech. Eng.* SI 12, 2023 (PETG and ABS rectilinear, 2 mm walls) backs out at eight times ρ·E: its 10% and its 0% are from different runs, read by the crosshead. Hozdić et al., *Polymers* 18:654, 2026, and *ATM* 49(2), 2024 (PETG) read their 100% below their 30%. Vraitch, Prince and Souppez, ICAC 2023 (flexural gyroid) gives its moduli only on a plot, and its walls two ways. Gonabadi, Yadav and Bull 2020 (*IJAMT*, PLA, with DIC) couldn't be read. Bembenek et al. 2022 gives strength only; Gaweł 2020 and 2023 start at 50%; and the rest found give no 100% of their own or one density.
- **Second methods.** Every figure has its test and its lines' reading beside it (`infill.checks`, `infill.bounds`). A third source agrees on which way each pattern is strongest, in PETG: Abbas et al., "Advanced FFF of PEEK: Infill Strategies and Material Characteristics for Rapid Tooling", *Polymers* 15(21):4293, 2023, doi:10.3390/polym15214293 (PMC10650530), crushed 12.7 × 12.7 × 10 mm cubes "printed with one perimeter": across the layers, "For PETG samples, the grid infill recorded 22 MPa, and the triangle infill recorded 21.7 MPa, both surpassing the honeycomb infill at 19.8 MPa ... the gyroid infill at 14.2 MPa and the cubic infill at 12.8 MPa"; and in the layers' plane, "in the PETG samples, the cubic infill averaged 12.8 MPa, making it more stable than the gyroid infill at 10.6 MPa. The grid infill recorded the lowest values for both materials". That's the walls grid and triangles build through their layers, and gyroid the same every way, as the lines read it. On the part, the whole solve again on the coarser mesh judges each core's figures as it does the skin's, `DISAGREE` either side of a limit; and the sandwich beam's formula checks the solve on the planted bar (below).
- **Each core is read at its own elements** (`load.element_strains`): its strain at each of its elements' four integration points and ten nodes, from the nodes' movement to CalculiX's seven figures, through the same second-order shape the nodes' balance takes. CalculiX's strain at a node is the mean of every element there, and where a core meets its skin, 10 to 30 times as stiff, that dilutes the core's. An element's own at its node is never less than its share of that mean, and `averaged_across_layers_pct` beside it is CalculiX's, for comparison. Read so, next to the planted beam's clamped root, where its bottom shells are pushed along it, a stiffer core takes more of the clamp's hold: at 2 walls, its pull across its layers there is 0.166%, 0.187% and 0.192% at 20, 40 and 60%, all under its 0.48%, while what bends the beam falls.
- **Each part** that's printed (`parts.NAME`, with `printed`): its skin's figures as any part's, against the material's limits, `skin_nodes` beside its `nodes`, and `cores`, each with its `cell`, its pattern, density and angle, its figures (`tested_as`, `scale`, `strain_share`, `modulus_along_mpa`, `modulus_across_mpa`, `scale_set_by`, `checked_from_pct`, its pattern's least density any test or bound reaches, and `unchecked` below that: 20% for grid and the line patterns, 40% for gyroid and triangles), the most it's pulled across its layers against its `across_limit_pct`, and its largest stretch any way against its `stretch_limit_pct`, with how far that points out of the layers' plane and off its lines (`stretch_from_layers_deg`, `stretch_from_lines_deg`). `printed.NAME` has each cell's settings, its walls' and shells' thickness, its core's volume and its box in the part's own axes (`core_box`). The verdict's most across any part's layers is the skin's or a core's, named.
- **The reply says what it assumed:** each cell's walls and shells, its core's figures and their source, and that they're published tests of PLA taken as shares for PETG, not yet checked against a print from this printer, which the printed coupons will do (mqucifer/print-shop#340).
- **What it leaves out:**
  - what's solved again round a joint, the plastic round each through-bolt, a dovetail's neck and walls and its latch's tab, is read solid, and the reply says so for each printed part: stronger than printed, so read it with a margin;
  - two passes: a printed part is read in one pass only so far, and in two it's `NO ANSWER`, saying so;
  - Orca's own rules for small areas, such as filling one under `minimum_sparse_infill_area` solid, its gap fill, and a slot narrower than a shell between a point and the one it tests: each leaves the core a little larger, and the part a little softer and weaker than printed;
  - a printed part's weight: it hangs at its solid weight, more than its own;
  - any test of a core pulled across its layers: the strain share across them is the one in their plane.
- **Planted** (`tests/cae_cases/make_fixtures.py`, `printed`; `tests/test_load_printed_cae.py`): the beam of `load/one.json`, 120 x 20 x 10, held at its root, 1 kg at 115 mm, printed grid at 0° with 0.45 mm walls and 0.2 mm layers, 5 on top and 3 under, own +z up. Its tip moves, and the sandwich formula FL³/(3·EI) with the skin's and the core's own section, about their own neutral axis, says (the solid beam reads 6% under its own formula, 1.870 mm against 1.99, its load spread over a patch whose middle is nearer the root, and the printed ones read the same, so they're held to it within 8%):

  | Walls | 20% | 40% | 60% |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | 2 | 4.156 mm (formula 4.314) | 3.841 (4.024) | 3.515 (3.702) |
  | 4 | 3.699 (3.889) | 3.477 (3.678) | 3.234 (3.433) |

  It reads stiffer and stronger as both rise: each moves less, and what bends it, its skin's strain and its core's stretch along it, is a smaller share of its limit. As two halves bolted at its middle, each printed and so walled where they meet, the joint carries 9.785 N where statics says 9.807, 0.22% apart, and 522.0 N·mm where it says 522.0, and its bolt 104.4 N, as statics. 60% in its first 30 mm moves 3.795 mm against 20% throughout's 4.156; with 4 walls of its own besides, an island, 3.607. Rectilinear at 20% with its lines along it moves 4.228 mm, and turned 45°, so none run along it, 4.335. Printed own -z up, its core runs from 1.0 to 9.4 mm, where own +z up it runs from 0.6 to 9.0. A block 40 x 40 x 10 pulled up by 50 kg over its top reads its core across its layers at its own elements, no less than CalculiX's mean at its nodes and more than a column of its shells and core in series beside its walls says on average.

### `optimise`: where a part's load goes

(`bench/optimise.py`, mqucifer/print-shop#286.) **It's a guide for a round's design, never a part.** A part designed to pass `load` comes out solid wherever nothing told it otherwise, and most of that material carries little of the weight. Making it lighter at the same strength is a design round, with ribs, pockets and channel sections, and `load` proving it as before. This shows the round where the load goes, so it knows where material can come out. The Sponsor's decision, 2026-11-25: add it to the bench, "at minimum to compare, fusion or BESO", the bench's answer beside Fusion's shape optimisation for the same part. **Nothing in the rounds takes its mesh as a design:** the part a run makes is read by `load` and every other check, none of which changes, and every reply says so in its first line after the verdict. A need can't name it (`needs.REFERENCE`): one that does is refused, and isn't run, and it's in no kind's toolset.

- **What it reads, the way `load` does.** A part with a `print` is read solid here, as if every wall, shell and core were the plastic's own, and the reply says so: only `load` reads it as printed ([a part as printed](#a-part-as-printed)). An assembly file of one part, with no joints: its STEP file, where it sits, its material, its `layers` and its `held` ([the assembly file](#the-assembly-file)). A part joined to others is given alone, held where they hold it. `--weight` kg shared between the places `--at` names, each on the surface within 0.5 mm and spread over it within `--over` mm (10 unless given), pulling `--toward` (`-z` unless given). The mesh, the held faces and the load are `load`'s own: a place off the part, or a held plane with no face in it, is refused as `load` refuses it, `NO ANSWER`.
- **`--volume FRACTION`**, the share of the whole part to keep, more than 0 and less than 1: anything else is refused before the image starts, exit 3. It counts what's kept solid, and is refused where that alone is as much.
- **Kept solid:** the elements under each load and on each held face, always, as a load on material that's been taken away has nothing to push; each `--keep X0,Y0,Z0,X1,Y1,Z1`, the elements whose middle is in the box (a bolt's boss, a nut's pocket); and each `--keep-face X,Y,Z`, the part's faces through that point, an element deep (a face that shows, a seat). The reply counts each, and `kept_solid_kept` is each one's share still kept at the end: 1 unless a piece of it was dropped as carrying nothing.
- **The tool:** BESO, bi-directional evolutionary structural optimisation (fandaL/beso, LGPL-3.0), on the bench's own CalculiX 2.23, in the second image at one commit (below). Each iteration solves the part, takes away 5% of the kept material by least strain energy and adds 2% back by most, averaged over twice the mesh's size so it doesn't checkerboard, until the volume is reached and the mean strain energy settles, or its limit of iterations. An element over the material's tensile yield is never taken away. A void element is left in at a millionth of the stiffness (BESO's soft kill).
- **The mesh:** second-order tetrahedra, about 12,000 in the part unless `--mesh` says, as each of tens of iterations solves it all. Curved onto the part's faces, and where gmsh can't curve them without turning one inside out, straight-sided, as `load` meshes, and the reply says so (`straight`). The planted beam's 7,331 took 108 s of BESO in the container; `optimise` may run for an hour (`host.TIMEOUTS`), and BESO is stopped at 55 minutes and said.
- **What's kept** is each piece of BESO's answer with a load on it, joined face to face. A piece with none, an island, carries nothing and is dropped, and the reply says how many. Where a load's piece reaches no held face, or the kept material no longer reaches one of the held faces, it's `NO ANSWER`, saying where, and to give a larger `--volume` or `--keep` the material between.
- **What comes back:** `volume_kept`, against `--volume`; the peak stress **before**, the part solid, and **after**, the kept material solved alone, each the largest von Mises stress against the tensile yield, 45 MPa, and the largest pull across the layers against the interlayer adhesion, 14 MPa, each with where (`before`, `after`). Those are the two figures `assembly.PRESS` takes from Prusament PETG's datasheet, at the low end of each, here read as what they are. `load`'s own reading, by strain against the same sheet, is still what passes a part. The pictures, `above.png` and `below.png`, show the kept material from three-quarters above and below, in the part's box, with each load's place marked; the mesh is `kept.stl`, faceted at the elements' size. All three are under `optimised/PART-vFRACTION/` (`--optimised-out` moves it).
- **The way up** (`--up DIR`, along the part's `layers`): BESO's only control for it is its casting filter, which keeps material only on material under it all the way down, so no overhang at all where a print takes one to 45°. Tried on the planted parts, it cut the beam's right half away with its support, and cut the cantilever's load off from its wall, as BESO can't bring a column back once it's gone. So it isn't used, and `--up` reads instead: `overhang` is the area of the kept material's outside that faces down past 45° from up, above its lowest level by more than an element, which a print would have to support, and the box round it. Without `--up` that isn't read, and the reply says so.
- **Verdict:** `OK` where the kept material is within both limits, `FAIL` where it's over either, saying to keep more; either way it's a reference. `DISAGREE` where a second reading lands across a limit from the first, or the volumes differ.
- **Second methods:** the peak after by BESO's own last solve, the taken-away material left in at a millionth of the stiffness, against the kept material solved with it taken out; the peak before by BESO's first solve; the held faces' reaction in both of ours against the weight put on, within 1% or `NO ANSWER`; and the kept volume read back from `kept.stl`, by the divergence theorem over its triangles, against the kept elements' volumes summed, within 1e-4.
- **Same inputs, same result.** gmsh meshes on one thread, as for `load`, CalculiX solves on the image's two (`OMP_NUM_THREADS`), and BESO has no random step: it sorts the elements by their strain energy. The reply gives `mesh_digest` and `kept_digest`, of the mesh's nodes and of the kept elements, and two runs of the planted cantilever gave every figure the same. `load` isn't run-to-run identical on main, as the mesh round a bolt can vary; `optimise` reads a part with no bolt modelled, and its digests say whether a mesh differed.
- **BESO, as checked on 2026-11-25:** *maintained*, lightly: its last commit is 2025-04-03 (5056d30, which the image holds), its author answered an issue on 2026-03-29 to say it runs with FreeCAD 1.1, and six fixes sent in September and October 2026 are open. Of those, two touch what's used here: the simple filter's sectors can be keyed apart and stop the run (fandaL/beso#57), which `optimise` says as BESO's error, and a filter that finds no neighbour carries on unfiltered and says so only in its log (fandaL/beso#58), which `optimise` reads and answers `NO ANSWER`. *Its licence*, LGPL-3.0, lets it be installed in the image unchanged, as it is, with its `LICENSE` beside it. *Headless*: `beso_main.py` runs as a plain Python script beside a settings file, with Matplotlib's Agg backend and no display; it imports nothing from FreeCAD, whose workbench it only offers. It isn't on conda-forge or PyPI, so its five files are pinned in `cae-image/lock-*.txt` as `github` lines, by commit and SHA-256, and the build fetches and checks each.
- **What it leaves out:** walls and infill (the part is solid), any load case but the one given, one stiffness every way, and creep; and BESO's answer is for this mesh, jagged at its size, a guide to where material carries load, not a shape to print.
- **Planted** (`tests/cae_cases/make_optimise.py`, `tests/test_optimise_cae.py`): **the MBB beam**, 120 × 20 × 4 mm on two feet 4 mm long, standing, 1 kg at the middle of its top, kept to half: the known truss, read point by point from `kept.stl`. The top chord is kept a third and two thirds along, the bottom chord at the middle and a third along, and the diagonal at the left support; the top corners over both supports are empty, as are the hole under the load half way up and those a fifth and three quarters along. 0.4999 kept, 4.64 MPa von Mises after against 2.19 solid, by both solves. **The cantilever**, 80 × 20 × 4, fixed at x = 0, 2 kg at the top of its free end, kept to 0.4 with a 10 mm block at its middle kept: every element of the block in the result, and a second run gives every figure the same. **Refused:** a `--volume` of 0 or 1.2, exit 3; a load 20 mm off the part, and an `--up` across its layers, `NO ANSWER`.

### `fit-bed`

(`bench/fit_bed.py`.) `--bed X,Y,Z` is the bed's width, depth and height, 256 mm each unless given.

- **The sides.** A part lies on a flat face that the rest of it stands wholly above. Given STEP files, every such face is a side, and the best is one that fits, with the most flat on the bed, which is what holds a print down; the reply names the next two as well, and the record has them all (`parts.NAME.sides`). Given an assembly file, each part's `layers` says the way up it's printed, so only the two faces square to that are read, on the part's own file, before it's turned into its place. A part with no flat face there fails, and says so.
- **Whether it fits.** Laid on a side, the part is turned about the bed's upright axis by the least turn at which its footprint fits (`turned_deg`): 0 where it fits as it's drawn. A part longer than the bed is wide can still fit toward its corners. Where no turn fits, it's the turn that needs least of the bed, and the reply says how far over it is and to split it. Its height is its extent above the side.
- **It's the whole bed,** with no margin for a brim or a skirt, which the slicer adds, and it doesn't read whether the side needs supports, but for a declared drop-in channel's (below). In a run, the side it lays a part on is what the stops read that part's downward faces on, square to its `layers`, and what `load`'s `layers` are held to (mqucifer/print-shop#192, [the rounds](rounds.md)).
- **Verdict:** `OK` when every part has a side that fits, `FAIL` naming each that doesn't. `parts.NAME.fits` is each part's.
- **Second method:** the exact solid turned the same way and measured by OpenCascade's own box round it (`BRepBndLib.AddOptimal`, with no triangulation and no margin: CadQuery's own box is padded, and read a disc exactly as wide as the bed as 0.001 mm over). The two differ by the mesh's chords on a curved part. Where they land on opposite sides of the bed's size it's `DISAGREE`.
- **A nut's pocket** (mqucifer/print-shop#168 and mqucifer/print-shop#183). Where the assembly's `through` joint states a `pocket`, its nut sits in one at the far end of the second part's hole. `fit-bed` reads it from that part's own faces, where the assembly puts it (`bench/through.py`): the flat walls that stand on the hole's end with their faces at the hole, face its axis, and face each other across it in pairs. Each pair holds the nut by two flats: six walls are a hex pocket, and two with an open end a slot the nut slides into. `pockets.NAME.N` has each bolt's: `across_flats_mm`, the narrowest pair; `play_mm`, half of that less the nut's size across its flats; `depth_mm`; `goes_in`, `turn_deg` and `off_axis_mm`; and `fits`. The bolt's bore in each part is round or peaked (mqucifer/print-shop#237, under `load`), read by the same code from this image's own faces, and `bores` says which: a peaked one's point is read again from the fine mesh's section.
  - **A pocket's walls are those whose faces are at the hole** (mqucifer/print-shop#204). A wall counts where the point of its plane nearest the bolt's axis is between its face's ends along the wall, or past an end by no more than the nut's corners reach: half its least size across them and the room the bolt has in its hole, 4.645 mm for an M5 nut on a bolt in a 5.5 mm hole. So a wall cut in two over the hole, and a neck the nut's corners reach, are still the pocket's, and a like wall further along a row of slots is another pocket's. The face's ends are its own corners', whatever their height: a face is one piece, so one with ends either side of the hole comes past it, at the seat or over it. Where a pocket fails or reads `DISAGREE`, the reply says the walls it took, each by how far from the axis, which way, and where its face is.
  - **The nut going in** (mqucifer/print-shop#183). Walls that hold the flats in pairs don't say whether the corners get between them: on eval-table-1 the frame's pockets were 8.2 mm squares round M5 nuts, which hold the 8 mm flats with 0.1 mm of play, and they passed, though in any turn an M5 nut is at least 8.79 x cos 15° = 8.49 mm wide along one side or the other. So the nut is read as a hexagon at its least size across its corners, the table's `e`, and turned round its middle, a quarter of a degree at a time and then a thousandth near the best, until it's clear of every wall's plane. Its middle is on the bolt's axis, or as far off it as the bolt can move in the narrower of the two parts' holes, the hole's radius less half the bolt's width, and no further (`room_in_hole_mm`): the nut is on the bolt. Where some turn and place clear every wall, `goes_in` is true, with the turn and how far off the axis it is. Where none does, the reply gives the turn at which, with its middle on the axis, its corners pass the walls least, each wall they pass, toward which way from the axis and by how much, and how far off the axis it would have to be, where it could go in at all.
  - **It fails** where the pocket is narrower than the nut's flats, which then don't go in; where the nut's corners don't go in, as above; where it's as wide as the nut's least size across its corners, so the nut turns in it; where its play isn't what the file states, within 0.005 mm; and where no two flat walls face each other there. A pocket shallower than the nut is said, and isn't a failure: the nut stands proud.
  - **Second method:** the part's fine mesh cut square to the bolt half way up the pocket, and the nearest that outline comes to the axis, against the nearest wall by its faces; and with the nut's hexagon where the walls' reading puts it, how far that outline reaches into it (`into_nut_by_mesh_mm`), against how far the outline of the walls' planes does (`into_nut_mm`): a wall's deepest point inside the hexagon, from its nearest side. Past 0.01 mm apart, either, it's `DISAGREE`. A curved wall, such as a slot's round end, is in the mesh's outline and not the walls', and so reads `DISAGREE` where it's in the nut's way.
  - **What it leaves out:** the nut's way in along a slot, as only the walls at the hole are read: a slot that necks narrower further along than the nut's corners reach isn't failed for it, so a covered slot necked at every way out reads `OK` though the nut can't be slid into it. The nut's own corners are at their least, so a nut whose corners are sharper than the standard's least is bigger than this reads; and the walls are read as planes, so a pocket whose walls don't make a convex outline is read as smaller than it is.
  - **Planted** (`bed/pocket*`): an M5 nut, 8 mm across its flats and at least 8.79 across its corners, through a 5.5 mm hole, so the bolt has 0.25 mm to move. A hex pocket 8.4 mm across, 4 mm deep, stated at 0.2: `OK`, 0.200 mm a side by its faces and 4.2000 mm to its nearest wall by both methods, the nut 0.70 mm proud, going in square on with 0.3938 mm to spare by both. The same pair turned on its side reads the same. A slot 8.4 mm wide, open at the plate's edge: `OK`, three walls in one pair; the nut goes in turned 17.13° with its middle 0.085 mm toward the open end, its corners passing all three walls by 0.045 mm with it on the axis. 7.9 mm across: `FAIL`, the nut doesn't go in. 9.0: `FAIL`, the nut turns in it. A round pocket: `FAIL`, no flat walls. Stated at 0.3 where it's drawn at 0.2: `FAIL`, saying both. **A square 8.2 mm across, stated at 0.1:** `FAIL`, turned its best, 15°, its corners pass each of the four walls by 0.145 mm, and the walls reach 0.1302 mm into it by both methods. An octagon 8.75 mm across, stated at 0.375: `OK`, turned 7.5° off a flat, 0.0154 mm clear. **Three slots in a row** (`bed/row*`), 8.3 mm wide and 70 mm apart along their walls, each 0.5 mm off the last across them, stated at 0.15: `OK`, each by its own four walls, where planes without end made pairs 7.3 mm across. With the middle one 7.9 wide: `FAIL`, the nut doesn't go in there, and there only. eval-table-1's own files, read once in a scratch folder and not kept: its frame's four squares fail the same way, and its leg's eight hex pockets go in turned 30°.
- **A drop-in channel** (mqucifer/print-shop#302). Where a `through` joint declares its bolt in one ([under `load`](#a-drop-in-channel)), `fit-bed` reads it in both parts where the assembly puts them (`bench/fit_channel.py`), and `channels.NAME.N` has each bolt's, with each part's stretch under `ends`:
  - **its round** against the bolt's shank: `round_mm`, and `play_mm` a side. Narrower than the shank, or open narrower than it where the round leaves off (`open_mm`), fails;
  - **each wall** it bears on, the head's in the first part and the nut's in the second (`wall`, `plane`): where the channel ends, or it fails naming the wall. The washer under the head or the nut bears on the ring less the channel's cut by arithmetic (`seat_mm2`), and on the part's flat faces in the wall's plane, facing out, cut to the ring by OpenCascade (`bears_mm2`). Less than that is a wall too narrow, or not flat and square to the bolt, and fails naming the wall. Second method: the fine mesh's triangles in that plane cut to the ring by Shapely (`bears_by_mesh_mm2`); either side of the seat from the faces is `DISAGREE`;
  - **the open side clear** for the bolt to drop in: nothing of the part within the shank's width of the axis, from the axis out through the open side and past the part, along the channel's stretch, read 0.005 mm inside that box so a wall at the shank's width isn't in it. By the exact solid cut to it (`over_mm3`) and by the fine mesh's triangles clipped to it (`over_by_mesh_mm2`); one with something in the way and the other without is `DISAGREE`, both is `FAIL`;
  - **facing up** as the part is laid (`side`, `faces_up`): its round then prints as a floor and its walls straight up. Laid any other way it needs support, and fails, leading with that and what to do, naming the joint, the part and its side. Where a part could lie either way up, the side with every channel up is taken, as long as it fits, before the side with more flat; a part with no channel is laid as before.
  - **Planted** (`channel/`, the halves under `load` above): `OK`, 0.250 mm a side, each washer on 40.618 mm² by its faces and by the mesh, nothing in the way, both printed on their bottoms; with a 2 mm hollow under the second half, which leaves its bottom 96 mm² of flat to its top's 428, it's still laid on its bottom. **Controls,** each `FAIL` naming the joint: the second half with a rod under it, so it can only lie on its top, open side down, needs support; 9 mm wide, its washer on 37.68 mm² of 40.62; a bridge 2 mm wide over its channel, 19.96 mm³ in the bolt's way; and the nut's wall declared at x=30, 2 mm past where the channel ends. With no `channel`, its nut stated in a pocket so the bolt is read, it's `NO ANSWER`, a slot's end, as on main.
- **Planted** (`tests/bench_cases/make_fixtures.py`, `bed/`): a 120 mm beam fits as it's drawn, on its largest flat; a 300 x 30 one fits a 256 mm bed only turned 37.75°, at 255.57 x 207.39; a 400 mm one needs 304.1 mm at its best, 48.1 over, and fails, saying to split it; a rail 300 mm long printed standing is 44 mm too tall, and lying down fits; a disc 256 mm across fits, and one 256.2 doesn't. At the table's planned size, a leg 520 mm tall fits no way round, standing or lying.

## A part built flat, read where it sits

mqucifer/print-shop#201. A design run made a table's apron, whose stained-glass panel sits on a face square to y in the table's frame. `capture`, `light`, `pause` and `thickness` read a pane's pocket along z only, and the joints are read against the neighbouring parts, which are given in the table's frame. Built either way, one set of checks couldn't read it. The Sponsor, 2026-11-21: "The glass itself gets designed as a regular flat panel. The orientation only matters when it meets other pieces."

So a part is built flat, in its own frame, and the glass commands read it there, as they always have. `clearance`, `fit-on` and `render` take `--placed ASSEMBLY` (`bench/placed.py`): each file the assembly file names, a part's `file` or one in its `with`, is read turned by the part's `turn`, in order, about the origin's axes, then moved by its `at`, exactly as `load` places it; every other file is read where it is. The file itself never changes, and no turned copy is written.

- **A file in several parts** (mqucifer/print-shop#343): an assembly often names one file in several parts, alike slices of a dome each turned to its place, or four alike legs. Such a file is read once for each part that names it, each copy at its own `turn` and `at`, and named by its part, `slice.step as s2`; so is a file in several parts' `with`. A file the assembly names once keeps its own name. A pair between two copies is read like any other. Given once, or more, on the command line, it's read once as each of its parts, and the reply says so, with how to read fewer: `--part NAME`, once a part or several after it, reads a file as only the parts named, where any of its parts is named, and as each of its parts where none is. A name that's no part placing a file named here is refused, naming those that are. Before this, a file was read once, at the place of the last part naming it, and no pair between alike parts could be read.
- **The reply says so:** a `placed:` line naming each placement, its file, its part, its turns and its move, and `placed` in its numbers, by the name it's read by, each with its `file`. An assembly that places none of the files named says that, and `placed` is empty, so the reading isn't taken for a placed one.
- **`clearance`** reads every pair, `--lift`, `--slide`, `--box` and `--facing` with the files placed, so a place's box is in the assembly's coordinates. `--lift` and `--slide` move one part, the last file named: where the assembly names that file in several parts, `--as PART` says which, and its other parts stay where they sit, read as ones it could meet. Without `--as` such a reading is `NO ANSWER`, saying it moves one of them, so no part is left unread. With `--placed`, one file named may be enough, as it may be several parts.
- **`fit-on`** places its moving file and what it goes onto before the first step. It moves one part: where the assembly names its moving file in several parts, `--as PART` says which, and without it the reading is `NO ANSWER`, naming them. What it goes onto is read once a part, by `--part` as above, and the moving file's other parts are among them where `--onto` names its file too; it's never read onto itself. Its places are in the assembly's coordinates, each with its `file` and `read_as`, the copy it's on; the harness turns a place back into that copy's own frame before `arm` and `bend` read the file (`end_checks.own_frame`). The harness's end checks read a held part's way on once for each part its file is named in, `--as` each, and judge each; a way `fit-on` still won't read for want of `--as` fails, and is never passed as one it can't tell.
- **`render`** draws placed files where they sit, a file in several parts once for each, marked by its part, the first in the file's colour and each other in one of its own, unless `--look` gives the file one. With `--variant`, a row holding a placed file is drawn with it placed among the others, `its placed files where they sit`, and again under it as `NAME flat`, with only its placed files, as built.
- A STEP part is turned and moved by OCCT's own rotations and move, not a matrix: a matrix of floats isn't quite orthogonal, and OCCT refuses it. An STL's mesh is moved by the same placement as a 4 × 4 matrix.
- **A pair far apart is read by its bounds** (mqucifer/print-shop#354). `clearance` read every pair by its exact distance, about a second a pair on real parts, so a whole assembly of alike parts placed by `--placed`, on the order of ten thousand pairs, overran the bench's 600 s, and a design run split its fit check into one command a seam. Most of those pairs are far apart: parts on opposite sides of a ring can't touch. Now, past 3 pairs, a pair whose bounds lie farther apart than any gap the reading judges is read by them alone, `CLEAR`. What it judges is `--min-gap`, `--max-gap` where places are named, a `--lift` and the chords round it, and touching. A bound is never more than the true gap, and they're tried cheapest first: the distance between the pair's axis-aligned boxes (OCCT's, or an STL's own); how far apart their convex hulls lie along the way from one's middle to the other's or along either's main axes, which holds a turned part its box doesn't (`bench/far.py`); and Manifold's least gap between their fine meshes, for bodies whose boxes and hulls reach far past them: curved slices that each meet one part, or a pin through a hole. Each STEP body's hull and mesh are padded for the curve between the chords: twice what its own triangulation says it's off the surface, face by face (`Poly_Triangulation::Deflection`), and at least 0.05 mm (`clearance.pad_of`). BRepMesh misses its nominal 0.002 mm on B-spline faces: made up, a swept spline's fine triangulation says 0.023 mm, a revolved spline 0.0047, a loft 0.0073, a twisted extrusion 0.0029, each at or above the deviation sampled inside each face. The coarse mesh, which this bound first read, says up to 0.050 mm on the swept spline, against a pad of 0.05, so it reads the fine one. A face with no triangulation gives no bound: nothing is ruled out by it. One body wholly inside another reads 0 by the meshes, as an overlap does, so it's read exactly. The meshes are searched only 0.5 mm past the gap judged (`MESH_SEARCH`): the search costs as the triangles within it, 2 ms at 2 mm and a minute at 100 mm on a sphere of 80,000 triangles, so a pair the meshes rule out reads a bound of at most that much past it. With `--box` places, each place must lie clear of one of the pair's boxes, so it catches neither surface, `NOT CAUGHT`.
  - **No verdict changes.** A pair the bounds can't rule out, or with a part that can't be read, is read exactly, as before, with its second methods. A pair read by its bounds is only ever `CLEAR`: nothing that touches, overlaps or is too close is read that way. Every pair is read exactly with `--exact`, with `--slide` or `--facing`, up to 3 pairs, where each is said whole and a need names one, and where `--max-gap` holds each pair's least gap, as a pair far apart is then `TOO LOOSE`.
  - **The reply counts them,** at the end of the line that counts the pairs with nothing to report: "and N pair(s) farther apart than any gap judged, read by their bounds, CLEAR: least bound gap at least B mm, pairs.I", with "as far as its meshes were searched" where the least is a mesh bound that searched no farther, as each bound is a floor on the true gap, and the verdict's tally says "N clear by their bounds". It isn't a line of its own, so a reply stays short: the planted lid of 5 bodies over a fan (`where/lid5.step`), 15 pairs, 9 of them read by their bounds, says them in 171 more characters, 2,171 in all. Each keeps its place in `pairs`, so `pairs.N` is the same pair either way, with `read_by` `bounds`, `bound_gap_mm` its bound, `bound` which it was (`box`, `hull` or `mesh`), `bound_at_search` where a mesh bound searched no farther, and no `gap_mm`: a bound isn't the gap, and a need on its `gap_mm` is told so and to add `--exact`, never passed on it. `numbers.by_bounds` has the count, each bound's (`by_box`, `by_hull`, `by_mesh`), the gap past which, and the least bound and its pair; `pairs_s` how long the pairs took. The second check says how many each bound ruled out. A `clearance` need on a figure under `pairs.` is run by the harness with `--exact`, so the figure it holds is the exact gap (`needs.read_argv`); a pair's figure read by hand from a pair read by its bounds has no `gap_mm`, and a need on one says so.
  - **`--plan` says how it would read them,** and judges nothing: `NO ANSWER`, "a plan, which judges nothing: N pairs: B by their boxes, H by their hulls, M by their meshes, E to read exactly, about T s in all". It loads, meshes and bounds the pairs as a reading does, reads 3 of those it would read exactly, spread through them, to time them, and says how long each part took and the estimate, in `numbers.plan`. A whole assembly that ran past the 600 s limit said only that, so the split couldn't be seen without paying for it.
